Nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions to the unforced incompressible 3D Navier–Stokes Equation
Thomas Hou, Yixuan Wang, Changhe Yang

Abstract
The nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions to the unforced incompressible 3D Navier–Stokes equations is one of the central open problems in mathematical fluid dynamics. In this paper, we provide, to our knowledge, the first rigorous computer-assisted proof demonstrating such nonuniqueness. Inspired by earlier works [ 30 , 1 , 25 ] , we construct a Leray–Hopf solution in the self-similar setting and then establish the existence of a second solution by analyzing the stability of the linearized operator around this profile, showing that it corresponds to an unstable perturbation. To achieve this, we develop an innovative numerical method that computes candidate solutions with high precision and propose a framework for rigorously establishing exact solutions in a neighborhood of these candidates. A key step is to decompose the linearized operator into a coercive part plus a compact perturbation, which is further approximated by a finite-rank operator up to a small error. The invertibility of the linearized operator restricted to the image of this finite-rank approximation is then rigorously verified using computer-assisted proofs. This certifies the existence of an unstable eigenpair and, consequently, yields a second solution–indeed, infinitely many Leray–Hopf solutions.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we study the three-dimensional incompressible Navier–Stokes equation on the whole space without
forcing:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u-\Delta u+\nabla p=0,\\
\mathrm{div}u=0,\\
u(t=0,x)=u_{\operatorname{in}}(x).\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

Leray
[
33
]
, and later Hopf
[
26
]
for bounded domains, constructed weak solutions that exist for all time and satisfy energy inequalities. These solutions are termed Leray–Hopf solutions, and Leray’s construction was moreover suitable, in the sense that it satisfies local energy inequality.

The main contribution of this paper is to develop a rigorous computer-assisted proof of the nonuniqueness of the
Leray–Hopf solutions to (
1.1
).

Theorem 1

(Nonuniqueness of
Leray–Hopf solutions)

There exist infinitely many distinct suitable Leray–Hopf
solutions to the Navier–Stokes equation (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,1]
with the same divergence-free initial condition
u_{\operatorname{in}}=u_{\operatorname{loc}}
of compact support, with
u_{\operatorname{loc}}\in L^{q}
for any
q<3
(in particular
u_{\operatorname{in}}\in L^{2}
). The solutions are
L^{s}([0,1];L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),
for any
q\geqslant 2
,
\frac{3}{q}+\frac{2}{s}>1
. Namely, they just miss the Prodi–Serrin condition that guarantees a unique solution, see (
1.3
).

Our work is inspired by the works of Jia and Sverak
[
29
,
30
]
, which studied the forward self-similar Navier–Stokes equation and reduced nonuniqueness to spectral assumptions of the self-similar profiles. In
[
25
]
, Guillod and Sverak provided numerical evidence that such spectral assumptions are satisfied for the self-similar solution profiles that they constructed numerically. In
[
1
]
, Albritton–Brue–Colombo built on this idea of unstable profiles in self-similar variables, but added a singular forcing term so that their background solution does not need to solve the Navier–Stokes equation exactly. In fact, their profile can be any divergence-free function. They instead leveraged the unstable vortex solutions constructed by Vishik
[
46
,
47
,
2
]

that are steady states of the 2D Euler equations, and lifted them to 3D axisymmetric vortex rings. The case of the unforced Navier–Stokes equations is much more challenging since it is almost impossible to find an explicit self-similar Leray–Hopf solution with an unstable eigenvalue.

In this paper, we adopt a different strategy by developing a novel computational method and an innovative computer-assisted strategy to rigorously verify the existence of a self-similar profile that solves the unforced Navier–Stokes equation exactly with an unstable eigenvalue. We then use the tools developed in
[
1
,
30
]
to establish the nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions.

1.1
Leray–Hopf solution and strong-weak uniqueness

Two important types of solutions to (
1.1
) are the
Leray–Hopf solutions
and
the
strong solutions
. We begin with the definition of Leray–Hopf
solutions. We denote divergence-free function spaces using a subscript
\sigma
, and locally integrable function spaces using a subscript
\operatorname{loc}
.

Definition 1

(Leray–Hopf
solution)

[
44
]

Let
T>0
,
u_{\operatorname{in}}\in L_{\sigma}^{2}
. A Leray–Hopf solution on

\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T)
with initial data
u_{\operatorname{in}}
is a divergence-free vector field

u\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,T);L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,T);H^{1}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),

with the following properties:

1.

It holds that for all test functions
v\in C^{\infty}_{0}([0,T);C^{\infty}_{0,\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))
:

-\int_{0}^{T}\langle u,\partial_{t}v\rangle+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle+\int_{0}^{T}\langle u\cdot\nabla u,v\rangle=\langle u_{\operatorname{in}},v(0,\cdot)\rangle.

2.

u
satisfies the energy inequality:

\frac{1}{2}\|u(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\|u_{\operatorname{in}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Remark 1

(Suitability)

Solutions built via Leray’s original construction are, moreover, suitable, which means they satisfy the stronger local energy inequality:

(\partial_{t}-\Delta)\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}+\mathrm{div}((\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}+p)u)\leqslant 0

(1.2)

in a distributional sense. Local energy inequality plays an important role in partial regularity theory on the dimension of the potential singular sets, as in
[
8
,
34
]
.

A Leray–Hopf solution is weakly continuous in time
[
44
]
and exists globally. Its uniqueness is not guaranteed.
A strong solution is a Leray–Hopf solution that satisfies the Prodi–Serrin
[
42
,
43
]

condition in
L^{s}L^{q}
spaces:

u\in L^{s}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,T);L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),\quad 3<q<\infty,\quad 2<s<\infty,\quad\frac{3}{q}+\frac{2}{s}\leqslant 1.

(1.3)

The critical case of
q=3
was established by Escauriaza–Seregin–Sveark
[
23
]
.
Under this condition, the solution exhibits higher regularity
[
42
,
43
,
23
]
,

u\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,T);H^{1}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}([0,T);H^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})).

A global strong solution ensures the uniqueness of weak solutions. They are locally well-posed. However, the
global-in-time existence of such solutions remains an open question.
Therefore, we shift focus to studying the nonuniqueness of the
Leray–Hopf solutions arising from blow-up times of strong solutions. In other
words, we consider singular initial data
u_{\operatorname{in}}
.

1.2
Self-similar setting

Self-similar solutions are thus a special class of solutions of interest that lie precisely at
the boundary of the known well-posedness theory. A scale-invariant initial condition takes the form:

u_{\operatorname{in}}(x)=\frac{1}{|x|}A(\frac{x}{|x|}),

(1.4)

with a singularity of
O(|x|^{-1})
at the origin.
The authors of
[
29
,
30
]
seek
solutions of the form:

u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\widetilde{U}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}}),

(1.5)

which preserves the scaling structure. The readers should not confuse this self-similar setting, which starts from singular initial data, with the backward self-similar setting related to finite-time singularities from smooth initial data. Plugging (
1.5
) into the Navier–Stokes equation, we obtain:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{U}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta\widetilde{U}=0,\\
\mathrm{div}\widetilde{U}=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.6)

where
\xi=\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}}
is the similarity variable and
\Pi
is the Leray projection. We stress that the pressure and the Leray projection formulations are interchangeable. The initial
data, as per (
1.4
), translates into
the following boundary condition for (
1.6
),

\widetilde{U}(\xi)=\frac{1}{|\xi|}A(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|})+o(\frac{1}{|\xi|}),\quad|\xi|\to+\infty,

(1.7)

where the remainder term in fact decays at the sharper rate
O(\frac{1}{|\xi|^{3}})
, as shown in
[
29
]
.

The divergence-free condition induces the following constraint on
A
:

\operatorname{div}(\frac{1}{|x|}A(\frac{x}{|x|}))=0.

(1.8)

In
[
29
]
, the authors proved that for any admissible

A
, there exists a self-similar solution for (
1.1
). However, whether this solution is unique remains an open question.

1.3
Eigenvalue of the linearized operator and nonuniqueness

In
[
30
]
, a sufficient condition for nonuniqueness was
proposed. It involves analyzing the eigenvalues of the linearized operator

\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}
, defined by:

\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}v:=\frac{1}{2}v+\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla v-\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})+\Delta v.

(1.9)

To establish nonuniqueness, a second solution is sought in the form:

\widetilde{u}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\widetilde{U}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\phi(t,\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}}).

Plugging this ansatz into (
1.6
) and introducing the rescaled
time
\tau=\log t
,
\phi
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{\tau}\phi-\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}\phi+\Pi(\phi\cdot\nabla\phi)=0,\\
\mathrm{div}\phi=0.\end{array}\right.

(1.10)

We aim to find a nontrivial solution to (
1.10
) with initial data

\phi(\tau=-\infty,x)=0.

In
[
30
]
, the author considered a series of solutions
parameterized by
\alpha
. For a given parameterized initial data

A_{\alpha}
, let
\widetilde{U}_{\alpha}
denote the corresponding solution to
(
1.1
) with the boundary condition
\widetilde{U}_{\alpha}(\xi)\sim\frac{A_{\alpha}(\xi)}{\xi}
as
|\xi|\to+\infty
. Suppose that

L_{\widetilde{U}_{\alpha}}
has a discrete eigenvalue
\lambda_{\alpha}

satisfying
\Re(\lambda_{\alpha})>-\frac{1}{4}
. They showed that if the
trajectory of
\lambda_{\alpha}
could cross the imaginary axis from negative
to positive as
\alpha
increases, then, under certain auxiliary assumptions, there exists a nontrivial solution to (
1.10
) and thus the solution to (
1.1
) is nonunique.

Remark 2

Note that due to the slow decay of
u_{\operatorname{in}}=O(\frac{1}{|x|})
, the solution is not in
L^{2}
and thus not a
Leray–Hopf solution. However, in
[
30
]
, the authors
showed that such solutions can be localized to yield valid Leray–Hopf
solutions.

In
[
1
]
, the authors employed a similar approach to prove the nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions for the forced Navier–Stokes
equation. The force corresponds to the right-hand side of the first equation of (
1.1
) of any divergence-free
U
, while we have to find a divergence-free profile
U
that solves the first equation of (
1.6
) exactly without any external forcing. They assumed that an unstable eigenvalue,
with real part
a>0
, exists for the linearized operator

\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}
and found the unstable manifold associated with the
most unstable eigenvalue. The perturbation of the solution follows the asymptotics

\phi=\widetilde{U}^{\operatorname{lin}}+O(e^{2a\tau})
. Here,

\widetilde{U}^{\operatorname{lin}}
solves the linearized equation:

\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{U}^{\operatorname{lin}}=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}\widetilde{U}^{\operatorname{lin}},

(1.11)

and decays at the rate
O(e^{a\tau})
, converging to zero as
\tau\to-\infty
, indicating nonuniqueness.

Verification of eigenvalues.

Having related the nonuniqueness of the Leray–Hopf solutions to the eigenvalue of the linearized operator in self-similar variables, we now focus on confirming the existence of an unstable eigenvalue. We aim to find a solution to the following system:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{U}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta\widetilde{U}=0,\\
-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{v}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}+\widetilde{v}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta\widetilde{v}=\widetilde{\lambda}\widetilde{v},\quad\widetilde{\lambda}<0,\\
\mathrm{div}\widetilde{U}=0,\\
\operatorname{div}\widetilde{v}=0.\end{array}\right.

(1.12)

Here,
\Pi
denotes the Leray projection. We remark that we flip a sign of the linearized operator in the second equation of (
1.12
) due to the convenience of elliptic estimates.
If we can prove that
\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}
has an eigenvalue
with a positive real part for some
\widetilde{U}
satisfying (
1.6
), then one can hope to show that the solution to (
1.1
) is nonunique. Through
localization, this leads to the nonuniqueness of the Leray–Hopf solution. We will elaborate on this point in Section
2
.
Numerical evidence supporting the existence of such profiles was provided in

[
25
]
.

Theorem 2

There
exists a weak solution
(\widetilde{U},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{\lambda})
to the system (
1.12
) with
\widetilde{U}\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}},\widetilde{v}\in H^{1}
.

We adopt a computer-assisted framework to verify existence for (
1.12
). The strategy is to
compute a high-accuracy numerical profile
\overline{U}
and then construct a perturbation
U
so that

\overline{U}+U
solves the equation exactly. This requires (i) a certified small residual for
\overline{U}

and (ii) an invertibility estimate for the linearized operator about
\overline{U}
. To obtain (ii) we split
the linearized operator into a coercive part and a compact remainder; the latter is rigorously approximated
by a finite-rank operator with a quantitatively controlled tail. We then verify the needed spectral
properties on this small matrix (with interval arithmetic), combine them with coercivity, and close a
fixed-point argument (plus elliptic estimates); see Sections
3
and
4
. A similar strategy also applied to
(\widetilde{v},\widetilde{\lambda})
.

A subtle point is the regularity and decay of the exact profile

\widetilde{U}:=\overline{U}+U
. From Proposition
1
we only know
U\in H^{1}
with

\|U\|_{H^{1}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{U}
(via Schauder), which does not by itself identify
\widetilde{U}
as a
Leray–Hopf profile. To bridge this, we introduce a smooth radial cutoff at scale
R\gg 1
and write

\widetilde{U}=U_{\mathrm{near}}+U_{\mathrm{far}}
, where
U_{\mathrm{far}}
is divergence free, smooth, and
constructed to match the exterior asymptotics of
\overline{U}
(hence the same far–field boundary condition
and
|\xi|^{-1}
decay). In these variables,
U_{\mathrm{near}}
solves the stationary self-similar
Navier–Stokes system with variable coefficients determined by
U_{\mathrm{far}}
; these coefficients are
smooth,
C^{k}
–bounded, and decay polynomially, and the corresponding forcing is smooth and decays like

O(|\xi|^{-3})
by our far-field analysis. Because neither the coefficients nor the forcing are compactly
supported, standard local estimates are insufficient. We therefore combine local elliptic bootstrapping
(for variable, decaying coefficients) with the coercivity of the linearized operator to obtain

U_{\mathrm{near}}\in H^{m}
for all
m
, together with weighted energy bounds

|\xi|\,U_{\mathrm{near}},\quad|\xi|\,\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}},\quad|\xi|^{2}\,\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}},\quad|\xi|^{2}\,\nabla^{2}U_{\mathrm{near}}\ \in L^{2}.

A dyadic rescaling then yields the quantitative decay

|U_{\mathrm{near}}(\xi)|=O(|\xi|^{-3/2})
as
|\xi|\to\infty
; see
Propositions
3
and
4
. Consequently,
\widetilde{U}
has the required
regularity and far-field decay, and is a Leray–Hopf self-similar profile.

Outline of the nonuniqueness proof (Section
2.2
).

We combine the localization idea of Jia-Sverak
[
30
]
with the fixed-point framework of Albritton-Brue-Colombo
[
1
]
. Writing the solution as

u=\widetilde{u}+u^{\mathrm{cut}}+u^{\mathrm{cor}}
, we first
localize the scale-invariant initial data
by a Bogovskii
decomposition
u_{\mathrm{in}}=u_{\operatorname{loc}}+w
, where
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
is compactly supported and divergence free, and then set

u^{\mathrm{cut}}=-e^{t\Delta}w
to peel off the far-field tail. Unlike
[
1
]
, our
self-similar profile
\widetilde{U}
is not compactly supported; the new ingredient is to control the
resulting
far-field interaction
via heat smoothing, which makes the associated linear term small when the localization radius
R
is large. Passing to similarity variables, we solve for the
correction
U^{\mathrm{cor}}
as a fixed point of a Duhamel map generated by the semigroup

e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}}
. A key idea is to use a Riesz projection to split the dynamics into a stable part and a unstable part as in
[
30
]
; see also
[
11
,
10
]
. We will show that the map is a contraction. The finitely many unstable modes will serve as free parameters at

\tau=0
. Different choices of these unstable coordinates yield distinct solutions on

(-\infty,0]
, producing infinitely many Leray–Hopf solutions with the same
L^{2}
initial data
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
.
In this way we retain the advantages of both approaches–localization from
[
30
]

and the fixed-point mechanism of
[
1
]
–while handling a non-compactly supported
self-similar background.

In our construction of the Leray–Hopf solution
\overline{U}
and the eigenfunction
\overline{v}
,
we impose even symmetry in
z
and odd symmetry in
z
for
\overline{v}
. The odd parity of
\overline{v}
breaks the base even symmetry of
\overline{U}
; thus the Leray–Hopf branch bifurcating in the
\overline{v}
-direction is symmetry-breaking, which was also observed by the previous work
[
25
,
1
]
.

1.4
Computer-assisted proofs

Our approach
motivates the following assumption for approximate profiles:

Assumption 1

(Approximate profiles)

The tuple
(\overline{U},\overline{P},\overline{v},\overline{q},\overline{\lambda})
is an approximate solution of (
1.12
) with small residuals:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{U}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla\overline{U}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{U}-\Delta\overline{U}+\nabla\overline{P}=\mathrm{E}^{U},\\
-\frac{1}{2}\overline{v}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla\overline{U}+\nabla\overline{q}-\Delta\overline{v}=\overline{\lambda}\overline{v}+\mathrm{E}^{v},\quad\overline{\lambda}<0,\\
\mathrm{div}\overline{U}=0,\\
\operatorname{div}\overline{v}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
\overline{v}
is normalized (i.e.,
\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{2}}=1
), and the
orthogonality condition
\langle\mathrm{E}^{v},\overline{v}\rangle=0
is
satisfied upon a possible modification of
\overline{\lambda}
. The small residuals
\mathrm{E}^{U}
and
\mathrm{E}^{v}
are bounded by

\|\mathrm{E}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{U},\|\mathrm{E}^{v}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{v}.

Here
\overline{U},\overline{v}\in C^{\infty}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{0\})\cap H^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. In the far field,
\overline{U}
satisfies the boundary condition

\overline{U}(\xi)=\frac{1}{|\xi|}A(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|})+O({|\xi|^{-3}}),\quad|\xi|\to+\infty,

(1.13)

which is consistent with the boundary condition in Section
1.2
, for a suitable
A
with the divergence-free constraint (
1.8
). Moreover, for any index
j\geqslant 0
, we have

\nabla^{j}\overline{U}(\xi)=\nabla^{j}(\frac{1}{|\xi|}A(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}))+O(|\xi|^{-j-3}).

(1.14)

Our choice of the numerical basis guarantees the integrability and decay; see Sections
5.5
and
5.6
.

Suppose we have constructed a sufficiently accurate approximate solution. The next step is to perform rigorous error analysis to confirm the existence of a
nearby exact solution. This leads to our main theoretical results, summarized in the two propositions below:

Proposition 1

(Exact self-similar profile)

If Assumption
1
holds, then there exists a
U

satisfying the following equation in the distributional sense:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}U-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla U-\Delta U+\Pi(\overline{U}\cdot\nabla U+U\cdot\nabla\overline{U}+U\cdot\nabla U)=-\Pi\mathrm{E}^{U},\\
\operatorname{div}U=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.15)

so that

\widetilde{U}=\overline{U}+U

(1.16)

solves the first and third equations of
(
1.12
).
Moreover, the perturbation
U\in H^{1}
satisfies:

\|U\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{U}.

Proposition 2

(Exact unstable eigenpair)

If Assumption
1
holds, then there exists

(\lambda,v)
satisfying the following equation in the distributional sense:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}v-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla v-\Delta v+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+U\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla U)=\overline{\lambda}v+\lambda\overline{v}+\lambda v-\Pi\mathrm{E}^{v},\\
\operatorname{div}v=0.\end{array}\right.

(1.17)

Here,
\widetilde{U}=\overline{U}+U
is the solution stated in Proposition
1
. Hence,
(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{v})=(\overline{\lambda}+\lambda,\overline{v}+v)
is an eigenpair of the linearized operator
and solves the second and fourth equations of
(
1.12
).
Additionally,

v\in H^{1}
satisfies the orthogonality condition
\langle v,\overline{v}\rangle=0
, and the following estimate holds for
(v,\lambda)
:

\|v\|_{L^{2}}+|\lambda|\lesssim\varepsilon^{v}.

Therefore,
\widetilde{v}
is nontrivial, and the eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}
is strictly
negative.

One can immediately see that Theorem
2
can be inferred by the two preceding propositions.

One of the main contributions of this paper is that we
develop an innovative numerical framework to construct a smooth, axisymmetric, divergence-free candidate profile with high precision. A distinguished advantage of this framework is that we can start with any initial guess to search for a candidate unstable eigenmode. We refine our search iteratively by using gradient descent to locate a maximally unstable configuration. Specifically, we first solve the Navier–Stokes equations in a large finite domain using a finite element method, then extend it to the whole space by an analytic change of variables
r=\tan(\beta)
and interpolate it on an exactly divergence-free spectral basis, which is essential for rigorous computer-assisted verification. We also use an iterative optimization method to further improve the accuracy of the approximate profile until a sufficiently small residual is reached. An important property of our numerical framework is that our constructed numerical solution
\overline{U}
or
\overline{v}
is smooth except at the origin. Moreover,
\overline{U}
satisfies the far field boundary condition and the decay property to all orders.
For more details,
see Section
5
.

We would like to point out that our
Navier–Stokes nonuniqueness verification is far lighter than that for the 3D Euler singularity program
[
12
,
14
,
13
]
, because viscosity yields genuine coercivity.
In particular, the linearized operators enjoy relatively large coercivity coefficients in
L^{2}
:
about
0.054
for the
\overline{U}
–equation and about
0.16
for the
\overline{v}
–equation
(see Section
4.5
). This allows us to work in standard
L^{2}/H^{1}
norms without
singular weights and to close the a posteriori bounds using only the
L^{2}
residuals of

\overline{U}
and
\overline{v}
together with a few pointwise envelopes. The discrete operators we
assemble are modest (roughly
1200\times 1200
), and the finite-rank verification reduces to a small,
static generalized eigenproblems of size
25\times 25
. Thanks to the strong damping reflected by the
above coercivity and the effective approximation property of our generalized eigenfunctions, retaining
25
modes suffices to close the estimates via the Schauder fixed point
theorem.

Moreover, we use a trigonometric tensor-product representation in
(\beta,\theta)
for
\overline{U}
and
\overline{v}
after the change of variables

r=\tan\beta
with a relatively small number of modes (
N=600
in the
\beta
-direction and
N=300
in the
\theta
-direction). This representation enables us to perform
analytic
L^{2}
evaluation of the residuals
E^{U}
and
E^{v}
(no round-off accumulation) and also makes it easy for us to obtain relatively sharp upper bounds for
\|\overline{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}
as well as their gradients. The only
delicate numerical step is to evaluate the
Q
-inner products for the generalized eigenfunctions which involve Bessel functions along the
\beta
-direction. We need to approximate the inner products using a 4th order quadrature and supply a rigorous
W^{4,\infty}
error bound on the integrand, see Section
7
for more discussion. Altogether, viscous coercivity, low-dimensional spectral checks, and
analytic trigonometric calculus make the present verification substantially simpler than in the
Euler setting.

We remark that a candidate solution was found in
[
25
]
, but this approximate solution seems to be too
large to apply our computer-assisted proof, in the sense that the apprximation of the associated compact part of the linearized operator by a finite rank operator would require a large number of modes to reduce the residual error small enough for us to close the bootstrap argument, making the computer verification too expensive; see Section
4.2
for details.

1.5
Organization of the paper

In summary, our strategy consists of three main steps:

1.

Construct an accurate approximate solution to the eigenvalue system (
1.12
).

2.

Establish the existence of an exact solution (Theorem

2
).

3.

Prove the nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions (Theorem

1
).

The rest of the paper is structured as follows. We first conclude nonuniqueness based on Theorem
2
in Section
2
. Provided that Theorem
2
holds, we establish the smoothness of our profile by bootstrapping its regularity using elliptic estimates, even if the numerical profile is nonsmooth by itself only at the origin. We also show that the profile satisfies the boundary conditions necessary for the self-similar setting by establishing the fast decay of the perturbation. Then we adopt a fixed-point approach similar to
[
1
]
and
[
30
]
to establish nonuniqueness based on the unstable eigenpair, with a cutoff to obtain Leray–Hopf solutions.

In Section
3
, we outline the
framework for proving Theorem
2
, which serves as a
foundation for the numerical analysis of a broad class of problems. We believe that our framework is interesting in itself to establish eigenvalue properties rigorously for other problems.
In Section
4
, we apply this framework to our specific
system and establish Theorem
2
. We demonstrate the construction of a finite-rank operator to
approximate the compact part of the linear operator, verify that this
approximation has a sufficiently small error, and derive the necessary
nonlinear estimates to complete the argument. For both the nonlinear terms and the
approximation error, sharp estimates are essential to tighten the final bound.

Section
5
discusses the numerical implementation in detail. We
introduce the setting of the finite element method used to obtain an approximate solution, and interpolate it on a spectral basis
to ensure exact divergence-freeness. Section
6
details the numerical construction of the finite-rank approximation and verifies the invertibility of the
linear operator on a finite-dimensional subspace. Finally, we describe the numerical methods that we will use to rigorously verify our numerical computations using interval arithmetic in Section
7
and collect some proofs in the Appendix.

1.6
Literature review

Now we review related literature on nonuniqueness besides the programs of Jia–Sverak and Guillod, and Albritton–Brue–Colombo. We also discuss relevant literature on computer-assisted proofs.

Nonuniqueness.

One of the earliest examples of nonuniqueness to (
1.1
) dates back to Ladyzhenskaya
[
32
]
, in a time-varying domain with force. Similar to the program of Jia, Sverak, and Guillod, Bressan, Murray, and Shen
[
5
,
4
]
proposed some suggestive numerical evidence for nonuniqueness to the Euler equations without forcing. In addition to the work using self-similar solutions, there is another important line of work leveraging
convex integration, a powerful tool introduced to fluid dynamics that resulted in the proof of the Onsager conjecture by Isett
[
28
]
and Buckmaster–De-Lellis–Szekelyhidi–Vicol
[
6
]
. In particular, Buckmaster–Vicol
[
7
]
established that for any
L^{2}
initial data, distributional solutions to (
1.1
) are nonunique, and Cheskidov–Luo
[
18
,
19
]
established nonuniqueness in
L_{t}^{2-}L_{x}^{\infty}
and
L_{t}^{\infty}L_{x}^{2-}
for dimension
d\geqslant 2
. However, solutions obtained from convex integration schemes are very far from Leray regularity
H^{1}_{x}
. Very recently, Coiculescu–Palasek established nonuniqueness in a scaling critical space
BMO^{-1}

[
20
]
where global well-posedness for small initial data holds
[
31
]
, using techniques from a dyadic model introduced by Palasek
[
41
]
.

Computer-assisted proofs.

The idea of combining rigorous analysis with numerical candidates has been successfully applied to problems in fluid dynamics, where the inherent complex nature of solutions prohibited analytical candidates: particularly in the search for backward self-similar singularities, culminating in the computer-assisted proof for the 3D incompressible Euler equation with cylindrical boundary by Chen–Hou
[
12
,
14
,
13
]
based on numerical investigation by Luo–Hou
[
39
,
40
]
. We draw inspiration from the works of Chen–Hou
[
12
]
, Elgindi–Pasqualotto
[
21
]
on the singularity formulation of the Boussinesq equation with only radial nonsmoothness, and Castro–Cordoba–Gomez-Serrano
[
9
]
on the construction of smooth rotating solutions of the inviscid surface quasi-geostrophic equation. We also remark the linear framework of Liu–Nakao–Oishi for eigenvalue verifications
[
38
,
36
,
37
]
.

Stability analysis, especially linear stability, around the approximate solution is essential to conclude the computer-assisted proof, and we apply a coercive-compact decomposition of the linear operator. The compact part is approximated by a finite-rank operator, whose error, along with the residual of the approximate solution, will need to be verified rigorously by interval arithmetic. Our decomposition is obvious in a regular
H^{1}
norm, unlike in
[
12
]
, where building on results of 1D singularities in fluid models
[
15
,
16
]
, one needs to use singularly weighted estimates; see also
[
27
,
17
,
35
]
for the use of singular weights for singularity beyond self-similarity or full stability.

For numerical computation, we use traditional finite element methods for profile computation and spectral
methods for rigorous verification.
We remark that there has been encouraging recent progress in using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) to construct self-similar blowup profiles, including the 2D Boussinesq system with boundary
[
49
,
50
,
48
]
. However, a rigorous computer-assisted proof is not yet available to rigorously justify these numerical profiles.

1.7
Notations

We denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Omega}
the
L^{2}
-inner product over a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The Sobolev norm
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}
for functions in the Sobolev space
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
is defined by

\|f\|_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}:=(\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant k}\|\partial^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
denotes a multi-index and
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}
is its order.
In particular, we write
H^{k}(\Omega):=W^{k,2}(\Omega)
. These notations extend naturally to vector- and matrix-valued functions induced by the Frobenius norm. For example, for an

m\times n
matrix function
A(x)
with entries
A_{ij}
, we define its
L^{p}
norm as:

\|A\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}:=\|(\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m,1\leqslant j\leqslant n}|A_{ij}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}.

When
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we can omit
\Omega
.

We use
C
to denote a generic absolute constant, which may change from line
to line. The notation
A\lesssim B
indicates that
A\leqslant CB
for some
constant
C>0
. Constants such as
K_{0},K_{1},\ldots
are fixed throughout
the paper. Constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots
are tunable parameters that arise in both the analytical estimates and the computational components of the proof. We use
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\ldots
to denote error
bounds that can be made arbitrarily small. Constants like
M_{0},M_{1},\ldots

are abbreviations for long expressions.
To indicate dependence, we use subscripts and superscripts such as
K^{U}
, though not necessarily functional
dependence.

2
Nonuniqueness Based on Theorem
2

In this section, we detail the proof of Theorem
1
based on Theorem
2
, addressing the
nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions. First, we demonstrate the smoothness
of the profile
\widetilde{U}
, along with its far-field decay. In particular, it satisfies the boundary conditions of a self-similar solution. Then, via an approach inspired by prior works
[
1
,
30
]
, we establish the nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf weak solutions
starting from homogeneous initial data after cutoff, as in
[
30
]
. An important idea is to perform a stable-unstable decomposition of the semigroup and demonstrate that each appropriate unstable mode at
t=1
corresponds to a distinct solution with the same initial data.

2.1
Regularity of the profile

We begin by proving the smoothness of the profile and that it satisfies the appropriate boundary condition. Notice that by Proposition
1
and Assumption
1
, we know that
\widetilde{U}\in H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
. We will perform a decomposition into far and near fields of the profile, and bootstrap the regularity based on the steady state equation (
1.12
).

To be precise, by Assumption
1
,
\overline{U}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{0\})\cap H^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}
is divergence free, and we
construct
U_{\operatorname{far}}\in C^{\infty}
, also divergence
free, such that
\overline{U}-U_{\operatorname{far}}\in H^{1}
.
Let

\widetilde{\chi}
be a smooth truncation function:

\widetilde{\chi}(\xi)=0,\quad|\xi|\leqslant 1,\quad\widetilde{\chi}(\xi)=1,\quad|\xi|\geqslant 2,

and define:

U_{\operatorname{far}}=\Pi(\widetilde{\chi}\overline{U}).

(2.1)

Then,
U_{\operatorname{far}}
satisfies:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}U_{\operatorname{far}}=\widetilde{\chi}\overline{U}-\nabla P_{c},\\
\operatorname{div}U_{\operatorname{far}}=0,\end{array}\right.

(2.2)

where
P_{c}
solves:

\Delta P_{c}=\operatorname{div}(\widetilde{\chi}\overline{U})=\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{\chi}.

(2.3)

Here, the source term is of compact support and mean-zero, and we can invert the Laplacian in 3D to conclude that
the function
P_{c}
is smooth, and
\nabla^{j}P_{c}
decays sufficiently fast as

O({|\xi|^{-j-2}})
, ensuring
\overline{U}-U_{\operatorname{far}}=(1-\widetilde{\chi})\overline{U}+\nabla P_{c}\in H^{1}
. Then we decompose
\widetilde{U}
into two components:

\widetilde{U}=U_{\operatorname{near}}+U_{\operatorname{far}}.

(2.4)

By Proposition
1
and (
1.16
), we know
U\in H^{1}
, and thus
U_{\operatorname{near}}=\widetilde{U}-U_{\operatorname{far}}=U+\overline{U}-U_{\operatorname{far}}\in H^{1}
. Moreover,
U_{\operatorname{far}}
has the same far field asymptotics as
\overline{U}
:

\nabla^{j}U_{\operatorname{far}}(\xi)=\nabla^{j}(\frac{1}{|\xi|}A(\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}))+O(|\xi|^{-j-3}).

(2.5)

With this decomposition, we are ready to establish the smoothness and decay properties of
\widetilde{U}
.

Proposition 3

(Smoothness)

The solution
\widetilde{U}
in Proposition
1
is smooth.

Sketch of proof for Proposition
3
.

U_{\operatorname{far}}
is smooth, so we only need to show the smoothness of
U_{\operatorname{near}}
.
We defer the full proof to Appendix
G
and outline the main steps of coercive estimates here.

Near–far split and the equation for
U_{\mathrm{near}}
.

Plug the decomposition (
2.4
) into equation (
1.12
), we obtain an equation for
U_{\mathrm{near}}
below in the distributional sense,

-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{near}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}}+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta U_{\mathrm{near}}=F,\quad\nabla\cdot U_{\mathrm{near}}=0,

where we have the decay of
F
by the asymptotics (
2.5
) and leading order cancellation

F:=\tfrac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{far}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{far}}+\Delta U_{\mathrm{far}},\quad\nabla^{k}F(\xi)=O(|\xi|^{-3-k})\ \ (k\geqslant 0).

Mollification and the commutator.

Let
\rho_{\epsilon}
be a standard mollifier and set

u^{\epsilon}=\rho_{\epsilon}*U_{\mathrm{near}},\quad F^{\epsilon}=\rho_{\epsilon}*F.

Then
u^{\epsilon}\in H^{\infty}
,
\nabla\cdot u^{\epsilon}=0
, and we obtain

-\tfrac{1}{2}u^{\epsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla u^{\epsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\rho_{\epsilon}*,\,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\mathrm{near}}+\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta u^{\epsilon}=F^{\epsilon},

with the explicit commutator

[\rho_{\epsilon}*,\,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\mathrm{near}}=\rho_{\epsilon}*(\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}})-\xi\cdot\nabla(\rho_{\epsilon}*U_{\mathrm{near}})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\,y\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}}(\xi-y)\,dy,

and the bound

\|[\nabla^{k-1}\rho_{\epsilon}*,\,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\,\epsilon\,\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}}\quad\text{for all }k\geqslant 1.

Energy at level
k
and coercivity of drift and diffusion.

Apply
\nabla^{k}
, take inner products with
\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}
, and integrate by parts (localizing by a large cutoff
\chi_{R}
and letting
R\to\infty
at the end). One obtains

\langle-\Delta\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

and, using
\nabla\cdot\xi=3
, we get

\langle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=\tfrac{1}{4}\langle\nabla\cdot\xi,|\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}|^{2}\rangle=\tfrac{3}{4}\,\|\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Nonlinearity.

Recall
\widetilde{U}=U_{\mathrm{far}}+U_{\mathrm{near}}
. For
k\geqslant 2
,
H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
embeds into
L^{\infty}
, and we get

\|\rho_{\epsilon}*\nabla^{k-1}\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\nabla^{k-1}\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C_{k}(1+\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|^{2}_{H^{k}}),

since
U_{\mathrm{far}}
is smooth with the decay (
2.5
). For
k=1
we use a commutator representation,

\|\rho_{\epsilon}*\nabla^{k-1}\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C_{k}(1+\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|^{2}_{H^{k}}+\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}})\leqslant C_{k}(1+\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|^{2}_{H^{k}}+\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{k+1}}^{3/4}\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{5/4}),

The forcing term satisfies
\|\nabla^{k}F^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C_{k}
.

Bootstrap.

Collecting the previous bounds and treating
\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}}
as a constant render the bound

\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant C_{k}(1+\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{7/4}),

with a constant independent of
\epsilon
. We have a uniform bound on
\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{k+1}}
and letting
\epsilon\to 0
yields

U_{\mathrm{near}}\in{H^{k+1}}
.
Starting from
H^{1}
and iterating proves
U_{\mathrm{near}}\in H^{\infty}
.

In particular, along with the decay of
U_{\mathrm{far}}
(
2.5
), we get that

\widetilde{U}\in L^{4}\cap L^{\infty}.

(2.6)

Proposition 4

(Far field asymptotics)

The solution
\widetilde{U}
in Proposition
1
satisfies the boundary condition (
1.7
) with the same
A
as that associated with
\overline{U}
in Assumption
1
.

Sketch of Proof for Proposition
4
.
Since
U_{\operatorname{far}}
satisfies the desirable far field condition (
2.5
), we only need to establish
U_{\operatorname{near}}
has decay
O(|\xi|^{-3/2})
in the far field.
We will establish that
|\xi|U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
, and use a dyadic Sobolev embedding to conclude the far field decay. We defer the full proof to Appendix
G
and outline the weighted estimates and dyadic rescaling.

Weighted
L^{2}
at level
0
.

Let
\chi_{R}
be a standard cutoff function with radius
R
, for example
1-\widetilde{\chi}(\xi/R)
. Define the cutoff version of the radial weight
|\xi|^{2}
as
\phi_{L}
by:

\phi_{L}(\xi):=\phi\left(|\xi|/L\right),\qquad\phi(r)=r^{2}\ (0\leqslant r\leqslant 1),\quad\phi(r)=4\ (r\geqslant 2).

where
\phi
is increasing and smooth.
Test the near-field equation against
\chi_{R}\,\phi_{L}\,U_{\mathrm{near}}
, integrate by parts, and let
R\to\infty
. For the drift, we have

\langle-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\frac{3}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\frac{1}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\xi\cdot\nabla\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\geqslant\frac{3}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle,

since
\phi_{L}
is radially increasing. For the diffusion, we have

\langle-\Delta U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\Delta\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle,

with
\Delta\phi_{L}
uniformly bounded in
L
. For the nonlinear term, we split

|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|=|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\phi_{L},|U_{\operatorname{near}}|^{2}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle},

where we use the fact by (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) that
|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}},\widetilde{U}\in L^{4}\cap L^{\infty}
, and the uniform bounds on derivatives of
\phi_{L}
. For the pressure, we use the Riesz transform to represent
\Delta P=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}(\widetilde{U}_{i}\widetilde{U}_{j})
and Calderón–Zygmund theory give
\|P\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\widetilde{U}\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\lesssim 1
, hence

|\langle\nabla P,\,\phi_{L}U_{\mathrm{near}}\rangle|=|\langle P,\,\nabla\phi_{L}\cdot U_{\mathrm{near}}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

The forcing term has the same bound by the decay of
F
. Collecting the estimates yields a uniform bound on
\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle
, and we can take the limit
L\rightarrow\infty
to conclude

|\xi|U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2},\quad\quad|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}.

Weighted
L^{2}
after one derivative.

Differentiate the equation, test against
\chi_{R}\,\phi_{L}\,|\xi|^{\alpha}\,\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}}
with
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=2
, and repeat the previous bounds (now also using that
|\xi|^{\alpha}
is an admissible weight for the pressure estimate). One obtains, after letting
R\to\infty
and then
L\to\infty
,

|\xi|\,\nabla^{2}U_{\mathrm{near}}\in L^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}},\ |\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\mathrm{near}}\in L^{2}.

Dyadic rescaling and decay.
Fix
R>1
and the annulus
A_{R}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\ R<|\xi|<2R\}
.
Define
v(y):=U_{\mathrm{near}}(Ry)
on
A_{1}=\{y:\ 1<|y|<2\}
. By the local Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(A_{1})\subset L^{\infty}(A_{1})
,

\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{R})}=\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{1})}\leqslant C\sum_{k=0}^{2}\|\nabla_{y}^{k}v\|_{L^{2}(A_{1})}=C\sum_{k=0}^{2}R^{k-\frac{3}{2}}\|\nabla^{k}U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}(A_{R})}.

Since
|\xi|\sim R
on
A_{R}
and the global weighted bounds imply

\|\nabla^{k}U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}(A_{R})}\leqslant CR^{-k}
for
k=0,1,2
, we conclude

\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{R})}\leqslant C\,R^{-3/2},

that is,
U_{\mathrm{near}}(\xi)=O(|\xi|^{-3/2})
as
|\xi|\to\infty
, the desired decay.

Now we have established that
\widetilde{U}
indeed induces a self-similar Leray–Hopf solution.

2.2
Nonuniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Let
\widetilde{u}
be the self-similar solution corresponding to the profile
\widetilde{U}
in Theorem
2
. Recall that due to the slow decay of the scale-invariant initial data
u_{\text{in}}
,
\widetilde{u}
is not a Leray-Hopf solution. Therefore, we introduce a suitable cutoff function
u^{\text{cut}}
and
consider the following ansatz
u=\widetilde{u}+u^{\text{cut}}+u^{\text{cor}}
to the Navier–Stokes equation (
1.1
), where
u^{\text{cor}}
is the correction to be constructed that renders non-unique solutions. We adopt the change of variables between physical coordinates
(x,t)
and self-similar coordinates
(\xi,\tau)
:

\widetilde{u}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\widetilde{U}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}}),\quad u^{\text{cut}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}U^{\text{cut}}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}},\log t),\quad u^{\text{cor}}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}U^{\text{cor}}(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}},\log t).

Please note that
u=\widetilde{u}+u^{\text{cut}}+u^{\text{cor}}
is the Leray-Hopf solution that we construct in this subsection. It does not correspond to the correction
U
that we used in (
1.16
) to adjust
\overline{U}
; consequently,
u
does not satisfy the relation
u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}U(\frac{x}{\sqrt{t}},\log t)
with that
U
. We will first construct the cutoff function, as in
[
30
]
. Then we demonstrate that different choices of
u^{\text{cor}}(\cdot,1)
correspond to different solutions in the rescaled time interval
\tau\in(-\infty,0]
, as in
[
1
]
and
[
30
]
. Here, it is crucial to treat the stable and unstable parts separately via the Riesz projection, as in
[
30
]
; see also
[
11
,
10
]
.

Construction of cutoff.

As in
[
30
]
, via the Bogovskii operator, one can construct a decomposition of the initial condition
u_{\operatorname{in}}=u_{\operatorname{loc}}+w
such that
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
is compactly supported, divergence free,
w(x)=0
for
|x|<R
and
\|w\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim R^{-(p-3)/p}
based on the decay of
u_{\operatorname{in}}
, for any
p>3
. We will choose
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
of compact support as the modified initial data for our Leray–Hopf solution
u
.

Now we define our cutoff function as

u^{\text{cut}}=-e^{t\Delta}w,

where
e^{t\Delta}
is the standard notation for the heat semigroup. Recall the standard smoothing estimate:

\|\nabla_{x}^{k}e^{t\Delta}f\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{k}{2}}\,t^{-\frac{3}{2}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r})}\|f\|_{L^{p}},\quad 1\leqslant p\leqslant r\leqslant\infty.

(2.7)

By the similarity transform, we have the identities

\nabla_{\xi}^{k}U^{\text{cut}}(\xi,\tau)=t^{\frac{k}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}(\nabla_{x}^{k}u^{\text{cut}})(x,t)\Big|_{x=\sqrt{t}\xi},\quad\|g(\sqrt{t}\,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}}=t^{-\frac{3}{2r}}\|g\|_{L^{r}}.

(2.8)

Combining (
2.8
), (
2.7
), and the bound on the
p
-norm of
w
, we conclude the estimate

\|U^{\text{cut}}\|_{W^{k,r}}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2p}}\|w\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim(\frac{e^{\frac{\tau}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}.

(2.9)

We fix
p=4
from now on.
The constants in the inequalities are independent of
R
and
\tau
.

Duhamel setup.

We will find solutions
u^{\text{cor}}
to the following perturbative equation to (
1.1
)

\partial_{t}u^{\text{cor}}-\Delta u^{\text{cor}}+\Pi(u\cdot\nabla u-\widetilde{u}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{u})=0,\quad\operatorname{div}u^{\text{cor}}=0,

with
u^{\text{cor}}\to 0
in
L^{2}
as
t\to 0
so that
u=\widetilde{u}+u^{\text{cut}}+u^{\text{cor}}
will be a solution to the Navier–Stokes equation in
L^{2}
with initial data
u_{\operatorname{in}}-w=u_{\operatorname{loc}}
. In similarity coordinates, the equation for
U^{\text{cor}}
reads

\partial_{\tau}U^{\text{cor}}=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}U^{\text{cor}}+\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}U^{\text{cor}}+F+N(U^{\text{cor}},U^{\text{cor}}),

(2.10)

where we use the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}
in (
1.9
) and define the linear, quadratic, and residue terms as

\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}f:=-\Pi(U^{\text{cut}}\cdot\nabla f+f\cdot\nabla U^{\text{cut}}),\quad N(f,g):=-\Pi(f\cdot\nabla g),\quad F:=-\Pi(U^{\text{cut}}\cdot\nabla U^{\text{cut}}+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla U^{\text{cut}}+U^{\text{cut}}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}).

The Duhamel mapping, of which we want to show
U^{\text{cor}}
is a fixed point, reads

\mathcal{T}(U)(\cdot,\tau):=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{(\tau-s)\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}U(\cdot,s)+F(\cdot,s)+N(U(\cdot,s),U(\cdot,s)))ds.

(2.11)

By (
2.9
), the fact that
H^{2}
is an algebra, namely,

\|fg\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{2}}\|g\|_{H^{2}},

together with the fact that
U^{\text{cut}},\widetilde{U}
are in
W^{\infty,4}
, we have

\|\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}f(\cdot,s)\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim(\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}\|f\|_{H^{3}},\quad\|N(f,g)\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{3}}\|g\|_{H^{3}},\quad\|F(\cdot,s)\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim(\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}.

(2.12)

Decomposition via Riesz projection.

From Section 4.1 of
[
1
]
, we know that for the operators defined on
L^{2}_{\sigma}\to L^{2}_{\sigma}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}-\mathcal{L}_{0}
is a compact perturbation of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, and thus
\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}
has the same essential spectrum as
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, contained in the half-plane
\Re(\lambda)\leqslant-\frac{1}{4}
. Here we have used the same notation
\mathcal{L}_{0}:=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}\big|_{\widetilde{U}\equiv 0}
as in
[
1
,
30
]
. The other spectral values are finite sets of eigenvalues with finite ranks.

We proceed as in the proof of Theorem 4.1 in
[
30
]
to decompose into stable and unstable subspaces
L^{2}_{\sigma}=X_{s}\oplus_{j}X_{j}
via the Riesz projection
P_{\lambda_{j}}:L^{2}_{\sigma}\to X_{j}
. For every eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
with a positive real part, we define
P_{\lambda_{j}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\Gamma_{j}}(\lambda-\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}})^{-1}d\lambda
, where
\Gamma_{j}
is a suitable contour containing
{\lambda_{j}}
but no other spectral values. Here, each
X_{j}
is finite dimensional, and we define
P_{s}=I-\sum_{j}P_{\lambda_{j}}
. Finally, we denote
A_{j}=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}|_{X_{j}}
and
A_{s}=\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{U}}|_{X_{s}}
. Using Jordan block computation, we have the following estimates for any
\delta>0
by

[
22
]
:

\displaystyle e^{\Re(\lambda_{j})\tau-\delta|\tau|}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|e^{\tau A_{j}}f\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{\Re(\lambda_{j})\tau+\delta|\tau|}\|f\|_{L^{2}},\quad\forall f\in X_{j},\forall\tau,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|e^{\tau A_{s}}f\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{\delta\tau}\|f\|_{L^{2}},\quad\forall f\in X_{s},\tau>0,

since
A_{s}
has a growth bound equal to the maximum of its spectral bound and its essential spectrum (Lemma 4.2 of
[
1
]
), which is non-positive. We rewrite (
2.11
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}(\cdot,\tau):=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{(\tau-s)A_{s}}P_{s}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}U+F+N(U,U))ds,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}(\cdot,\tau):=e^{\tau A_{j}}U_{j0}-\int_{\tau}^{0}e^{(\tau-s)A_{j}}P_{j}(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{far}}U+F+N(U,U))ds,

and we need to show the existence of fixed points
\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}=P_{s}U
,
\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}=P_{j}U
, for appropriately chosen
U_{j0}=P_{j}U(\cdot,0)\in X_{j}
. We remark that by Theorem
2
, we have at least one unstable
j
.

Smoothing estimates of
A_{s}
.

With the growth bound (
2.13
), we obtain the smoothing estimate as in Lemma 4.4 of
[
1
]
: for any
\delta>0
,
\sigma_{2}\geqslant\sigma_{1}\geqslant 0
, there exists a constant only depending on
\delta,\sigma_{2},\sigma_{1}
, such that for any
f_{s}\in X_{s}\cap H^{\sigma_{1}}
and
\tau>0
, we have

\|e^{\tau A_{s}}f_{s}\|_{H^{\sigma_{2}}}\lesssim\tau^{-(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1})/2}e^{\delta\tau}\|f_{s}\|_{H^{\sigma_{1}}},

(2.15)

whose proof essentially combines the
L^{2}
growth bound with a gain of regularity for the short time, as in Step 1 in the proof of Lemma 4.4 in
[
1
]
. Compact support of
\widetilde{U}
is not essential for the proof therein, as only its boundedness in
C^{k}
norms is used in the standard estimates of energy dissipation along with a gain of regularity by the heat kernel, relating back to the equation in physical time.

Every finite-dimensional unstable eigenspace
X_{j}
is the kernel of
(A_{j}-\lambda_{j})^{k_{j}}
for some finite
k_{j}
. Thus it is spanned by bases
\{f_{j,k}\}
, where every
f_{j,k}
satisfies

(A_{j}-\lambda_{j})^{k_{j}}f_{j,k}=0.

Similarly, we have a smoothing estimate for
A_{j}
, so the bases
f_{j,k}
are smooth.

Contraction mapping.

We recall
p=4
and fix
\delta=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\frac{p-3}{2p},\min_{j}\Re(\lambda_{j})\}>0
. Consider the norm
\|U\|_{X}:=\sup_{\tau<0}R^{\frac{p-3}{2p}}e^{-\delta\tau}\|U(\cdot,\tau)\|_{H^{3}}
. We will show that there exists a sufficiently large
R
such that the Duhamel mapping
\mathcal{T}
in (
2.14
) induces a contraction on the unit ball of
X
.

Thanks to the integral computation for
\delta_{1}>\delta
that

\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}(\tau-s)^{-1/2}e^{\delta(\tau-s)}e^{\delta_{1}s}ds\leqslant e^{\delta\tau}(\int_{-\infty}^{\tau-1}e^{(\delta_{1}-\delta)s}ds+\int_{\tau-1}^{\tau}(\tau-s)^{-1/2}ds)\lesssim e^{\delta\tau},

by (
2.14
), (
2.15
), and (
2.12
), we have for
\tau<0
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{H^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}(\tau-s)^{-1/2}e^{\delta(\tau-s)}((\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}(1+\|U(\cdot,s)\|_{H^{3}})+\|U(\cdot,s)\|_{H^{3}}^{2})ds

(2.16)

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\delta\tau}R^{-1+\frac{3}{p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|U\|_{X}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}(\cdot,\tau)-\mathcal{T}(V)_{s}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{H^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}(\tau-s)^{-1/2}e^{\delta(\tau-s)}((\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}+\|U\|_{H^{3}}+\|V\|_{H^{3}})\|U-V\|_{H^{3}}ds

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\delta\tau}R^{-1+\frac{3}{p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|V\|_{X})\|U-V\|_{X}.

Multiplying
R^{\frac{p-3}{2p}}e^{-\delta\tau}
to (
2.16
), and taking
\sup_{\tau<0}
give

\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}\|_{X}\lesssim R^{-\frac{p-3}{2p}}\big(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|U\|_{X}^{2}\big),\quad\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{s}-\mathcal{T}(V)_{s}\|_{X}\lesssim R^{-\frac{p-3}{2p}}\big(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|V\|_{X}\big)\|U-V\|_{X}.

(2.17)

Thus, the stable part of
\mathcal{T}
is a strict contraction on the unit ball of
X
for
R
sufficiently large.

Since
U_{j0}\in X_{j}
are smooth, using (
2.13
),
for
\tau<0
, we have similarly that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{H^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{(\Re(\lambda_{j})-\delta)\tau}\|U_{j0}\|_{H^{3}}+\int_{\tau}^{0}e^{(\Re(\lambda_{j})-\delta)(\tau-s)}((\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}(1+\|U\|_{H^{3}})+\|U\|_{H^{3}}^{2})ds

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\delta\tau}\|U_{j0}\|_{H^{3}}+e^{\delta\tau}R^{-1+\frac{3}{p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|U\|_{X}^{2}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}(\cdot,\tau)-\mathcal{T}(V)_{j}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{H^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\tau}^{0}e^{(\Re(\lambda_{j})-\delta)(\tau-s)}((\frac{e^{\frac{s}{2}}}{R})^{(1-\frac{3}{p})}+\|U\|_{H^{3}}+\|V\|_{H^{3}})\|U-V\|_{H^{3}}ds

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\delta\tau}R^{-1+\frac{3}{p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|V\|_{X})\|U-V\|_{X}.

Similarly, by multiplying
R^{\frac{p-3}{2p}}e^{-\delta\tau}
to the above inequalities and taking
\sup_{\tau<0}
give

\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}\|_{X}\lesssim R^{\frac{p-3}{2p}}\|U_{j0}\|_{H^{3}}+R^{-\frac{p-3}{2p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|U\|_{X}^{2}),

(2.18)

\|\mathcal{T}(U)_{j}-\mathcal{T}(V)_{j}\|_{X}\lesssim R^{-\frac{p-3}{2p}}(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|V\|_{X})\|U-V\|_{X}.

(2.19)

Denote
\alpha:=1-\tfrac{3}{p}=\frac{1}{4}
.
For a small
\eta\in(0,1)
, consider the nonempty admissible set of the unit ball of
X
with fixed unstable projections at
\tau=0
:

\mathcal{D}_{R,\eta,U_{j0}}:=\Bigl\{\|U\|_{X}\leqslant 1:\ P_{j}U(\cdot,0)=U_{j0}\quad\forall j\Bigr\},

where
\sum_{j}R^{\alpha/2}\|U_{j0}\|_{H^{3}}\leqslant\eta
. Let
C
denote the maximal absolute constant in the inequalities (
2.17
), (
2.18
), and (
2.19
).
Choose
R
so large that
C\sum_{j}R^{-\alpha/2}\leqslant\frac{1}{12}
, and then choose
\eta>0
small enough so that

C\eta\leqslant\tfrac{1}{4}
. For any
U\in\mathcal{D}_{R,\eta,U_{j0}}
, we get by (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) that

\|\mathcal{T}(U)\|_{X}\ \leqslant\ CR^{\alpha/2}\sum_{j}\|U_{j0}\|_{H^{3}}+2C\sum_{j}R^{-\alpha/2}\bigl(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|U\|_{X}^{2}\bigr)\leqslant\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{1}{2}<1,

and hence we have
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{D}_{R,\eta,U_{j0}})\subset\mathcal{D}_{R,\eta,U_{j0}}
.
Moreover, we obtain by (
2.17
) and (
2.19
) that

\|\mathcal{T}(U)-\mathcal{T}(V)\|_{X}\leqslant 2C\sum_{j}R^{-\alpha/2}\bigl(1+\|U\|_{X}+\|V\|_{X}\bigr)\|U-V\|_{X}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\,\|U-V\|_{X}.

Thus
\mathcal{T}
is a strict contraction on
\mathcal{D}_{R,\eta,U_{j0}}
.
Hence, we conclude a solution to (
2.11
) and thus (
2.10
) for any small enough admissible
U_{j0}
, which contains infinitely many distinct solutions
U^{\text{cor}}
, different by their projections at time
\tau=0
, i.e.
t=1
.

Conclusion of the proof.

We compute by the scaling invariance of the heat semigroup

\widetilde{u}(x)+u^{\text{cut}}(x)=\widetilde{u}(x)-e^{t\Delta}u^{\text{in}}(x)+e^{t\Delta}u_{\operatorname{loc}}(x)=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{U}-e^{\Delta}u^{\text{in}})(\xi)+e^{t\Delta}u_{\operatorname{loc}}.

The decay estimate
[
29
]
implies
\widetilde{U}-e^{\Delta}u^{\text{in}}\in L^{q}
for any
q>1
.
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
is of compact support with
1/|x|
at the origin, so it is in
L^{3-\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
. Combined with the heat smoothing estimate (
2.7
) and the scaling (
2.8
), we get for
t\leqslant 1
, for constants independent of
t
, we have

\|\widetilde{u}(x)+u^{\text{cut}}(x)\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim t^{\frac{3}{2q}-\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon/3)}}+1,\quad\limsup_{t\to 0}\|\widetilde{u}(x)+u^{\text{cut}}(x)-u_{\operatorname{loc}}(x)\|_{L^{2}}\to 0.

(2.20)

Finally,
\|u^{\text{cor}}\|_{L^{2}}=t^{1/4}\|U^{\text{cor}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
t\to 0
, and we conclude the constructed
u=\widetilde{u}+u^{\text{cut}}+u^{\text{cor}}
are infinitely many Leray–Hopf solutions with the same
L^{2}
initial data
u_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Their smoothness implies that they are moreover suitable and (
1.2
) holds with equality, as in
[
1
]
.

By (
2.20
), the scaling (
2.8
), and
U^{\text{cor}}\in H^{3}\subset L^{q}
, we compute for
q\geqslant 2
,

\|u\|_{L^{q}}\leqslant\|u^{\text{cut}}+\widetilde{u}\|_{L^{q}}+\|u^{\text{cor}}\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim t^{\frac{3}{2q}-\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon/3)}}+1+t^{\frac{3}{2q}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim t^{\frac{3}{2q}-\frac{1}{2(1-\epsilon/3)}}+1.

Choosing
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, we conclude the
L^{s}L^{q}
boundedness and the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

3
A Framework for Numerical Analysis

In this section, we introduce a framework for
a posteriori
numerical analysis.
Consider a general nonlinear partial differential equation of the form:

J(\widetilde{w})=0.

(3.1)

Suppose we have an approximate solution
\overline{w}
, which satisfies

J(\overline{w})=\mathrm{E},

where the residual
\mathrm{E}
is small in a sense to be clarified later. Our
goal is to find an exact solution
\widetilde{w}=\overline{w}+w
that is close to
\overline{w}
and
satisfies

J(\overline{w}+w)=0,

where
w
represents the error between the exact and approximate solutions.

We expand the equation in terms of the linearized operator
L
and the nonlinear part
N
as

Lw+N(w)+\mathrm{E}=0.

(3.2)

If we can solve (
3.2
) for some
w
, then
\widetilde{w}=\overline{w}+w
solves the original equation (
3.1
). Since
w
is small,
the nonlinear term
N(w)
should be negligible and (
3.2
) is almost
linear. Therefore, the invertibility of the linear operator
L
essentially
determines the well-posedness of the equation (
3.2
), and we can use a fixed-point argument to conclude the existence of a solution.

In this section, we will develop a framework of computer-assisted proof for
the existence of solutions to equation (
3.2
), making the above heuristics rigorous. We will first
introduce a simplified example of a nonlinear Poisson equation to illustrate and motivate our method in Section

3.1
, followed by a general result in Section
3.2
, when
L
is not exactly invertible but can be decomposed into a coercive part plus a compact part. We remark that instead of invoking the Banach fixed-point theorem via the proof of a contraction mapping, we will use the Schauder fixed-point theorem since our fixed-point map can be shown to have a gain of regularity. We spend more effort on compactness arguments for analysis, so that the computer-assisted proofs will be easier and to the minimal extent possible.

3.1
Toy model: nonlinear Poisson equation

We illustrate our framework with a simple toy model: the nonlinear Poisson
equation on
D=[0,1]^{3}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta\widetilde{w}+\widetilde{w}^{2}=f,\text{\quad in }D,\\
\widetilde{w}=0,\text{\quad on }\partial D.\end{array}\right.

In general, we do not know the existence of the solution. Suppose we have a bounded
numerical solution
\overline{w}
satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta\overline{w}+\overline{w}^{2}=f+\mathrm{E}_{w},\text{\quad in }D,\\
\overline{w}=0,\text{\quad on }\partial D,\end{array}\right.

where
\mathrm{E}_{w}
stands for the residual. If an exact solution exists, the
error
w=\widetilde{w}-\overline{w}
should satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\Delta w+2\overline{w}w+w^{2}=-\mathrm{E}_{w},\text{\quad in }D,\\
w=0,\text{\quad on }\partial D.\end{array}\right.

(3.3)

In this formulation, we identify the linear and nonlinear components as:

Lw:=-\Delta w+2\overline{w}w,\quad N(w):=w^{2}.

We now analyze two cases. In the first case, we assume
L
is invertible and use a fixed-point argument to construct the solution. In the second case,
where the invertibility of
L
is unknown, we will use a finite-rank perturbation to approximate the compact part and verify numerically the invertibility of
L
on the image of the finite-rank perturbation. In this subsection, since all of the norms and inner products are defined on
D
, we will omit the
D
dependence.

3.1.1
Simple case:
\overline{w}\geqslant 0

First, we consider a simple case where
\overline{w}\geqslant 0
and
L
is
thus coercive. Specifically,

\langle Lw,w\rangle=\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle 2\overline{w},w^{2}\rangle\geqslant\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Hence, by the Lax-Milgram theorem,
L
is invertible in
H^{1}_{0}(D)
. Now we define
a fixed-point map
w^{\ast}=T(w)
by solving in
H^{1}_{0}(D)

Lw^{\ast}=-\mathrm{E}_{w}-N(w).

The solution
w^{\ast}
exists and satisfies

\|\nabla w^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\langle Lw^{\ast},w^{\ast}\rangle\leqslant|\langle\mathrm{E}_{w},w^{\ast}\rangle|+|\langle w^{2},w^{\ast}\rangle|.

By Holder’s inequality with multiple functions and Sobolev embedding, we have

\|\nabla w^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathrm{E}_{w}\|_{L^{2}}\|w^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}+\|w\|_{L^{3}}^{2}\|w^{\ast}\|_{L^{3}}\lesssim(\|\mathrm{E}_{w}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}^{2})\|\nabla w^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}},

where we have used the Poincaré inequality
\|w^{*}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla w^{*}\|_{L^{2}}
since
w^{*}
vanishes on
\partial D
.

Assume that the residual is small, i.e.,

\|\mathrm{E}_{w}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{w}\ll 1.

Then the following estimate holds for
w^{\ast}
:

\|\nabla w^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C(\varepsilon_{w}+\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}).

If
\varepsilon_{w}
is sufficiently small, we can choose a small radius
r
such that

C(\varepsilon_{w}+r^{2})\leqslant r.

This implies the map
T
sends the ball

B_{r}:=\{w\in H^{1}_{0}(D):\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant r\}

to itself. Next, we prove that
T
is continuous in a weaker topology. Let
w^{\ast}=T(w)
,
\widehat{w}^{\ast}=T(\widehat{w})
, for
w,\widehat{w}\in B_{r}
. Then we get

L(w^{\ast}-\widehat{w}^{\ast})=w^{2}-\widehat{w}^{2}=(w-\widehat{w})(w+\widehat{w}),

and similarly, using coercivity and Sobolev embedding, we have

\|w^{\ast}-\widehat{w}^{\ast}\|_{L^{3}}\lesssim\|\nabla(w^{\ast}-\widehat{w}^{\ast})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim(\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\widehat{w}\|_{L^{2}})\|w-\widehat{w}\|_{L^{3}}\lesssim r\|w-\widehat{w}\|_{L^{3}}.

We know that
T
is continuous in
L^{3}
-norm on
B_{r}
. Since the bounded ball in
H^{1}
is compact in
L^{3}
, by the Schauder fixed-point theorem, a solution to the equation (
3.3
) exists.

3.1.2
Difficult case:
\overline{w}\leqslant 0

Next, we consider a more difficult case, where
\overline{w}\leqslant 0
. As
before, we aim to establish the existence of a solution to equation
(
3.3
). However, the operator
L
is no longer
coercive, and directly proving its invertibility is challenging. To address
this, we observe that the operator can be decomposed as
L=L_{1}+L_{2}
, where

L_{1}w:=-\Delta w
is coercive and
L_{2}w:=2\overline{w}w
is a compact perturbation.

Following the strategy in
[
12
]
, we leverage
computer-assisted methods to avoid directly verifying the invertibility of

L
. Specifically, we decompose
w
into
w=w_{1}+w_{2}
with:

\displaystyle-\Delta w_{1}=-\mathrm{E}_{w}-(w_{1}+w_{2})^{2},

\displaystyle-\Delta w_{2}+L_{2}w_{2}=-L_{2}w_{1}.

Summing up the two equations implies
(
3.3
). The key observation is that
w_{2}=-L^{-1}L_{2}w_{1}
. If
L_{2}
were of of finite-rank instead, we would only need to verify the invertibility of
L
on a
finite-dimensional space, which can be done numerically.

In our case, however,
L_{2}=2\overline{w}
is compact but not of finite rank.
To circumvent this, we approximate
w_{2}
using a finite-rank operator. That
is, we modify the system to:

\displaystyle-\Delta w_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1}=-\mathrm{E}_{w}-(w_{1}+w_{2})^{2},

(3.4)

\displaystyle-\Delta w_{2}+L_{2}w_{2}=-L_{\operatorname{ap}}w_{1}.

(3.5)

Here,
L_{\operatorname{ap}}
is a finite rank approximation of
L_{2}
, and the
residual
L_{\operatorname{res}}:=L_{2}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}
is small and can be
absorbed by the coercivity of
L_{1}
. Therefore, the modified linear part of
equation (
3.4
), namely
-\Delta w_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1}
, remains coercive, enabling a fixed-point argument for the existence
of
w_{1}
.

Now we show how to construct
L_{\operatorname{ap}}
. To begin with, we choose an
interpolation operator
I_{n}
with a finite rank
n
to approximate
w
. This
operator, typically derived from finite element or spectral methods, ensures a
small approximation error:

\|w-I_{n}w\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\delta_{0}(n)\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}},

where
\delta_{0}(n)
tends to zero as
n
increases.
We use
L_{2}I_{n}
to approximate
L_{2}
. Consider an associated basis
\Psi=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n})
of the image of
I_{n}
. We can represent

I_{n}w_{1}
as:

I_{n}w_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})\psi_{j},

where
s_{j}(w_{1})
are the projection coefficients. Next, we verify the
invertibility of
L
by solving

-\Delta\phi_{i}+L_{2}\phi_{i}=-L_{2}\psi_{i},i=1,\ldots,n.

If this equation can be exactly solved, the solution of (
3.5
) is thus given by

w_{2}=Gw_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})\phi_{j}.

(3.6)

In practice, small numerical residuals are unavoidable. We will get
approximate solutions
\phi_{i}
with numerical residual
r_{i}
, that is:

L\phi_{i}=-L_{2}\psi_{i}+r_{i},i=1,\ldots,n.

These residuals are expected to be
small, ensuring that the overall approximation remains accurate enough. We can
absorb this residual into
\psi_{i}
by slightly modifying
L_{\operatorname{ap}}

as:

L_{\operatorname{ap}}w_{1}:=L_{2}I_{n}w_{1}+Rw_{1},\quad Rw_{1}:=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})r_{j}.

Then
w_{2}
defined by (
3.6
) solves the
equation (
3.5
) precisely. Here, we need to numerically
verify that
G
is bounded and
R
is small in
L^{2}(D)
, i.e.,

\|Rw_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{R}\|w_{1}\|_{L^{2}},\quad\|Gw_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}.

The boundedness of
G
ensures the invertibility of
L
within the
finite-dimensional space. Moreover, since the residual
R
is small,

L_{\operatorname{res}}=L_{2}(I-I_{n})-R
also remains small as we want.

Finally, by substituting (
3.6
) into (
3.4
), we address the first equation in our modified system:

-\Delta w_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1}+(w_{1}+Gw_{1})^{2}=-\mathrm{E}_{w}.

Using the Poincare’s inequality, which gives
\|w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle-\Delta w_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1},w_{1}\rangle=\langle-\Delta w_{1},w_{1}\rangle+\langle 2\overline{w}(w_{1}-I_{n}w_{1}),w_{1}\rangle-\langle Rw_{1},w_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\|\nabla w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-2\|\overline{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|w_{1}-I_{n}w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}-\|Rw_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\geqslant(1-C\delta_{0}(n)-C\varepsilon_{R})\|\nabla w_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Thus,
coercivity is preserved with large
n
and small
\varepsilon_{R}
.
Consequently, a fixed-point argument similar to the simpler scenario ensures
the existence of a solution to equation (
3.3
).

3.2
Generalization

In this section, we formalize the methods and ideas introduced earlier into a
general theorem. For our toy model, the essential structure of the linear operator is characterized by its decomposition into a coercive part and a
compact perturbation. Accordingly, we impose the following assumption for the
linear operator in the equation (
3.2
):

Assumption 2

(A priori bounds)

Let
\mathcal{H}^{0}
be a Hilbert space equipped with
inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}
and
corresponding norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}
. Suppose
\mathcal{H}^{1}\subset\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}^{0}
are dense subsets of
\mathcal{H}^{0}
, respectively endowed with stronger norms
{\mathcal{H}^{1}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}
, and that the identity embedding
\mathcal{H}^{1}\to\mathcal{B}
is compact. In particular, there exists a constant
K_{0}>0
such that

\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant K_{0}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},\quad\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}.

(3.7)

The operator

L
admits the decomposition:

L=L_{1}+L_{2},

where
L_{1}
is coercive and bounded, and
L_{2}
is a compact perturbation. Specifically, we impose:

1.

Coercivity of
L_{1}
for a constant
K_{1}>0
:

\langle L_{1}f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\geqslant K_{1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(3.8)

2.

Boundedness of
L_{1}
as a bilinear form, where the exact constant is not important:

|\langle L_{1}f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(3.9)

3.

Compactness of
L_{2}
via boundedness in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
for a constant
K_{2}>0
:

\|L_{2}f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant K_{2}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}^{0}.

(3.10)

3.2.1
Finite rank approximation

Following our earlier approach, we now construct a finite-rank approximation
of the operator
L_{2}
. First, we select an interpolation operator
I_{n}

satisfying:

\|f-I_{n}f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\delta_{1}(n)\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},

where
\delta_{1}(n)
tends to zero as
n
increases. Consider an associated basis
\Psi=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n})
of the image of
I_{n}
. We express
I_{n}f
in terms of its basis functions:

I_{n}f=S(f)\cdot\Psi:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(f)\psi_{j}.

Next, we construct a set of
functions
\Phi=(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{n})
satisfying:

L\phi_{i}=-L_{2}\psi_{i}+r_{i},i=1,\ldots,n,

(3.11)

where
R=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})^{T}
represents the small residual terms. The
finite-rank approximation operator
L_{\operatorname{ap}}
is defined as:

L_{\operatorname{ap}}w_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})(L_{2}\psi_{j}-r_{j}).

The residual of this approximation is given by:

L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1}:=(L_{2}-L_{\operatorname{ap}})w_{1}=L_{2}(I-I_{n})w_{1}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})r_{j}.

(3.12)

We then decompose the equation (
3.2
) into the following system:

L_{1}w_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}w_{1}=-\mathrm{E}-N(w_{1}+w_{2}),\quad Lw_{2}=-L_{\operatorname{ap}}w_{1}.

(3.13)

With the definitions of
\phi_{j}
and
s_{j}
established, the solution
w_{2}
is
explicitly represented as:

w_{2}=Gw_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(w_{1})\phi_{j}.

The boundedness of the operator
G
guarantees the invertibility of
L

within this finite-dimensional space. In summary, to validate the numerical
approach, we will verify the following assumption:

Assumption 3

(Finite-rank approximation)

For any
n
, consider the finite-rank operator
I_{n}
.
We assume that the interpolation error satisfies:

\|I_{n}f-f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\delta_{1}(n)\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},\quad\forall f\in\mathcal{H}^{1}.

(3.14)

Suppose
\Phi
solves equation (
3.11
) with a small residual
R
, specifically:

\|R\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1},

where
\varepsilon_{1}
, though dependent on
n
, can be made sufficiently
small in practice. The coefficients
S(f)
and the solution
\Phi
satisfy the bounds:

|S(f)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}},\quad|S(f)|\leqslant\gamma_{1}(n)\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},\quad\|\Phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant\gamma_{2}(n).

(3.15)

Here, in the first estimate of (
3.15
), we therefore have a gain of regularity that

\|Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}},

which is crucial for the continuity estimate in a weaker topology
\mathcal{B}
to follow, while the precise constant is not important.

3.2.2
Residual and nonlinear terms

In this subsection, we analyze the first equation of
(
3.13
). Having established the coercivity of
L_{1}
and the smallness of
L_{\operatorname{res}}
, we now shift our focus to the residual and nonlinear
terms.
Regarding the residual term, we pose the following assumption:

Assumption 4

(Residual estimate)

The residual
\mathrm{E}
is sufficiently small:

\|\mathrm{E}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{2},

where
\varepsilon_{2}
quantifies the
permissible magnitude of the residual.

For the nonlinear term, we pose the following assumption:

Assumption 5

(Nonlinear estimate)

The nonlinear term
N(w)
satisfies the bounded estimate:

|\langle N(u),w\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\leqslant K_{3}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},

and the continuous estimate

|\langle N(u_{1})-N(u_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\lesssim(\|u_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\|u_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Remark 3

We remark that the constant in the continuity estimate is not important, and that the nonlinear estimates ensure a continuous mapping in
\mathcal{B}
and a bounded mapping in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
, in preparation for the Schauder fixed-point argument.

Essentially, a quadratic nonlinearity combined with Sobolev embedding will satisfy the above assumptions.
These assumptions can be relaxed to incorporate the nonlinearities of other orders.

3.2.3
Main theorem

With the assumptions above, we establish the following result:

Theorem 3

(Existence of exact solutions in the abstract case)

If Assumptions
2
and

5
hold, Assumption

3
holds for sufficiently large
n
and a
sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{1}
, ensuring:

K_{1}-K_{0}K_{2}\delta_{1}(n)-K_{0}\gamma_{1}(n)\varepsilon_{1}>0,

(3.16)

and Assumption
4
holds with a
sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{2}
such that the determinant

M_{4}:=(K_{1}-K_{0}K_{2}\delta_{1}-K_{0}\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1})^{2}-4\varepsilon_{2}K_{0}K_{3}(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{2}\geqslant 0,

(3.17)

then the equation (
3.13
) has a solution
w_{1}
with:

\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant x^{w}:=\frac{K_{1}-K_{0}K_{2}\delta_{1}-K_{0}\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1}-\sqrt{M_{4}}}{2K_{3}(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{2}}.

(3.18)

Consequently, there exists a solution
\widetilde{w}
to equation
(
3.1
) such that:

\|\overline{w}-\widetilde{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})x^{w}.

Remark 4

The logical sequence for addressing the assumptions in the preceding theorem
is as follows: First, Assumptions
2
and

5
describe the qualitative properties of the operators and
must initially be verified. Next, we select a sufficiently large
n
to construct the
approximation operator so that Assumption
3
and (
3.16
) hold. Finally, the residue
\varepsilon_{2}
is chosen
sufficiently small satisfying Assumption
4
and (
3.17
).

Proof.

We employ a Schauder fixed-point argument to establish this theorem.

Well-definedness of the fixed point map.

We will first establish that
L_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}
induces a coercive and bounded bilinear form.

We begin by quantifying the smallness of the residual operator
L_{\operatorname{res}}
. Recall (
3.12
) that:

L_{\operatorname{res}}f=L_{2}(I-I_{n})f+\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(f)r_{j}.

Using the boundedness of
L_{2}
(
3.10
) and the finite-rank approximation (
3.14
), we have:

\|L_{2}(I-I_{n})f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant K_{2}\delta_{1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

For the second term, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have:

\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{j}(f)r_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}|s_{j}(f)|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|r_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}}=|S(f)|\|R\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},

where we use Assumption
3
in the last inequality.
We conclude that

\|L_{\operatorname{res}}f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant(K_{2}\delta_{1}+\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1})\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

(3.19)

We conclude the coercivity of
L_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}}
using estimates (
3.8
), (
3.19
), and the embedding (
3.7
):

\langle(L_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}})f,f\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\geqslant(K_{1}-K_{0}K_{2}\delta_{1}-K_{0}\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1})\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}.

(3.20)

The boundedness is trivial by (
3.19
), (
3.7
), and Assumption
2
. By Lax-Milgram, we define the mapping
T
by
setting
T(w_{1})=w_{1}^{\ast}
, where
w_{1}^{\ast}
solves:

(L_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}})w_{1}^{\ast}=-\mathrm{E}-N(w_{1}+Gw_{1}).

(3.21)

A priori estimate in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
.

From Assumption
4
and
5
, the residual term and the nonlinear term are respectively controlled by:

|\langle\mathrm{E},w_{1}^{\ast}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\leqslant\varepsilon_{2}\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{2}K_{0}\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},

(3.22)

|\langle N(w_{1}+Gw_{1}),w_{1}^{\ast}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\leqslant K_{3}\|w_{1}+Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant K_{3}(\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\|Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})^{2}\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

The estimate of
Gw_{1}
follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\|Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{n}|s_{j}(w_{1})|\|\phi_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant|S(w_{1})|\|\Phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},

(3.23)

where we use (
3.15
) from Assumption
3
.
We finally have

|\langle N(w_{1}+Gw_{1}),w_{1}^{\ast}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|\leqslant K_{3}(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{2}\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

(3.24)

By collecting the estimates (
3.20
), (
3.22
), and (
3.24
), we obtain:

(K_{1}-K_{0}K_{2}\delta_{1}-K_{0}\gamma_{1}\varepsilon_{1})\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{2}K_{0}+K_{3}(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})^{2}\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Solving the inequality as in (
3.18
) shows that
if
\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant x^{w}
, then
\|w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant x^{w}
.
T
maps the ball
B_{M}
to itself.

Continuity estimate in
\mathcal{B}
.

Next, we establish the continuity of
T
. Let
w_{1}^{\ast}=T(w_{1}),\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}=T(\widehat{w}_{1})
. Subtracting the equation (
3.21
) that they satisfy, we get:

(L_{1}+L_{\operatorname{res}})(w_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast})=N(\widehat{w}_{1}+G\widehat{w}_{1})-N(w_{1}+Gw_{1}).

Instead of the estimate (
3.23
), we need the gain of regularity in
G
by (
3.15
):

\|Gf\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant|S(f)|\|\Phi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{B}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}},
