where we use the comparison of the norms
{\mathcal{H}^{0}},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}^{1}
in Assumption
2
.
Combining with the nonlinear estimate in Assumption
5
, the difference between the nonlinear term is
bounded by

\displaystyle|\langle N(\widehat{w}_{1}+G\widehat{w}_{1})-N(w_{1}+Gw_{1}),\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}-w_{1}^{\ast}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|w_{1}+Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\|\widehat{w}_{1}+G\widehat{w}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})\|\widehat{w}_{1}-w_{1}+G\widehat{w}_{1}-Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\|\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}-w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\|\widehat{w}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}})\|\widehat{w}_{1}-w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\|\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}-w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\lesssim\|\widehat{w}_{1}-w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\|\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}-w_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{B}},

for
\widehat{w}_{1},{w}_{1}\in B_{M}
.
By the coercivity estimate (
3.20
) and the embedding (
3.7
), it follows that

\|w_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{B}}\lesssim\|w_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim\|\widehat{w}_{1}-w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\|w_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

If
w_{1}^{\ast}\neq\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}
, cancel the common factor to obtain

\|w_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{w}_{1}^{\ast}\|_{\mathcal{B}}\;\lesssim\;\|\widehat{w}_{1}-w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Hence
T
is (Lipschitz) continuous in the weaker norm
\mathcal{B}
.

The ball
B_{M}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
is compact in
\mathcal{B}
by Assumption
2
, and by the Schauder fixed-point theorem, there exists
a solution
w_{1}
to equation (
3.13
) satisfying

\|w_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant x^{w}.
By (
3.23
), we know that

\|\overline{w}-\widetilde{w}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=\|w_{1}+Gw_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\leqslant(1+\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})x^{w}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

4
Proof of Theorem
2
: Analysis Part

In this section, we apply the framework developed earlier to establish Theorem

2
, whose proof consists of two parts: demonstrating the
existence of the profile
\widetilde{U}
, and proving the existence of the
eigenpair
(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{v})
, via Propositions
1
and
2
.
We will use superscript
U
and
v
, such as
L^{U}
and
L^{v}
, to indicate dependence on the operators associated with Propositions
1
and
2
, respectively. The strategies are largely the same, with minor modifications highlighted. From this section onward, we will only work in the self-similar variables, and upon an abuse of notation, we still use
x
rather than
\xi
for the spatial variables.

Remark 5

(Key Idea)

In order to obtain sharper constants, we choose to
verify Assumptions
2
and
4
, while
replacing Assumptions
3
and
5
by obtaining the
key estimates (
3.20
), (
3.24
), and (
3.2.3
) from the proof of Theorem
3
directly.

We remark that the key challenge lies in the linear estimate (
3.20
), while the nonlinear and residual estimates are more straightforward. The crucial changes are that the linear operator is no longer bounded due to the term
x\cdot\nabla
, and that
H^{1}
is not compactly embedded into
L^{2}
on the whole space.
We truncate the domain so that the associated bilinear form will be bounded, as in Assumption
2
to apply Lax-Milgram. Moreover, thanks to the gain of regularity, we show that the fixed point map maps a bounded set in
H^{1}
to itself and is continuous in
L^{4}
. Therefore, in the truncated domain, we can apply the Schauder fixed-point theorem to obtain a fixed point, and taking weak limits yields the desired solution.

We first discuss the concrete setting and the linear operators in Section
4.1
. The finite-rank approximation for a general type of compact perturbations applicable to
U
and
v
simultaneously is discussed in Section
4.2
. Proposition
1
is addressed in Section
4.3
, as
Proposition
2
follows a similar verification process in
Section
4.4
. Section

4.5
briefly presents our numerical results, providing evidence
for the existence of the solution. Implementation details of the numerical
algorithm are deferred to Sections
5
and
6
.

4.1
Setting and decomposition of linear operators

We discuss the setting and the definition of the linear operators.

Setting for
U
for Proposition
1
.

For
U
, we separate the linear and nonlinear components in equation (
1.15
):

\displaystyle L^{U}U

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}U-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla U-\Delta U+\Pi(\overline{U}\cdot\nabla U+U\cdot\nabla\overline{U}),

\displaystyle N^{U}(U)

\displaystyle:=\Pi(U\cdot\nabla U).

Our strategy involves decomposing the linear operator
L^{U}
into two parts as in Section
3
: a
coercive operator
L_{1}^{U}
and a compact operator
L_{2}^{U}
. We first specify the Hilbert spaces under consideration:
\mathcal{H}^{0}=L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\mathcal{H}^{1}=H^{1}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For numerical purposes, we will later restrict to a subspace with certain symmetries, which does not affect the analysis presented below. Noticing the cancellation via an integration by parts, an initial attempt at this decomposition could be:

L_{1}^{U}U=-\frac{1}{2}U-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla U-\Delta U+\Pi(\overline{U}\cdot\nabla U),\quad L_{2}^{U}U=\Pi(U\cdot\nabla\overline{U}).

While this decomposition is correct, the construction of a finite rank approximation prompts
us to ensure the matrix function in
L_{2}^{U}
is compactly supported. To achieve
this, we truncate the gradient
\nabla\overline{U}
as follows. We first
decompose
\nabla\overline{U}
into antisymmetric and symmetric parts:

\nabla\overline{U}(x)=Q_{a}(x)+Q_{s}(x),\quad Q_{a}(x):=\frac{\nabla\overline{U}(x)-\nabla^{T}\overline{U}(x)}{2},\quad Q_{s}(x):=\frac{\nabla\overline{U}(x)+\nabla^{T}\overline{U}(x)}{2}.

Note that
Q_{a}
is antisymmetric and thus does not contribute to the scalar
product
\langle L_{2}^{U}U,U\rangle
. Therefore, we focus on truncating the
symmetric part
Q_{s}
.

To truncate
Q_{s}
, we choose a constant
0<\eta_{1}<\frac{1}{4}
. Then, the
symmetric matrix function
Q_{s}+\eta_{1}I_{3}
(where
I_{3}
is the
3\times 3

identity matrix) is decomposed into positive and negative parts:

Q_{s}(x)+\eta_{1}I_{3}=Q_{p}(x)-Q(x),\quad Q_{p}(x)\geqslant 0,\quad Q(x)\geqslant 0.

Since
\nabla\overline{U}(x)
decays at infinity, we observe that:

\lim_{|x|\to+\infty}Q_{s}(x)+\eta_{1}I_{3}=\eta_{1}I_{3}\geqslant 0.

Therefore, the negative part
Q(x)
of
Q_{s}(x)
has compact support. Using
\nabla\overline{U}=Q_{a}+Q_{p}-\eta_{1}I_{3}-Q
, the operator
L_{U}
is finally decomposed as:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{U}U

\displaystyle:=(-\eta_{1}-\frac{1}{2})U-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla U-\Delta U+\Pi(\overline{U}\cdot\nabla U+U\cdot(Q_{a}+Q_{p})),

(4.1)

\displaystyle L_{2}^{U}U

\displaystyle:=-\Pi(U\cdot Q).

(4.2)

With this refined decomposition, we explicitly verify coercivity:

\langle L_{1}^{U}U,U\rangle=(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1})\|U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle Q_{p}U,U\rangle\geqslant(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1})\|U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.3)

Since
Q
is bounded of compact support,
L_{2}^{U}
is a compact operator from

\mathcal{H}^{1}
to
\mathcal{H}^{0}
and bounded from
\mathcal{H}^{0}
to
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. We have verified Assumption
2
: the coercivity of
L_{U}^{1}
and the compactness of
L_{U}^{2}
.

Setting for
v
for Proposition
2
.

For
v
, the only nonlinear term is
\lambda v
, and we need to decompose
\lambda
appropriately. Starting from the equation
(
1.17
), we take the inner product with
\overline{v}
to obtain:

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\langle-\frac{1}{2}v-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla v-\Delta v+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+U\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla U)-\overline{\lambda}v-\lambda v+\Pi\mathrm{E}^{v},\overline{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla v-\Delta v+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla U,\overline{v}\rangle,

where we use orthogonalities to
\overline{v}
and divergence-free assumptions in Assumption
1
combined with an integration by parts.
It can be decomposed into:

\lambda(v)=\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v)+\lambda^{\text{con}},\quad\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v):=\langle-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla v-\Delta v+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U},\overline{v}\rangle,\quad\lambda^{\text{con}}:=\langle\overline{v}\cdot\nabla U,\overline{v}\rangle.

Here
\lambda^{\text{lin}}
is linear in
v
, while
\lambda^{\text{con}}
is independent of
v
with the trivial bound

|\lambda^{\text{con}}|\leqslant\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{\infty}.

(4.4)

Consequently, equation
(
1.17
) can be decomposed as:

L^{v}v=-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v}-N^{v}(v),

where we define

\displaystyle L^{v}v

\displaystyle:=-(\frac{1}{2}+\overline{\lambda}+\lambda^{\text{con}})v-\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla v-\Delta v+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v)\overline{v},

\displaystyle N^{v}(v)

\displaystyle:=-\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v)v,\quad\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v}:=\Pi\mathrm{E}^{v}+\Pi(U\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla U)-\lambda^{\text{con}}\overline{v}.

L^{v}
resembles
L^{U}
,
except for a few additional terms in the linear operator. The

(\overline{\lambda}+\lambda^{\text{con}})v
term provides enhanced coercivity, while the
extra terms
U\cdot\nabla v
and
v\cdot\nabla U
are treated as
perturbations.
The term
-\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v)\overline{v}
should be interpreted as a projection.
More explicitly, we define:

P_{\overline{v}}f:=f-\langle f,\overline{v}\rangle\overline{v}.

Since
\overline{v}
is divergence-free, it follows that
P_{\overline{v}}

commutes with the Leray projection operator
\Pi
. With this projection, the
linear operator can be represented as:

L^{v}v=-(\frac{1}{2}+\overline{\lambda}+\lambda^{\text{con}})v-\frac{1}{2}P_{\overline{v}}(x\cdot\nabla v)-P_{\overline{v}}\Delta v+P_{\overline{v}}\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}).

We
decompose the linear operator
L
into a coercive operator
L_{1}^{v}

and a compact perturbation
L_{2}^{v}
.

Recall the definitions of
Q_{a},Q_{p},Q
from the decomposition of
L^{U}
. Similarly, we split the linear operator
L^{v}
into two components:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{v}v

\displaystyle:=(-\eta_{1}-\frac{1}{2}-\overline{\lambda}-\lambda^{\text{con}})v-\frac{1}{2}P_{\overline{v}}(x\cdot\nabla v)-P_{\overline{v}}\Delta v+P_{\overline{v}}\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla v+v\cdot\nabla U+v\cdot(Q_{a}+Q_{p})),

\displaystyle L_{2}^{v}v

\displaystyle:=-P_{\overline{v}}\Pi(v\cdot Q).

(4.5)

We choose the relevant Hilbert spaces as follows:

\mathcal{H}^{0}=\{u\in L^{2}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\langle u,\overline{v}\rangle=0\},\quad\mathcal{H}^{1}=\{u\in H^{1}_{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\langle u,\overline{v}\rangle=0\}.

(4.6)

Similarly, we will impose symmetries later for numerical purposes.
We verify the coercivity of the operator
L_{1}^{v}
. Indeed, it holds that

\langle L_{1}^{v}v,v\rangle\geqslant(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-|\lambda^{\text{con}}|)\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-|\langle v\cdot\nabla U,v\rangle|.

By Holder’s inequality with multiple functions and Sobolev embedding in Lemma
6
, where we define the sharp Sobolev constant
K_{4}
,
we have

|\langle v\cdot\nabla U,v\rangle|\leqslant\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|v\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\leqslant 2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}(\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}),

we obtain:

\langle L_{1}^{v}v,v\rangle\geqslant(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-|\lambda^{\text{con}}|)\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}})\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(4.7)

ensuring coercivity for sufficiently small
\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}
and
|\lambda^{\text{con}}|
. Finally, since
Q
is bounded of compact support,
L_{2}^{v}
is a compact operator from

\mathcal{H}^{1}
to
\mathcal{H}^{0}
and bounded from
\mathcal{H}^{0}
to
\mathcal{H}^{0}
. We have verified Assumption
2
: the coercivity of
L_{U}^{1}
and the compactness of
L_{U}^{2}
.

Finally, we
establish the following bound for
\lambda^{\text{lin}}
via an integration by parts:

|\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v)|=|\langle\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla\overline{v}-\Delta\overline{v}-\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla^{T}\widetilde{U},v\rangle|\leqslant M_{0}\|v\|_{L^{2}},

(4.8)

M_{0}:=\|P_{\overline{v}}(\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla\overline{v}-\Delta\overline{v}-\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla^{T}\overline{U})\|_{L^{2}}+\|U\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{\infty},

where we use the orthogonality of
v
to
\overline{v}
and the definition of
P_{\overline{v}}
.

4.2
Finite rank approximation

For a general compact operator in the form of (
4.2
) and (
4.5
) as

L_{2}=-\mathcal{P}(U\cdot Q),

(4.9)

where
Q
is compactly supported and positive definite, and
\mathcal{P}
denotes a projection on
L^{2}
that equals the Leray projection
\Pi
for the case of
U
and
P_{\overline{v}}\Pi
for the case of
v
. Here,
L_{2}^{U}
and
L_{2}^{v}
only differ by the projection operator. Notice that
L
maps the image of the projection operator to itself: for any
f=\mathcal{P}f
, we have
\mathcal{P}Lf=Lf
.

Recall that we want the finite-rank operator to have a number of bases as small as possible so that numerical verification of invertibility on the space can be easier.
Ideally, we should choose the eigenspace corresponding to the largest eigenvalues of
L_{2}
so that the residue of the finite-rank approximation is small. This is not tractable in practice, however, since the eigenvectors are not explicit. We instead solve the eigenvalue problem on an explicitly constructed finite-dimensional subspace numerically, and require that
L_{2}
acting outside of the space is small.
To be precise, the construction involves three main steps:

1.

Choose a domain
\Omega
sufficiently large to encompass
the support of
Q
, and construct a finite-dimensional collection of spectral bases on
\Omega
.

2.

Compute the eigenvectors corresponding to the largest eigenvalues and
construct their spanning eigenspace in the finite-dimensional space.

3.

Construct
approximations of
L_{2}
and invert
L
numerically restricted to the eigenspace.

4.2.1
Construction of the finite rank approximation

We first identify a suitable domain
\Omega
that fully encompasses the
support of the matrix function
Q(x)
. Now, we introduce spectral approximations for functions
defined on
\Omega
. Let
V_{N}
denote the finite-dimensional space with the first
N
spectral bases. We remark that the space and thus the eigenpairs associated with
U
and
v
will be different due to their respective parity symmetries. Consider the following
E
-inner product and its induced norm

\langle u,v\rangle_{E}:=\lambda(\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle_{\Omega}+\eta_{2}\langle u,v\rangle_{\Omega}),

where we choose the constant
\eta_{2}
as
0.005
. Notice that by construction of the spectral basis, the
L^{2}
-projection
I_{N}
defined by (
6.5
) in Section
6
onto the space
V_{N}
is also an
H^{1}
-projection, hence an
E
-projection.

To obtain an efficient finite-rank approximation, we consider the following
eigenvalue problem within the space of spectral bases
V_{N}
:

\langle u,Qv\rangle_{\Omega}=\lambda\langle u,v\rangle_{E}.

(4.10)

Specifically, we arrange the eigenvalues as:

\lambda^{Q}_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda^{Q}_{k}>0=\lambda^{Q}_{k+1}=\cdots=\lambda^{Q}_{N},

and denote their corresponding eigenfunctions as
\{\psi^{Q}_{1},\ldots,\psi^{Q}_{N}\}
. The
eigenfunctions are orthonormal in the
E
-inner
product:

\langle\psi^{Q}_{i},\psi^{Q}_{i}\rangle_{E}=1;\quad\langle\psi^{Q}_{i},\psi^{Q}_{j}\rangle_{E}=\langle\psi^{Q}_{i},Q\psi^{Q}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}=0,i\neq j.

We retain only eigenfunctions with index
j\leqslant m
, for a small
m
.
For each
j\leqslant m
, we numerically obtain the eigenpair
(\overline{\lambda}_{j},\overline{\psi}_{j})
, and solve for
\overline{\phi}_{j}
as:

L\overline{\phi}_{j}=\mathcal{P}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}+r_{j},\quad\mathcal{P}\overline{\phi}_{j}=\overline{\phi}_{j},

(4.11)

where
r_{j}
denotes a small residual term that satisfies
\mathcal{P}r_{j}=r_{j}
by definition. As in Section
3.2.1
, to ensure an exact inversion of
L
, we define the final finite-rank approximation as:

L_{\operatorname{ap}}f:=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}\mathcal{P}(Q\overline{\psi}_{j}+r_{j}),

(4.12)

with
Gf:=-L^{-1}L_{\operatorname{ap}}
given by

Gf=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}\overline{\phi}_{j}.

4.2.2
Estimates of the finite rank approximation

Since our approximate eigensystem incurs numerical errors, we first present a lemma concerning projection onto the eigensystem, whose proof is deferred to Appendix
B
. We denote the eigenvalues
\overline{\Lambda}=\operatorname{diag}\{\overline{\lambda}_{1},\cdots,\overline{\lambda}_{m}\}
, and the space
V_{\operatorname{lg}}=\operatorname{span}\{\overline{\psi}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
. The Gram matrices

G_{E}:=(\langle\overline{\psi}_{i},\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{E})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m},\quad G_{Q}:=(\langle\overline{\psi}_{i},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m},

are close to the identity matrix
\mathbb{1}_{m}
and
\overline{\Lambda}
respectively.

Lemma 1

(Quasi-orthonormal projection)

For any
f
with the orthogonal projection
f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\in V_{\operatorname{lg}}
such that
\langle f-f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}=0
, we have the estimates

|\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}|\leqslant\varepsilon_{3}\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|^{2}_{{L^{2}}(\Omega)}.

(4.13)

Here we use the notation that

\varepsilon_{3}:=\|\mathbb{1}_{m}-(G_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\Lambda}^{-1}(G_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|,

(4.14)

measuring how close the approximate eigenpairs are to being

exactly
orthonormal in the
Q
-geometry.

We now present a lemma on quasi-orthogonality in
E
-inner product; see its proof in Appendix
B
.

Lemma 2

(Quasi-orthogonality in
Q
- and
E
- inner products)

Denote the residues of the approximate eigenvector problem as
R^{e}:=\{r^{e}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
defined via

\langle\overline{\psi}_{j},Qv\rangle_{\Omega}=\overline{\lambda}_{j}\langle\overline{\psi}_{j},v\rangle_{E}+\langle r^{e}_{j},v\rangle_{E},\quad\forall v\in V_{N}.

For any
f\in V_{\operatorname{lg}}
and
u
orthogonal to
V_{\operatorname{lg}}
in the
Q
-inner product as
\langle\overline{\psi}_{j},Qu\rangle_{\Omega}=0
, we have

|\langle f,u\rangle_{E}|\leqslant\varepsilon_{4}\|f\|_{E}\|u\|_{E}.

Here we use the notation that

\varepsilon_{4}:=|(G_{E})^{\frac{-1}{2}}\overline{\Lambda}^{{-1}}(\|r^{e}_{1}\|_{E},\cdots,\|r^{e}_{m}\|_{E})^{T}|,

where

\varepsilon_{4}
is the quasi-orthogonality constant between the “large” subspace

V_{\mathrm{lg}}
and its
Q
–orthogonal complement,

measured in the
E
–inner product
.

Now we work on bounded estimates.
The approximation rate associated with
V_{N}
is defined as

K_{5}:={\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}}\sup_{u\neq 0,u\bot_{L^{2}}V_{N}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}}.

K_{5}
tends to
0
as
N
increases by our spectral basis construction of
V_{N}
, since the Rayleigh quotients are upper bounded by the inverse of eigenvalues of the Laplacian.
Defining the
L^{2}
-projection on
V_{N}
as
I_{N}
, we have the estimate

\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}(u-I_{N}u)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant K_{5}\|(u-I_{N}u)\|_{{E}}^{2}.

(4.15)

We define the following constant

K_{6}:=\sup_{u\in V_{N},u\neq 0,u\bot_{Q}V_{\text{lg}}}\frac{\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}},

(4.16)

the maximal Rayleigh quotient of
Q
measured in the
E
–energy on the
Q
–orthogonal complement of the captured subspace
V_{\mathrm{lg}}=\mathrm{span}\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{m}\}\subset V_{N}
,
i.e. the top generalized eigenvalue of
Q
on
V_{\mathrm{lg}}^{\perp_{Q}}
.
Moreover,

K_{6}
can be computed rigorously in the matrix equivalent characterization of

K_{6}\leqslant\sup_{u\in V_{N},u\neq 0}\frac{\langle u,Qu\rangle_{\Omega}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle u,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}},

which should approximately be the eigenvalue smaller than
\overline{\lambda}_{m}
. With the bounded estimates in hand, we are in a position to present
the following lemma on a rigorous estimate of the approximation error
associated with
L_{\operatorname{ap}}
.

Lemma 3

(Finite-rank approximation)

For
L_{\operatorname{ap}}
defined by (
4.12
)
and
L_{2}
defined by (
4.9
),
we have the estimate for any
f
such that
\mathcal{P}f=f
:

|\langle(L_{2}-L_{\operatorname{ap}})f,f\rangle|\leqslant M_{1}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+M_{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2},

where

M_{1}:=K_{5}+\frac{K_{6}}{(1-\varepsilon_{4})^{2}},\quad M_{2}:=\eta_{2}M_{1}+\varepsilon_{3}\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}+K_{7}.

Here, the constant
K_{7}
is related to the inner product of the Gram matrices

K_{7}:=\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(G_{Q^{2}}G_{r})},\quad G_{Q^{2}}:=(\langle\overline{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{i},\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m},\quad G_{r}:=(\langle r_{i},r_{j}\rangle_{\Omega})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}.

(4.17)

Finally, we remark that by construction,
\overline{\phi}_{j}\in H^{2}
.

We defer the proof of the lemma to Appendix
B
. Here,
K_{7}
is related to the cross term resulting from the residue of the finite-rank approximation.

4.3
Proof of Proposition
1

We begin by addressing Proposition
1
. We construct a finite-rank
approximation
L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U}
of
L_{2}^{U}
by the strategy in Section
4.2
as

L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U}f:=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}^{U}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}\Pi(Q\overline{\psi}^{U}_{j}+r_{j}^{U}).

(4.18)

By Lemma
3
and the coercivity of
L_{1}^{U}
in (
4.3
), we have the linear estimate:

\langle L_{1}^{U}U_{1}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1},U_{1}\rangle\geqslant(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})\|U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-M_{1}^{U})\|\nabla U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.19)

This verifies (
3.20
). We will subsequently choose the parameters
appropriately to ensure that both coefficients on the right-hand side are
strictly positive.

This approximation allows us to split the solution
U=U_{1}+U_{2}
and
rewrite equation (
1.15
) as:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{U}U_{1}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1}=-N^{U}(U_{1}+U_{2})-\Pi\mathrm{E}^{U},

(4.20)

\displaystyle L^{U}U_{2}=-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U}U_{1}.

(4.21)

Finally, we define:

U_{2}=G^{U}U_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle U_{1},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U},

(4.22)

which gives an exact solution to (
4.21
).
By construction,
\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}
and thus
U_{2}
are divergence-free.
We detail our fixed-point approach for (
4.20
) as follows.

Definition of the fixed point map.

Typically, the fixed-point map

T^{U}
is defined as:

L_{1}^{U}U_{1}^{\ast}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1}^{\ast}=-\Pi((U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1}))-\Pi\mathrm{E}^{U}.

where
U_{1}^{\ast}:=T^{U}(U_{1})
. To simplify nonlinear estimates, we replace
\nabla U_{1}
by
\nabla U_{1}^{\ast}
in the nonlinear term. In the weak form, we slightly
modify the map
T^{U}_{R}
to:

\langle L_{1}^{U}U_{1}^{\ast}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1}^{\ast}+(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla U_{1}^{\ast},g\rangle=\langle-(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}-\mathrm{E}^{U},g\rangle,

(4.23)

for all
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
. Here we denote
U_{1}^{\ast}:=T^{U}_{R}(U_{1})
, and
work in the space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
consisting of functions in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
of compact support in the open ball
B_{R}(0)
. Recall that
\mathcal{H}^{1}
consists of divergence-free functions, so we can remove the Leray projection. We require
R
sufficiently large so that
\Omega\subset B_{R}(0)
in the finite rank approximation.

Well-definedness of the fixed point map.

Integration by parts yields:

\langle(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla g,g\rangle=0,

(4.24)

and thus we know by (
4.19
) that

\langle L_{1}^{U}f+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})f+(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla f,g\rangle

(4.25)

induces a coercive bilinear form for
(f,g)
on
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
.

By definition of the finite rank approximation in (
4.18
), we compute using the boundedness of
Q
,
\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}
and
r_{j}^{U}
that
\|L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U}f\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

Using Sobolev embedding, along with the forms (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), we deduce that (
4.25
) induces a bounded bilinear form for
(f,g)
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
.

By definition (
4.22
) and Lemma
3
,
G^{U}U_{1}\in H^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}
, and by Sobolev embedding we have

-(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}-\mathrm{E}^{U}\in L^{2}\,.

As a consequence of Lax-Milgram applied to the bilinear form (
4.25
), we know that there exists
U_{1}^{\ast}=T_{R}^{U}(U_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
such that (
4.23
) holds.

A priori estimate in
H^{1}
.

By (
4.23
), (
4.19
), and (
4.24
), we obtain:

(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})\|U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-M_{1}^{U})\|\nabla U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant-\langle(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}+\mathrm{E}^{U},U_{1}^{\ast}\rangle.

(4.26)

The residual term is bounded by:

|\langle\mathrm{E}^{U},U_{1}^{\ast}\rangle|\leqslant\|\mathrm{E}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}\|U^{\ast}_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon^{U}\|U^{\ast}_{1}\|_{L^{2}},

with
\varepsilon^{U}
sufficiently small defined in Assumption
1
.

To handle the nonlinear term, we establish the following bound:

|\langle(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1},U_{1}^{\ast}\rangle|\leqslant\|(U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1})\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\|U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{3}^{U}\|U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}},

(4.27)

where we recall the definition of
G^{U}_{Q^{2}}
in (
4.17
) and define
M^{U}_{3}

M^{U}_{3}:=\sqrt{\|G^{U}_{Q^{2}}\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}+\|G^{U}_{Q^{2}}\|\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{i}^{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

Proof of (
4.27
).

Now we estimate the LHS by the definition in (
4.22
) as

\displaystyle\|U_{1}\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}+G^{U}U_{1}\cdot\nabla G^{U}U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle U_{1},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}U_{1}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle U_{1},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}\overline{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}\langle U_{1},Q\overline{\psi}_{i}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}\overline{\phi}_{i}^{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}=:I_{4}+I_{5}

The absolute value of the first term is bounded by Cauchy-Schwarz as

|I_{4}|\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle U_{1},\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}\|U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}.

The second term is similarly bounded by

|I_{5}|\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle U_{1},\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\langle U_{1},\overline{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{i}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{i}^{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

Relating the projection to the Gram matrix as in (
B.1
), we conclude the proof of (
4.27
).
∎

We collect the estimates in
H^{1}
by (
4.26
):

(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})\|U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-M_{1}^{U})\|\nabla U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant(\varepsilon^{U}+M_{3}^{U}\|U_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2})\|U_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}.

We analyze the bounded mapping with the following assumption.

Assumption 6

(Bounded mapping assumption for
U
)

We assume

M_{4}^{U}:=(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})^{2}-4M_{3}^{U}\varepsilon^{U}>0.

With Assumption
6
, we define

x_{0}^{U}:=\frac{2\varepsilon^{U}}{\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U}+\sqrt{M_{4}^{U}}},\quad\text{ so that}\quad\varepsilon^{U}+M_{3}^{U}(x_{0}^{U})^{2}=(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})x_{0}^{U}.

We can solve the
H^{1}
estimates to see
T_{R}^{U}
maps
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{U},x_{1}^{U}}
to itself, where

B^{R}_{x_{0},x_{1}}:=\{U\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}:\|U\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant x_{0},\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant x_{1}\},\quad x_{1}^{U}:=\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U}}{1-M_{1}^{U}}}\frac{x_{0}^{U}}{2}.

We will verify Assumption
6
in Subsection
4.5
.

Continuity estimate in
L^{4}
.

We estimate the difference between
U_{1}^{\ast}:=T_{R}^{U}(U_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
and
\widehat{U}_{1}^{\ast}:=T_{R}^{U}(\widehat{U}_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
. By (
4.23
), we take differences and plug in
g=U_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{U}_{1}^{\ast}
, to get

\langle L_{1}^{U}\delta U^{\ast}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})\delta U^{\ast},\delta U^{\ast}\rangle=-\langle(\widehat{U}_{1}+G^{U}\widehat{U}_{1})\cdot\nabla(G^{U}\delta U)+(\delta U+G^{U}\delta U)\cdot\nabla(U_{1}^{\ast}+G^{U}U_{1}),\delta U^{\ast}\rangle,

(4.28)

where we denote
\delta U^{\ast}:=U_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{U}_{1}^{\ast}
,
\delta U:=U_{1}-\widehat{U}_{1}
, and use the cancellation (
4.24
).

Notice that for the continuity estimate, we do not require sharp constants, but want to gain some regularity to the extent possible. We first provide some estimates for
G^{U}f
by
\|f\|
. Recall the definition (
4.22
) and notice that
\overline{\phi}^{U}_{j}\in H^{2}
by Lemma
3
.
We conclude that

\|\nabla^{i}G^{U}f\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{L^{2}},

(4.29)

where
i=0,1
and
C
is a constant depending on
\overline{\phi}^{U}_{j},\overline{\psi}^{U}_{j}
.

We estimate the difference of the nonlinear term as follows:

\displaystyle|\langle(\widehat{U}_{1}+G^{U}\widehat{U}_{1})\cdot\nabla(G^{U}\delta U)+(\delta U+G^{U}\delta U)\cdot\nabla(U_{1}^{\ast}+G^{U}U_{1}),\delta U^{\ast}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leqslant C(\|\widehat{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{U}_{1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}})\|\delta U\|_{L^{4}}\|\delta U^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}},

(4.30)

where
C
is some constant only depending on
\overline{\phi}^{U}_{j},\overline{\psi}^{U}_{j},R
.

Proof of (
4.3
).

Combining the boundedness of
G^{U}
in (
4.29
) with Holder’s inequality with multiple functions and Sobolev embedding, we bound the LHS by

\displaystyle\leqslant(\|\widehat{U}_{1}+G^{U}\widehat{U}_{1}\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla(G^{U}\delta U)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta U+G^{U}\delta U\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla(U_{1}^{\ast}+G^{U}U_{1})\|_{L^{2}})\|\delta U^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|\widehat{U}_{1}+G^{U}\widehat{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}}\|\delta U\|_{L^{2}}+(\|\delta U\|_{L^{4}}+\|G^{U}\delta U\|_{H^{1}})(\|{U}_{1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}}))\|\delta U^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|\widehat{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{U}_{1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{1}}+\|{U}_{1}\|_{H^{1}})\|\delta U\|_{L^{4}}\|\delta U^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}},

where in the last inequality, we use the fact that
\delta U
is compactly supported.
∎

Combined with (
4.28
), the coercivity (
4.19
), Sobolev embedding of
H^{1}
into
L^{4}
with
\|f\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{1}}
, and the previous step on the a priori bound of
H^{1}
norm, we conclude that

\|\delta U^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\|\delta U\|_{L^{4}}.

Thus
T^{U}_{R}
is a continuous mapping in
L^{4}
, in the space
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{U},x_{1}^{U}}
.

Notice that
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{U},x_{1}^{U}}
is bounded in
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
, and by Rellich–Kondrachov embedding theorem we know that it is a compact subset of
L^{4}
.
We apply Schauder fixed-point theorem to the topology induced by
L^{4}
. Since
T^{U}_{R}
is continuous, maps the compact subset
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{U},x_{1}^{U}}
to itself, there exists a fixed point
U_{1}^{R}
such that by (
4.23
), for any
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
, we have

\langle L_{1}^{U}U_{1}^{R}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1}^{R},g\rangle=\langle-(U_{1}^{R}+G^{U}U_{1}^{R})\cdot\nabla(U_{1}^{R}+G^{U}U_{1}^{R})-\mathrm{E}^{U},g\rangle,

(4.31)

Taking weak limits.

Notice that the divergence-free condition is preserved in the weak limit, and
U_{1}^{R}
has uniform bounds in
H^{1}
by definition of
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{U},x_{1}^{U}}
. We can thus take weak limits for a subsequence of
U_{1}^{R}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
for
R\to\infty
. Denote
U_{1}^{\infty}
as the weak limit.

Now fix any test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
that is divergence free, for sufficiently large
R
,
B_{R}(0)
contains the support of
\varphi
. We compute by taking limits of (
4.31
) to get

\langle L_{1}^{U}U_{1}^{\infty}+(L_{2}^{U}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{U})U_{1}^{\infty},\varphi\rangle=\langle-(U_{1}^{\infty}+G^{U}U_{1}^{\infty})\cdot\nabla(U_{1}^{\infty}+G^{U}U_{1}^{\infty})-\mathrm{E}^{U},\varphi\rangle,

where we use an integration by parts for the diffusion term on the left-hand side.
For the convergence of the right-hand side, we use the fact that in
L^{2}
,
\nabla^{i}G^{U}U_{1}^{R}\to\nabla^{i}G^{U}U_{1}^{\infty}
by definition (
4.22
),
i=0,1
, and
(U_{1}^{R}+G^{U}U_{1}^{R})\varphi\to(U_{1}^{\infty}+G^{U}U_{1}^{\infty})\varphi
due to the compact support of
\varphi
upon taking a subsequence by Rellich–Kondrachov again, and
\langle f_{R},g_{R}\rangle\to\langle f_{\infty},g_{\infty}\rangle
for
f_{R}\to f_{\infty}
and
g_{R}\rightharpoonup g_{\infty}
.
Since the test function
\varphi
is arbitrary, we conclude that (
4.20
) holds for our
U_{1}^{\infty}
and we conclude Proposition
1
.

4.4
Proof of Proposition
2

We work on Proposition
2
following a similar argument to the previous subsection assuming that Proposition
1
holds, and we only highlight the key changes. We construct the finite-rank
approximation operator
L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v}
of
L_{2}^{v}
as:

L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v}f:=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{v}\rangle_{\Omega}P_{\overline{v}}\Pi(Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{v}+r_{j}^{v}).

Similarly, Lemma
3
and the coercivity of
L_{1}^{v}
in (
4.7
) indicate the estimate:

\displaystyle\langle L_{1}^{v}v_{1}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})v_{1},v_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-|\lambda^{\text{con}}|-M_{2}^{v})\|v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-M_{1}^{v})\|\nabla v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.32)

We choose parameters to ensure that both coefficients on the right-hand side are
strictly positive.

Consequently, equation (
1.17
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{v}v_{1}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})v_{1}=-N^{v}(v_{1}+v_{2})-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v},

\displaystyle L^{v}v_{2}=-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v}v_{1}.

Finally, we define
v_{2}
as follows:

v_{2}=G^{v}v_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle v_{1},Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{v}\rangle_{\Omega}\overline{\phi}_{j}^{v}.

(4.33)

Well-definedness of the fixed point map.

Define
T^{v}_{R}
via the weak form:

\langle L_{1}^{v}v^{\ast}_{1}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})v^{\ast}_{1},g\rangle=\langle-N^{v}(v_{1}+G^{v}v_{1})-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v},g\rangle,

(4.34)

for all
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
. We know by (
4.32
) that the left-hand side induces a coercive bilinear form on
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
. As in the case for
U
, we know it is a bounded bilinear form.
N^{v}(v_{1}+G^{v}v_{1})-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v}\in L^{2}
trivially and by Lax-Milgram, we know that there exists
v_{1}^{\ast}:=T_{R}^{v}(v_{1})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
such that (
4.34
) holds.

A priori estimate in
H^{1}
.

We have the following nonlinear estimate by (
4.8
), (
4.33
), Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and relating the projection to the Gram matrix as in the proof of (
4.27
):

|\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v_{1}+G^{v}v_{1})(v_{1}+G^{v}v_{1}),v_{1}^{\ast}\rangle|\leqslant M_{0}(\|v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|G^{v}v_{1}\|_{L^{2}})^{2}\|v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{3}^{v}\|v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}},

where we define

M_{3}^{v}:=M_{0}(1+\sqrt{\|G^{v}_{Q^{2}}\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{j}^{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}})^{2}.

The residual term
\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v}
satisfies trivially:

|\langle\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v},v_{1}^{\ast}\rangle|\leqslant(\varepsilon^{v}+\|U\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}})\|v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}.

We thus collect the following
H^{1}
estimate by (
4.34
) and (
4.32
):

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-|\lambda^{\text{con}}|-M_{2}^{v})\|v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-M_{1}^{v})\|\nabla v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\varepsilon^{v}+\|U\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+M_{3}^{v}\|v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}^{2})\|v_{1}^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}.

As in the case of
U
, we prepare the following assumption.

Assumption 7

(Bounded mapping assumption for
v
)

Recall from Proposition
1
that

\|U\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant x_{0}^{U},\quad\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant x_{1}^{U}.

We assume

M_{4}^{v}:=(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}x_{1}^{U}-|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|-M_{2}^{v})^{2}-4M_{3}^{v}(\varepsilon^{v}+x_{0}^{U}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+x_{1}^{U}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}})>0.

We see that the map
T_{R}^{v}
maps the ball
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{v},x_{1}^{v}}
to itself, where

x_{0}^{v}:=\frac{2(\varepsilon^{v}+x_{0}^{U}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+x_{1}^{U}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}})}{\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}x_{1}^{U}-|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|-M_{2}^{v}+\sqrt{M_{4}^{v}}},

x_{1}^{v}:=\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}x_{1}^{U}-|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|-M_{2}^{v}}{1-2K_{4}x_{1}^{U}-M_{1}^{v}}}\frac{x_{0}^{v}}{2}.

We will verify Assumption
7
in Subsection
4.5
.

Continuity estimate in
L^{4}
.

For
v_{1}^{\ast}:=T_{R}^{v}(v_{1}),\widehat{v}_{1}^{\ast}:=T_{R}^{v}(\widehat{v}_{1})
, we denote
\delta v^{\ast}:=v_{1}^{\ast}-\widehat{v}_{1}^{\ast}
,
\delta v:=v_{1}-\widehat{v}_{1}
. By (
4.34
), we have

\langle L_{1}^{v}\delta v^{\ast}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})\delta v^{\ast},\delta v^{\ast}\rangle=-\langle\lambda^{\text{lin}}(\widehat{v}_{1}+G^{v}\widehat{v}_{1})(\delta v+G^{v}\delta v)+\lambda^{\text{lin}}(\delta v+G^{v}\delta v)(v_{1}+G^{v}v_{1}),\delta v^{\ast}\rangle,

(4.35)

Recalling (
4.33
) and noticing that
\overline{\phi}^{v}_{j}\in H^{2}
, we conclude similarly
\|G^{v}f\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\|f\|_{L^{2}}
.
Using (
4.8
) to estimate the right-hand side of (
4.35
), we get the upper bound

M_{0}(C+1)^{2}(\|v_{1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\widehat{v}_{1}\|_{L^{2}})\|\delta v\|_{L^{2}}\|\delta v^{\ast}\|_{L^{2}}.

By (
4.35
), (
4.32
), Sobolev embedding of
H^{1}
into
L^{4}
into
L^{2}
, and the a priori bound of
H^{1}
norm, we conclude that

\|\delta v^{\ast}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\|\delta v\|_{L^{4}}.

Thus
T^{v}_{R}
is a continuous mapping in
L^{4}
in the space
B^{R}_{x_{0}^{v},x_{1}^{v}}
. By the Schauder fixed-point theorem, there exists a fixed point
v_{1}^{R}
such that by (
4.34
), for any
g\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{R}
, we have

\langle L_{1}^{v}v_{1}^{R}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})v_{1}^{R},g\rangle=\langle-N^{v}(v_{1}^{R}+G^{v}v_{1}^{R})-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v},g\rangle,

Taking weak limits.

Notice that the divergence-free condition and orthogonality condition are preserved in the weak limit. Denote
v_{1}^{\infty}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
as the weak limit for a subsequence of
v_{1}^{R}
. For any test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}
, we have

\langle L_{1}^{v}v_{1}^{\infty}+(L_{2}^{v}-L_{\operatorname{ap}}^{v})v_{1}^{\infty},\varphi\rangle=\langle-N^{v}(v_{1}^{\infty}+G^{v}v_{1}^{\infty})-\widehat{\mathrm{E}}^{v},\varphi\rangle,

where the only change is to show
\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v_{1}^{R}+G^{v}v_{1}^{R})\to\lambda^{\text{lin}}(v_{1}^{\infty}+G^{v}v_{1}^{\infty})
, this is true by definition of the linear operator
\lambda^{\text{lin}}
in (
4.8
). Since the test function
\varphi
is arbitrary, we conclude Proposition
2
.

4.5
Numerical results

Although comprehensive numerical details are presented in subsequent sections,
we summarize the key results here for convenience. We will conclude the
propositions by verifying Assumption
6
and
7
. We remark that our verifications are carried effectively in a subspace of

H^{1}_{\sigma}
, leveraging axissymmetry and parities, to reduce the number of
bases in our finite-rank approximation.

4.5.1
Verification of Proposition
1

To verify Proposition
1
, we need to check that

M_{4}^{U}=(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U})^{2}-4M_{3}^{U}\varepsilon^{U}>0,

where the residual satisfies

\varepsilon^{U}\leqslant 7\times 10^{-7}.

For
\eta_{1}
, we take
\eta_{1}=0.191
and verify that

\frac{\nabla\overline{U}+\nabla^{T}\overline{U}}{2}+\eta_{1}I+Q\succeq 0.

Then
M_{4}^{U}
reduces to

M_{4}^{U}\geqslant(0.059-M_{2}^{U})^{2}-2.8\times 10^{-6}\times M_{3}^{U}.

We still need to estimate
M_{2}^{U}
and
M_{3}^{U}
.

Step 1: Estimate of
M_{2}^{U}
.

M_{2}^{U}
is defined as

M_{2}^{U}=\eta_{2}M_{1}^{U}+\varepsilon^{U}_{3}\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}+K_{7}^{U},

where
\eta_{2}=0.005
is the
L^{2}
coefficient of
E
-norm in (
4.10
).
The term
M_{1}
is given by

M_{1}^{U}=K^{U}_{5}+\frac{K_{6}^{U}}{(1-\varepsilon^{U}_{4})^{2}},\quad K^{U}_{5}=\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\sup_{u\neq 0,u\bot_{L^{2}}V^{U}_{N}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}},\quad

where

K_{6}^{U}=\sup_{u\in V_{N}^{U},u\neq 0}\frac{\langle u,Qu\rangle_{\Omega}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle u,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{U}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}}\leqslant 0.46.

We choose
N=1257
,
m=19
and get:

\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{\infty}\leqslant 4.5,\quad\sup_{u\neq 0,u\bot_{L^{2}}V^{U}_{N}}\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}}\leqslant 0.1024.

It follows that

K^{U}_{5}\leqslant 0.47.

\varepsilon^{U}_{3}
and
\varepsilon^{U}_{4}
arise from machine precision and are
bounded by

\varepsilon^{U}_{3},\varepsilon^{U}_{4}\leqslant 10^{-10}.

Therefore, we get

M_{1}^{U}\leqslant 0.47+\frac{0.46}{(1-10^{-10})^{2}}\leqslant 0.9301.

The constant
K_{7}^{U}
defined by

K_{7}^{U}=\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(G_{Q^{2}}G_{r})}\leqslant 0.0002.

Therefore,

M_{2}^{U}\leqslant 0.005\times 0.9301+10^{-10}\times 4.5+0.0002\leqslant 0.005.

Step 2: Estimate of
M_{3}^{U}
and
M_{4}^{U}
.

M_{3}^{U}
is defined by

M_{3}^{U}=\sqrt{\|G^{U}_{Q^{2}}\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}+\|G^{U}_{Q^{2}}\|\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{i}^{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}.

Numerical results show that

\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}\leqslant 0.9,\quad\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{i}^{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}^{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\leqslant 0.11,\quad\|G^{U}_{Q^{2}}\|\leqslant 0.025,

which lead to,

M_{3}^{U}\leqslant\sqrt{0.025}\times 0.9+0.025\times 0.11\leqslant 0.16.

Therefore,
M_{4}^{U}
has the following lower bound:

M_{4}^{U}\geqslant(\frac{1}{4}-0.191-0.005)^{2}-4\times 0.16\times 7\times 10^{-7}\geqslant 0.0029.

Step 3: Estimate of
x_{0}^{U}
and
x_{1}^{U}
.

Finally, we conclude that

x_{0}^{U}=\frac{2\varepsilon^{U}}{\left(\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U}+\sqrt{M_{4}^{U}}\right)}\leqslant\frac{2\times 7\times 10^{-7}}{0.25-0.191-0.005+\sqrt{0.0029}}\leqslant 1.3\times 10^{-5},

and

x_{1}^{U}=\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-M_{2}^{U}}{1-M_{1}^{U}}}\frac{x_{0}^{U}}{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{4}-0.191}{1-0.9301}}\times\frac{1.3\times 10^{-5}}{2}\leqslant 6\times 10^{-6}.

This finishes the proof of Proposition
1
.

4.5.2
Verification of Proposition
2

For Proposition
2
, similarly, we need to verify

M_{4}^{v}=(M_{5}^{v})^{2}-4M_{3}^{v}\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}>0,

where we denote

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{v}+\|U\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}},

\displaystyle M_{5}^{v}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}-\eta_{1}-\overline{\lambda}-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|-M_{2}^{v}.

The eigenvalue
\overline{\lambda}
is

\overline{\lambda}\approx-0.11314203274385938\leqslant-0.113.

Step 1: Estimate of
\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}
and
|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|
.

The residual satisfies

\varepsilon^{v}\leqslant 1.8\times 10^{-5}.

The
L^{\infty}
norm of
\overline{v}
and
\nabla\overline{v}
are:

\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 3,\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 2.3.

Therefore,
|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|
and
\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}
are
bounded by

\displaystyle|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 6\times 10^{-6}\times 2.3\leqslant 1.4\times 10^{-5},

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 1.8\times 10^{-5}+1.3\times 10^{-5}\times 3+6\times 10^{-6}\times 2.3\leqslant 7.1\times 10^{-5}.

Step 2: Estimate of
M_{5}^{v}
.

K_{4}\leqslant 0.091
is introduced in Lemma
6
. We can
simplify
M_{5}^{v}
into

M_{5}^{v}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}-0.191+0.113-2\times 0.091\times 6\times 10^{-6}-1.4\times 10^{-5}-M_{2}^{v}\geqslant 0.17-M_{2}^{v}.

M_{2}^{v}
is defined by

M_{2}^{v}=\eta_{2}M_{1}^{v}+\varepsilon_{3}^{v}\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}+K_{7}^{v}.

We choose
N=1260,m=24
and get:

\displaystyle M_{1}^{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K^{v}_{5}+\frac{K_{6}^{v}}{(1-\varepsilon^{v}_{4})^{2}},

\displaystyle K_{7}^{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(G_{Q^{2}}^{v}G_{r}^{v})}\leqslant 0.00027,

\displaystyle K_{6}^{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in V_{N}^{v},u\neq 0}\frac{\langle u,Qu\rangle_{\Omega}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle u,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}^{v}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}}{\|u\|^{2}_{E}}\leqslant 0.4532.

Therefore,
M_{1}^{v}
,
M_{2}^{v}
are bounded by

\displaystyle M_{1}^{v}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0.47+\frac{0.4532}{(1-10^{-10})^{2}}\leqslant 0.924,

\displaystyle M_{2}^{v}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0.005\times 0.924+4.5\times 10^{-10}+0.00027\leqslant 0.0049.

M_{5}^{v}
thus has the following lower bound:

M_{5}^{v}\geqslant 0.17-0.0049=0.1651.

Step 3: Estimate of
M_{3}^{v}
and
M_{4}^{v}
.

M_{3}^{v}
is defined by

M_{3}^{v}=M_{0}(1+\sqrt{\|G_{Q^{2}}^{v}\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{j}^{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}})^{2},

where

M_{0}=\|P_{\overline{v}}(\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla\overline{v}-\Delta\overline{v}-\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla^{T}\overline{U})\|_{L^{2}}+\|U\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}\|\overline{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

We collect the numerical computations:

\|P_{\overline{v}}(\frac{1}{2}x\cdot\nabla\overline{v}-\Delta\overline{v}-\overline{U}\cdot\nabla\overline{v}+\overline{v}\cdot\nabla^{T}\overline{U})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 6.3581,\quad\sqrt{\|G_{Q^{2}}^{v}\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|\overline{\phi}_{j}^{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\leqslant 0.03,

which yield the following approximate values:

\displaystyle M_{0}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 6.3581+1.3\times 10^{-5}\times 3+6\times 10^{-6}\times 2.3\leqslant 6.36

\displaystyle M_{3}^{v}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 6.36\times(1+0.03)^{2}\leqslant 7,

\displaystyle M_{4}^{v}

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle 0.1651^{2}-4\times 7\times 7.1\times 10^{-5}\geqslant 0.025.

Step 4: Estimate of
\|v\|
and
|\lambda|
.

We collect all estimates above and obtain:

\displaystyle x_{0}^{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\varepsilon^{v,\operatorname{tot}}}{M_{5}^{v}+\sqrt{M_{4}^{v}}}\leqslant\frac{2\times 7.1\times 10^{-5}}{0.1651+\sqrt{0.025}}\leqslant 4.4\times 10^{-4},

\displaystyle x_{1}^{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{M_{5}^{v}}{1-2K_{4}\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}}-M_{1}^{v}}}\frac{x_{0}^{v}}{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{0.1651}{1-2\times 0.091\times 6\times 10^{-6}-0.924}}\times\frac{4.4\times 10^{-4}}{2}\leqslant 3.3\times 10^{-4}.

Hence, we have verified the existence of
v
. The estimate of

\lambda^{\operatorname{lin}}(v)
follows:

|\lambda^{\operatorname{lin}}(v)|\leqslant M_{0}\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant M_{0}(1+\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|G_{Q^{2}}^{v}\|\|\overline{\phi}_{j}^{v}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}})x_{0}^{v}\leqslant 6.36\times(1+0.03)\times 4.4\times 10^{-4}\leqslant 0.00293.

Finally, we confirm that:

|\lambda|\leqslant|\lambda^{\operatorname{lin}}(v)|+|\lambda^{\operatorname{con}}|\leqslant 0.003\ll|\overline{\lambda}|\approx 0.113.

This concludes the numerical verification and thereby completes the proof of
Proposition
2
.

5
Numerical Construction of Approximate Solutions

In this section, we detail the numerical procedures employed to solve the
equation (
1.12
). In prior research
[
25
]
, boundary conditions of the
form:

A_{\alpha}(\theta)=\alpha(0,0,e^{-4\cot^{2}\theta}),

were used, where
\alpha
is a parameter. As
\alpha
increased, the
eigenvalue associated with the linear operator
\mathcal{L}(\overline{U})

crossed the imaginary axis at approximately
\alpha\approx 292
. However, the finite-rank approximation in Section
4.2
for solutions with such large values of boundary conditions will be inefficient, rendering the numerical verification challenging. To overcome this, we introduce an alternative
numerical strategy aimed at obtaining a smaller solution associated with a
negative eigenvalue.
Instead of tuning
\alpha
and tracking eigenvalue crossings, we optimize the field
\overline{U}
directly: we compute
\nabla\lambda(\overline{U})
and run a projected gradient descent to obtain a small solution with a negative eigenvalue. Then, we interpolate the approximate solution to
enforce exact divergence-free conditions.

This section is structured as follows. In Section
5.1
, we derive the
gradient descent method for the eigenvalue
\lambda(\widetilde{U})
. Section

5.2
simplifies the problem by leveraging symmetries (axisymmetry in spherical coordinates). In Section

5.3
, we discretize the equations using the finite element method.
Section
5.4
details the computation of the eigenvalue. The first four subsections produce a finite-element candidate; we then refine it to a high-precision candidate using a spectral basis, which is divergence-free and smooth away from the origin. In Section

5.5
, we extend the obtained solutions to the entire space. Section

5.6
presents the construction of a divergence-free basis. In
Section
5.7
, we interpolate our numerical solutions onto this
basis and reduce residuals. Finally, Section
5.8
summarizes our
numerical approach and provides pseudo-code for the implemented algorithm.

5.1
Gradient descent for eigenvalue

To compute the desired eigenvalue, we adopt a gradient descent approach. The
iterative update from
\bar{U}_{n}
to
\bar{U}_{n+1}
is defined as follows.
Recall that
\lambda(\tilde{U})
, as a functional of
\tilde{U}
, is the
eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{U})
with the smallest real part. First, we
calculate the Fréchet derivative of
\lambda(\tilde{U})
. Throughout, we
assume that
\lambda(\tilde{U})
is simple (thus real) and isolated, which
guarantees that it is also a simple and isolated eigenvalue for
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{U})^{\ast}
. Let
v^{l}(\tilde{U}),v^{r}(\tilde{U})
denote the left
and right eigenvector associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda(\tilde{U})
,
respectively satisfying:

\mathcal{L}(\tilde{U})v^{r}(\tilde{U})=\lambda(\tilde{U})v^{r}(\tilde{U}),\quad\mathcal{L}(\tilde{U})^{\ast}v^{l}(\tilde{U})=\lambda(\tilde{U})v^{l}(\tilde{U}).

Denoting
v^{r}_{n}=v^{r}(\bar{U}_{n})
and
v^{l}_{n}=v^{l}(\bar{U}_{n})
respectively,
the gradient of
\lambda(\bar{U}_{n})
is explicitly given by:

\frac{\delta\lambda(\bar{U}_{n})}{\delta\tilde{U}}=\frac{v^{l}_{n}\cdot\nabla^{T}v^{r}_{n}-v^{r}_{n}\cdot\nabla v^{l}_{n}}{\langle v^{l}_{n},v^{r}_{n}\rangle}.

(5.1)

A formal derivation of the above equation is provided in Appendix

C
. This is also known as the non-self-adjoint
Hellmann–Feynman formula:

\partial\lambda=\langle v^{l},(\partial\mathcal{L})v^{r}\rangle/\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle,\quad\partial\mathcal{L}(\delta U):=-(\delta U)\hskip-1.69998pt\cdot\hskip-1.69998pt\nabla\hskip 1.69998pt(\cdot)\hskip 1.69998pt+(\cdot)\hskip-1.69998pt\cdot\hskip-1.69998pt\nabla(\delta U).

Without additional constraints, the increment
g_{n}
would ideally be given by

g_{n}=-k_{n}\frac{\delta\lambda(\bar{U}_{n})}{\delta\tilde{U}},

where
k_{n}
denotes step size. In practice,
k_{n}
can be chosen by different
strategies; we use the Barzilai–Borwein method
[
3
]
for
efficiency.

However, since
\tilde{U}
is constrained to lie on the solution manifold
defined by equation (
1.6
), the increment
g_{n}
must
belong to the tangent space of this manifold at the current iterate

\bar{U}_{n}
:

T_{\bar{U}_{n}}:=\{\hskip 1.69998ptg:\mathrm{div}g=0,\mathcal{L}(\bar{U}_{n})\hskip 1.69998ptg=0\hskip 1.69998pt\}.

Moreover, the boundary condition
A_{n}(\omega)
with
\omega=\frac{x}{|x|}

is updated together with
\bar{U}_{n}
. In addition to the tangent-space
constraint, we impose conditions on
A(\omega)
that governs the far-field
behavior of
\tilde{U}
. The key motivation is to ensure that the most
negative eigenvalue of the symmetric gradient

Q_{s}(x)=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla U(x)+\nabla^{T}U(x)),

decays as rapidly as possible in the far field. Using the ansatz
\bar{U}_{n}\approx\frac{A_{n}(\omega)}{|x|}
, we obtain

Q_{s,n}\approx\frac{Q^{A}_{s,n}(\omega)}{|x|^{2}},

so the radial decay rate is fixed at
|x|^{-2}
, while the prefactor
Q_{s,n}^{A}(\omega)
depends on
A_{n}(\omega)
and controls the size of the most
negative eigenvalue. To control this prefactor and to ensure numerical
stability, we introduce a positive constant
\gamma_{U}>0
and impose two
boundary constraints on
A_{n+1}
:

1.

Regularity bound (to sufficient smoothness of
A(\omega)
):

\|\nabla^{2}A(\omega)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\gamma_{U},

(5.2)

2.

Spectral bound. (to suppress the most negative eigenvalue in the far
field):

\lambda_{\max}(-Q_{s,n}^{A}(\omega))\leqslant\gamma_{U}.

(5.3)

Combining these requirements, we determine the increment by solving the convex
problem:

\min_{g_{n}}\|g_{n}+k_{n}\frac{\delta\lambda(\bar{U}_{n})}{\delta\tilde{U}}\|_{L^{2}},\text{ \ s.t. $g_{n}\in T_{\bar{U}_{n}}$, $A_{n+1}$
satisfies \eqref{eq:bd an} and \eqref{eq:lbd an}.}

This problem can be solved efficiently in MATLAB. With the increment
g_{n}

determined by solving the constrained optimization problem, we obtain the
tentative update

\bar{U}^{\ast}_{n+1}=\bar{U}_{n}+g_{n}.

Since
\bar{U}^{\ast}_{n+1}
is not yet a solution to equation
(
1.6
), we project it onto the solution manifold while
fixing the boundary condition
A_{n+1}(\omega)
, yielding the updated
solution
\bar{U}_{n+1}
. In practice, we discretize the system
(
1.6
) using the finite element method, resulting in a
nonlinear equation for the numerical solution
\bar{U}
. We then fix the
boundary condition
A_{n+1}
and apply Newton’s method with initial guess

\bar{U}^{\ast}_{n+1}
to solve the discretized version of equation
(
1.6
), thereby obtaining
\bar{U}_{n+1}
. A few
Newton steps suffice; we stop once the discrete residual falls below a
prescribed tolerance.

We iterate this procedure until convergence. If the eigenvalue satisfies

\lambda<-0.1
, the iteration terminates. Otherwise, we incrementally
increase
\gamma_{U}
and repeat the above steps.

5.2
Axisymmetry, parity and boundary conditions

We consider the axisymmetric case throughout the computations and denote the cylindrical coordinates as
(\rho,z,\phi)
. We also impose symmetry conditions in
z
: even for the profile problem
(U,P)
and odd for the eigenvalue problem
(v,q)
. In Euclidean coordinates, a vector field
(u_{x},u_{y},u_{z})
is
even in
z
if

u_{x}(x,y,-z)=u_{x}(x,y,z),\quad u_{y}(x,y,-z)=u_{y}(x,y,z),\quad u_{z}(x,y,-z)=-u_{z}(x,y,z).

In other words, the horizontal components are even functions of
z
, while the vertical component is odd. In spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi)
, the reflection
z\mapsto-z
corresponds to
\theta\mapsto\pi-\theta
. The condition of being even in
z
then reads

u_{r}(r,\pi-\theta,\phi)=u_{r}(r,\theta,\phi),\quad u_{\phi}(r,\pi-\theta,\phi)=u_{\phi}(r,\theta,\phi),\quad u_{\theta}(r,\pi-\theta,\phi)=-\,u_{\theta}(r,\theta,\phi).

The definition of
odd in
z
is the opposite.

We note that if
\widetilde{\lambda}
is a simple eigenvalue, since
\widetilde{U}
is even in
z
,
\widetilde{v}
must be either even or odd.
Numerical evidence indicates that eigenvalues associated with odd
eigenvectors decrease more rapidly. Therefore, we assume
\widetilde{v}
is odd
with respect to
z
.

In practice, for the construction of accurate approximate solutions in this section, we adopt spherical coordinates
(r,\theta,\phi)
. Due to the axisymmetry and parity, the computational
domain reduces to
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\theta\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
. Since spherical coordinates
(r,\theta)
correspond to polar representation of cylindrical coordinates
(\rho,z)
, boundary conditions in spherical coordinates are thus given by:

\bullet

At
\theta=0
, which corresponds to
\rho=0
, we have
e_{r}=e_{z}
and
e_{\theta}=e_{\rho}
. To ensure
\Delta U,\Delta v\in L^{2}
and smoothness of the pressure, we impose Dirichlet zero condition on
\rho
and
\phi
component of the velocity field and Neumann zero condition on
z
component and the pressure. This implies
\widetilde{U}_{\theta}(r,0)=\widetilde{U}_{\phi}(r,0)=0
and
\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{r}(r,0)=\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{P}(r,0)=0
. Likewise,
\widetilde{v}_{\theta}(r,0)=\widetilde{v}_{\phi}(r,0)=0
and
\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{v}_{r}(r,0)=\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{q}(r,0)=0
.

\bullet

At
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, which corresponds to
z=0
, we have
e_{r}=e_{\rho}
and
e_{\theta}=-e_{z}
, implying
\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{r}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\widetilde{U}_{\theta}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{\phi}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{P}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=0
by even symmetry. Conversely, by odd symmetry, we have
\widetilde{v}_{r}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{v}_{\theta}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\widetilde{v}_{\phi}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\widetilde{q}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=0
.

\bullet

At
r=0
, which corresponds to the origin in cylindrical coordinates, the even symmetry in
z
and Dirichlet zero condition in
\rho
directly
lead to
\widetilde{U}(0,\theta)=(0,0,0).
Similarly,
\partial_{r}\widetilde{P}(0,\theta)=0
and
\widetilde{q}(0,\theta)=0
. Finally, since at the origin in cylindrical coordinates,
\widetilde{v}
must align with the direction

e_{z}
, we know that
\widetilde{v}
takes the following form for a constant
C
:

\widetilde{v}=C(\cos\theta,-\sin\theta,0).

(5.4)

Another reason why we need to impose this boundary condition at
r=0
is because we have to cancel the two terms that contain the singular factor
\frac{1}{r^{2}}
in the Laplacian operator
\Delta
in the spherical coordinates, see Appendix
D
.
If we do not enforce this boundary condition and allow
\widetilde{v}
to have modes
\sin(m\theta)
or
\cos(m\theta)
with
m\geqslant 2
, then the equation for
\widetilde{v}
will generate some singular terms that do not cancel each other, rendering
\widetilde{v}\notin H^{2}
.

\bullet

At the far-field boundary, the condition is given by the function
A(\theta)
:
\lim_{r\to+\infty}r\widetilde{U}(r,\theta)=A(\theta).
We require
\widetilde{v}
to decay faster than
\widetilde{U}
by imposing
\lim_{r\to+\infty}r\widetilde{v}(r,\theta)=0.
In

[
25
]
, the authors estimate the decay rate as
approximately
O(r^{-4})
.

Here,
A(\theta)
satisfies the consistency condition derived from the
incompressibility constraint (
1.8
):

A_{r}(\theta)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(A_{\theta}(\theta)\sin\theta)=0.

(5.5)

5.3
Spherical coordinates and finite element method

In this section, we detail our setting for finite element approximation. The
incompressible Navier–Stokes equations in spherical coordinates read:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{U}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\widetilde{U}+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{P}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}=0,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}=0,\end{array}\right.

(5.6)

where
\nabla_{\text{sph}}
,
\text{div}_{\text{sph}}
, and

\Delta_{\text{sph}}
are the gradient, divergence, and Laplacian operators in
spherical coordinates, respectively (see Appendix
D
for explicit
expressions). The weak form of (
5.6
) is:

\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U},V\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle x\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U},V\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U},V\rangle-\langle\widetilde{P},\operatorname{div}V\rangle-\langle Q,\operatorname{div}\widetilde{U}\rangle+\langle\nabla\widetilde{U},\nabla V\rangle=0,

for all test functions
V\in H_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{3},Q\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the spherical coordinates, the weak form
reads:

\begin{array}[]{l}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle r\partial_{r}\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\langle\widetilde{P},\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}\\
-\langle Q,\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}+\langle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U},\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}=0,\end{array}

for all test functions
V\in H^{1}_{0,\operatorname{sph}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])^{3},Q\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{sph}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
.
The inner product is

\langle f,g\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}=\int_{\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\int_{\left[0,\infty\right]}f^{T}(r,\theta)g(r,\theta)r^{2}\sin\theta\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\theta.

In practice, we truncate the
domain to a finite domain
B(0,R)
with
R=20
. The far-field boundary condition
\lim_{r\to+\infty}r\widetilde{U}(r,\theta)=A(\theta)
is approximated by
the Dirichlet boundary condition:

\widetilde{U}(r=R,\theta)=\frac{1}{R}A(\theta),

where
A(\theta)
must satisfy the consistency constraint (
5.5
) derived from incompressibility. We impose a Neumann boundary condition for the pressure:
\partial_{r}\widetilde{P}(R,\theta)=0
.

We discretize the domain using finite elements, following closely the method
detailed in
[
25
]
. Specifically, the computational
domain is divided into
N_{r}\times N_{\theta}
squares, with
N_{r}=128
and

N_{\theta}=64
. Each square is subdivided into two triangles. As the
parameter
\gamma_{U}
increases, a larger computational domain is required to
ensure that truncation errors remain negligible. To efficiently handle this
domain expansion without modifying the finite element mesh, we adopt a spatial
rescaling strategy following the approach described in

[
25
]
. Specifically, we fix the computational domain
radius at
R=20
and introduce a spatial rescaling given by:

\widetilde{U}_{\kappa}(x)=\widetilde{U}(\kappa x).

This rescaling is equivalent to expanding the computational domain from
B(0,R)
to
B(0,\kappa R)
, while allowing the reuse of the same finite element mesh
for varying values of
\gamma_{U}
. The scaling factor
\kappa
is chosen to be:

\kappa=\sqrt{1+\frac{\gamma_{U}}{4}}.

Thus, the rescaled equations are given by:

\begin{array}[]{l}\kappa^{-1}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle r\partial_{r}\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\kappa^{-1}\langle\widetilde{P},\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}\\
-\kappa^{-1}\langle Q,\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}+\kappa^{-2}\langle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U},\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}=0,\end{array}

(5.7)

with boundary conditions:

\widetilde{U}(r=R,\theta)=\frac{1}{\kappa R}A(\theta).

Here, we omit the subscript
\kappa
for simplicity.
Finally, we discretize
the weak form (
5.7
) using Lagrange quadratic polynomials (P2
elements) for
\widetilde{U}
and linear polynomials (P1 elements) for

\widetilde{P}
. The discretization involves assembling not only bilinear forms
but trilinear forms, such as the convection term
\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\text{sph}}\widetilde{U},V\rangle_{\text{sph}}
, which require handling
third-order tensors. For this reason, we implement our finite element
discretization using the Python package
scikit-fem
rather than the
more commonly used
FEniCS
.

5.4
Eigenvalue solver

Suppose we have obtained a solution
\widetilde{U}
. Our next step is to compute
the eigenvalues of the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})
. The eigenvalue problem in spherical coordinates reads:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{v}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\widetilde{v}+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{v}+\widetilde{v}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{q}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{v}=\widetilde{\lambda}\widetilde{v},\\
\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{v}=0.\end{array}\right.

We start
by truncating the domain to a ball
B(0,R)
.
The same spatial rescaling by
\kappa
discussed earlier is then applied. Consequently,
the weak form of the eigenvalue problem is expressed as:

\begin{array}[]{l}\kappa^{-1}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}v,u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}+\kappa^{-1}\langle v\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U},u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle r\partial_{r}v,u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle v,u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}-\kappa^{-1}\langle q,\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}\\
-\kappa^{-1}\langle p,\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}v\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}+\kappa^{-2}\langle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}v,\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}}=\lambda\langle v,u\rangle_{\operatorname{sph}},\end{array}

(5.8)

for all test functions
u\in H^{1}_{0,\operatorname{sph}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])^{3},p\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{sph}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
. We impose a homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition at the truncation
radius
r=R
:
\widetilde{v}(R,\theta)=(0,0,0)
and a Neumann boundary condition for the pressure:
\partial_{r}\widetilde{q}(R,\theta)=0
.

We discretize this weak form using the finite element method described in the
previous section, which yields a generalized matrix eigenvalue problem of the
form:

Ax=\lambda Bx,

where
A
corresponds to the discretization of the left-hand side of equation
(
5.8
), and
B
represents the discretization of the inner
product term on the right-hand side. The resulting eigenvalue problem is
solved numerically using MATLAB.

Remark 6

In
[
25
]
, the authors noted that directly imposing
Dirichlet boundary conditions at a finite radius
R
might slightly distort
the computed solutions. For this reason, the numerical settings described in
this subsection and the previous section are employed only within the
gradient descent iterations for obtaining coarse solutions
\overline{U}

and
\overline{v}
, rather than for rigorous numerical analysis. To minimize
potential distortions and obtain accurate numerical solutions, we adopt an
interpolation-based extension technique, as detailed in the subsequent
section.

5.5
Extension to whole space

After obtaining a numerical solution
(\overline{U},\overline{P})
on the
bounded domain, we need to extend it to the entire space. To achieve this, we
introduce the coordinate transformation:

r=\tan\beta.

Under this transformation, we define new variables to capture the decay at the far field:

\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)=\frac{\widetilde{U}(\tan\beta,\theta)}{\cos\beta},\quad\widehat{P}(\beta,\theta)=\frac{\widetilde{P}(\tan\beta,\theta)}{\cos\beta}.

In these new variables, equation (
5.6
) takes the following
form:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\frac{1}{2}\cos^{2}\beta(\cos\beta\widehat{U}+\sin\beta\partial_{\beta}\widehat{U})+\cos\beta\widehat{U}\cdot\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}+\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{P}-\widetilde{\Delta}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}=0,\\
\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}=0,\end{array}\right.

where the definitions of
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}
,

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\operatorname{sph}}
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}_{\operatorname{sph}}
are
listed in Appendix
D
. The corresponding weak form can be expressed
similarly as:

\begin{array}[]{l}\kappa^{-1}\langle\cos\beta\widehat{U}\cdot\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U},V\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\cos^{2}\beta(\cos\beta\widehat{U}+\sin\beta\partial_{\beta}\widehat{U}),V\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}-\kappa^{-1}\langle\widehat{P},\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}\\
-\kappa^{-1}\langle Q,\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}+\kappa^{-2}\langle\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U},\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}V\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}=0,\end{array}

(5.9)

for all test functions
V\in H^{1}_{0,\operatorname{sphcv}}([0,\frac{\pi}{2}]^{2})^{3},Q\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{sphcv}}([0,\frac{\pi}{2}]^{2})
, with the
inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\operatorname{sphcv}}=\int_{\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]^{2}}f^{T}(\beta,\theta)g(\beta,\theta)\frac{\tan^{2}\beta}{\cos^{2}\beta}\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\beta\mathrm{d}\theta.

The far-field boundary condition transforms into:

\widehat{U}\left(\frac{\pi}{2},\theta\right)=\frac{A(\theta)}{\kappa}.

When numerically solving the weak form (
5.9
), it is
beneficial to select a larger scaling factor
\kappa
:

\kappa=2\sqrt{1+\gamma_{U}/4}.

We employ the same discretization method as before to solve this weak form
numerically. Given a solution
\overline{U}
defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, we first interpolate it onto the finite
element basis corresponding to the new mesh. Although the interpolated
function generally does not exactly satisfy equation (
5.9
), it provides a sufficiently accurate initial guess. Consequently, by
applying Newton’s iteration with fixed boundary conditions, we can efficiently
solve the transformed system within a few steps, obtaining a good
approximation over the entire spatial domain.

Next, we linearize the operator at the solution profile
\overline{U}

obtained from equation (
5.9
) and compute the corresponding
eigenvectors. We note that the computed solution profile
\overline{U}
and
eigenvector
\overline{v}
are close to being divergence-free, though not exactly
so. In the next subsections, we introduce a procedure to enforce the
divergence-free condition exactly.

Remark 7

Numerical instabilities occasionally lead to spurious eigenvalues with
highly oscillatory eigenvectors, which do not correspond to actual
eigenmodes of the operator. However, despite these numerical artifacts, the
method still enables us to approximate the genuine eigenvectors accurately.

5.6
Construction of divergence-free basis

To exactly enforce the divergence-free condition, we construct a
divergence-free basis. We first use vector spherical harmonics (see Appendix

E
for introduction) to expand the vector
\widetilde{U}
:

\widetilde{U}(r,\theta)=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}(\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}(r)Y_{l}(\theta)+\widetilde{U}_{l}^{X}(r)X_{l}(\theta)+\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Z}(r)Z_{l}(\theta)).

We note that when
l
is even, the vector field
(Y_{l},X_{l},0)
is even with
respect to
z
while
(0,0,Z_{l})
is odd. When
l
is odd, they have the
opposite parities. Therefore, for the even
\widetilde{U}
, we choose even
l
for

Y_{l},X_{l}
and odd
l
for
Z_{l}
. For the odd
\widetilde{v}
, it is the other way
around. Therefore, we modify our expansion to:

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}(\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}(r)Y_{2l}(\theta)+\widetilde{U}_{l}^{X}(r)X_{2l}(\theta))+\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Z}(r)Z_{2l-1}(\theta),

(5.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{v}(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{+\infty}(\widetilde{v}_{l}^{Y}(r)Y_{2l-1}(\theta)+\widetilde{v}_{l}^{X}(r)X_{2l-1}(\theta)+\widetilde{v}_{l}^{Z}(r)Z_{2l}(\theta)).

The divergence of
\widetilde{U}
is

\operatorname{div}\widetilde{U}=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}+\frac{2}{r}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}-\frac{\mu_{2l}}{r}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{X}\right)S_{l},\quad\mu_{2l}=2l(2l+1).

Therefore, the divergence-free condition is

r\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}+2\widetilde{U}_{l}^{Y}-\mu_{2l}\widetilde{U}_{l}^{X}=0.

Under the previously introduced coordinate transformation, the divergence-free
condition becomes:

\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\beta\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\beta}\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}+\frac{1}{2}(3+\cos 2\beta)\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}-\mu_{2l}\widehat{U}_{l}^{X}=0.

(5.11)

In practice, we truncate the series up to
L
terms. Therefore, the divergence
free condition splits into
L
equations relating the radial coefficient
functions
\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}
and
\widehat{U}_{l}^{X}
.

In order to expand the coefficient functions into trigonometric series, we first examine the boundary behavior. Specifically, we recall the boundary conditions in Section Section

5.2
as
\widehat{U}(\beta=0,\theta)=(0,0,0)
at the origin. The far field behavior (
1.13
) in Assumption
1
implies that

\widehat{U}=\frac{\widetilde{U}}{\cos\beta}=(\frac{A(\theta)}{r}+O(\frac{1}{r^{3}}))/O((\frac{\pi}{2}-\beta))=A(\theta)+O((\frac{\pi}{2}-\beta)^{2}),

where we have used the change of variable
r=\tan\beta=O((\frac{\pi}{2}-\beta)^{-1})
. These conditions lead naturally to the following expansion of
\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}(\beta)
and
\widehat{U}_{l}^{X}(\beta)
as:

\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{m,l}\sin((2m-1)\beta),\quad\widehat{U}_{l}^{X}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M+1}\mathrm{B}^{U,X}_{m,l}\sin((2m-1)\beta).

(5.12)

It should be noted, however, that this expansion lacks smoothness at the origin
\beta=0
. Substituting these into the divergence-free constraint (
5.11
)
yields the algebraic relations:

\mathrm{B}^{U,X}_{m,l}=-\frac{1}{\mu_{2l}}\left(\frac{m}{2}\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{m+1,l}-\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{m,l}-\frac{m-1}{2}\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{m-1,l}\right),\quad\forall m\geqslant 1,l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}

(5.13)

with boundary conditions:
\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{0,l}=\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{M+1,l}=\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{M+2,l}=0
.

When
l=0
, we have
\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{m,l}=0
, and hence
\widehat{U}_{l}^{Y}(\beta)
becomes trivial. Moreover, since
X_{0}(\theta)=0
, the first term

\widetilde{U}_{0}^{Y}(r)Y_{0}(\theta)+\widetilde{U}_{0}^{X}(r)X_{0}(\theta)
vanishes.
Consequently, in the first series of (
5.10
), the summation
effectively starts from 1.

For the eigenvector
\overline{v}
, the boundary conditions are given by:
\overline{v}(\beta=0,\theta)=C(\cos\theta,-\sin\theta,0)
and
\overline{v}\left(\beta=\frac{\pi}{2},\theta\right)=(0,0,0)
. Consequently, only the
functions
\widehat{v}_{1}^{X}
and
\widehat{v}_{1}^{Y}
remain nonzero at
\beta=0
. To
satisfy these boundary conditions, we adopt the following expansions:

\widehat{v}_{1}^{Y}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m,1}\cos((2m-1)\beta),\quad\widehat{v}_{1}^{X}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M+1}\mathrm{B}^{v,X}_{m,1}\cos((2m-1)\beta),\quad\text{for }l=1,

and

\widehat{v}_{l}^{Y}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m,l}\sin(2m\beta),\quad\widehat{v}_{l}^{X}(\beta)=\sum_{m=1}^{M+1}\mathrm{B}^{v,X}_{m,l}\sin(2m\beta).\quad\text{for $l\geqslant 2$.}

The coefficients must satisfy the algebraic relations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}^{v,X}_{m,1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{2l-1}}\left(\frac{m}{2}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m+1,1}-\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m,1}-\frac{m-1}{2}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m-1,1}\right),

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{B}^{v,X}_{m,l}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{2l-1}}\left(\frac{2m+1}{4}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m+1,l}-\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m,l}-\frac{2m-1}{4}\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{m-1,l}\right),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

with boundary conditions:
\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{0,l}=\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{M+1,l}=\mathrm{B}^{v,Y}_{M+2,l}=0
.

These algebraic constraints ensure strict divergence-freeness of
\overline{U}
and

\overline{v}
. In the next subsection, we demonstrate how to interpolate our
solutions onto this basis.

5.7
Divergence-free interpolation

In the preceding sections, we obtained numerical approximations for the
profile
\overline{U}
and the eigenvector
\overline{v}
using the finite element
method. To ensure strict adherence to the divergence-free condition, we now
interpolate these solutions onto the carefully structured divergence-free
basis developed in the previous subsection.

We use
\overline{U}_{\theta}
as an example. We select a computational mesh
defined by
\beta_{j}=\frac{j\pi}{2M},\theta_{k}=\frac{k\pi}{2L}
, and
evaluate
\overline{U}_{\theta}
at each grid point
(\beta_{j},\theta_{k})
. We apply
discrete Fourier transforms in
\theta
to obtain expansions:

\overline{U}_{\theta}(\beta_{j},\theta)=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{F}_{\theta}\overline{U}_{\theta}^{l}(\beta_{j})\sin(2l\theta),\quad j=1,\ldots,M.

where
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
denotes the discrete Fourier transform with
respect to
\theta
. We then convert the obtained Fourier expansions to the
eigenfunction basis
X_{2l}
. Therefore, we get the value of
\widehat{U}_{l}^{X}
at
each
\beta_{j}
. Applying another discrete Fourier transform in
\beta
, we
derive the coefficients
\mathrm{B}^{U,X}_{m,l}
. This process is similarly
conducted for other components
\overline{U}_{r}
,
\overline{v}_{r}
and

\overline{v}_{\theta}
, with their respective boundary conditions. For components

\overline{U}_{\phi}
,
\overline{P}
,
\overline{v}_{\phi}
and
\overline{q}
, the linear
transformation is not required since we do not need to represent them in the
Legendre basis to enforce divergence-free conditions.

We explicitly enforce the divergence-free conditions by setting coefficients

\mathrm{B}^{U,X}_{m,l}
and
\mathrm{B}^{v,X}_{m,l}
according to the derived
algebraic constraints (
5.13
) and (
5.14
). In practice, we observe only small deviations from
the original values.

Typically, the initial interpolation yields residuals insufficiently small for
rigorous numerical analysis. To address this, we further refine our solutions.
Defining the residual:

R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}(\overline{U},\overline{P})=-\frac{1}{2}\overline{U}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\overline{U}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{P}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}.

We want to find a correction
\delta\overline{U},\delta\overline{P}
to minimize

\|R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}(\overline{U}+\delta\overline{U},\overline{P}+\delta\overline{P})\|
.
We approximate
\delta\overline{U}
within the structured divergence-free basis as
follows:

\delta\overline{U}_{r}(\beta,\theta)=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta\mathrm{B}^{U,Y}_{ml}\sin((2m-1)\beta)Y_{2l}(\theta).

(5.15)

For the other entries, we adopt similar ansatz expansions. Motivated by
Newton’s method, we linearize
R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}
around
(\overline{U},\overline{P})
,
yielding the following equation:
