-\frac{1}{2}\delta\overline{U}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\delta\overline{U}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{U}+\delta\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{P}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{U}=R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}(\overline{U},\overline{P}),

(5.16)

which cannot be solved precisely by the approximated
\delta\overline{U}
. Hence,
we solve the resulting optimization problem:

\min_{\delta\overline{U}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}\delta\overline{U}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\delta\overline{U}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{U}+\delta\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{P}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\delta\overline{U}-R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}(\overline{U},\overline{P})\right|.

(5.17)

The above optimization problem is essentially a least squares problem for the
coefficients of
\delta U
and
\delta P
and can be efficiently solved using
the conjugate gradient method without the need to construct the matrix
corresponding to the bilinear form. The updated profile
(\overline{U}+\delta\overline{U},\overline{P}+\delta\overline{P})
exhibits a smaller residual. We iterate
this refinement process until the residual is sufficiently small for precise
numerical analysis. This iterative refinement method is similarly applied to
refine the eigenvector
\overline{v}
. Notice that by Assumption
1
, the
residue is orthogonal to
\overline{v}
and
\lambda
is uniquely determined given

\overline{v}
, and we thus update
\overline{v}
and
\overline{q}
before updating

\overline{\lambda}
.

5.8
Summary

The numerical algorithm is summarized as follows:

Input:

Initial bound
\gamma_{\lambda}
and boundary
condition
A_{0}

Output:

Profile
\overline{U}^{\text{cv}}
and eigen pair
(\overline{\lambda},\overline{v}^{\text{cv}})

Construct a mesh on the computational domain
[0,R]\times\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
;

Assemble the weak form and obtain the corresponding matrices and
tensors;

Set initial bound
\gamma_{U},\varepsilon
;

Initialize iteration index
n=0
and eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=2
;

while

\lambda_{n}>-\gamma_{\lambda}

do

Update scaling factor
\kappa=\sqrt{1+\gamma_{U}/4}
and adjust boundary condition
A_{n}
;

while

n=0
or
|\lambda_{n}-\lambda_{n-1}|>\varepsilon

do

Solve equation (
5.7
) under boundary condition
A_{n}
using Newton’s method;

Compute the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}
of the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}(\overline{U}_{n})
;

Evaluate the gradient
\frac{\delta\lambda}{\delta U}
using (
5.1
);

Solve the projected gradient problem to determine descent direction;

Update solution and boundary condition:
U_{n},A_{n}\to U_{n+1}^{\ast},A_{n+1}
;
//
Note
U_{n+1}^{\ast}
is not yet on the solution manifold; it will be corrected in the next iteration of the loop.

Increment the iteration index
n\leftarrow n+1
;
end while

Increase
\gamma_{U}
;
end while

Construct a new mesh on the extended domain
\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]^{2}
;

Transform the computed solution
\overline{U}_{n}
onto the new mesh;

Solve equation (
5.9
) using Newton’s method:
\overline{U}_{n}\to\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}}
;

Compute eigenpair
(\overline{\lambda}_{n},\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}})
;

Interpolate the solutions onto a divergence-free basis:
\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}}\to\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{df}},\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{df}}
;

Initialize iteration index
n=0
;

Set the bound for residual
\varepsilon_{\operatorname{res}}
;

while

\|R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{U}(\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{df}},\overline{P}^{\operatorname{df}}_{n})\|>\varepsilon_{\operatorname{res}}
or
\|R_{\operatorname{sph}}^{v}(\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{df}},\overline{q}^{\operatorname{df}}_{n})\|>\varepsilon_{\operatorname{res}}

do

Solve (
5.17
) to obtain
(\delta\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\delta\overline{P}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n})
and an analogous problem for
(\delta\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\delta\overline{q}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n})
;

Update
\overline{U}_{n+1}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{P}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n+1},\overline{v}_{n+1}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{q}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n+1}\leftarrow\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}}+\delta\overline{U}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{P}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n}+\delta\overline{P}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}}+\delta\overline{v}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}},\overline{q}^{\operatorname{cv}}_{n}+\delta\overline{q}_{n}^{\operatorname{cv}}
;

Update
\overline{\lambda}_{n+1}
based on orthogonality by Assumption
1
;

Increment the iteration index
n\leftarrow n+1
;
end while

return

Profile
\overline{U}^{\text{cv}}
and eigen pair
(\overline{\lambda},\overline{v}^{\text{cv}})
;

Algorithm 1

Gradient descent for eigenvalue

We also present our numerical results in cylindrical coordinates.
Figure
1
shows the three components of
\overline{U}
together with the streamlines, while Figure
2
displays the corresponding components of
\overline{v}
. Compared with the results of Guillod and Sverak
[
25
]
, our profile
\overline{U}
exhibits relatively larger components in the
\rho
- and
z
-directions and resembles a hyperbolic flow. The azimuthal component of the eigenvector
\overline{v}
is comparatively small, indicating that the bifurcation primarily occurs in the
\rho
-
z
plane, where it disrupts the hyperbolic structure. Figure
3
illustrates
\overline{U}+\sigma\overline{v}
for
\sigma=10,20,30,50
, which can be interpreted as the flow at different times
\tau
after the bifurcation has taken place. As
\sigma
increases, the parity symmetry with respect to
z
is broken, and small swirls emerge near the
\rho
-axis. In figure
4
, we display the vorticity
\omega_{\phi}
of
\overline{U},\overline{v}
and
\overline{U}+10\overline{v}
. The results show that the vorticity is concentrated along the
\rho
-axis, where
z=0
.

6
Finite Rank Approximation

In this section, we construct the finite rank approximation for
L_{2}=-\Pi(U\cdot Q)
. Recall the decomposition of
\nabla\overline{U}
:

Q_{s}=\frac{\nabla\overline{U}+\nabla^{T}\overline{U}}{2},\quad Q_{s}+\eta_{1}I=Q_{p}-Q_{m},\quad Q_{p}\succeq 0,\quad Q_{m}\succeq 0.

Here,
Q_{m}
is the negative part of
Q_{s}+\eta_{1}I
. In previous sections, we
choose
Q=Q_{m}
. We truncate the domain
\Omega=[0,R_{0}]\times\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
with
R_{0}=32
and verify that outside
\Omega
, we
have
\lambda_{\max}(Q_{s})<0.191
. Thus, we set
\eta_{1}=0.191
so that the
support of
Q_{m}
is fully contained in
\Omega
.

Although it is natural to take
Q=Q_{m}
, this choice has the drawback that

Q
is not smooth, which complicates the future error analysis of numerical
integrals. Fortunately, we only need
Q
to satisfy

Q_{s}+\eta_{1}I+Q\succeq 0.

(6.1)

Therefore, we design a smooth matrix function
Q(x)
on
\Omega
that meets
this condition. The algorithm is described in Section
6.1
. Next,
we construct an appropriate basis to approximate functions defined on
\Omega

in Section
6.2
and solve an eigenvalue problem to build

L_{\operatorname{ap}}
in Section
6.3
. Finally, in Section
6.4
, we solve

L\phi_{i}=Q\psi_{i},

to define the operator
G
.

6.1
Construction of
Q

We first construct
Q
. Since
Q
is a symmetric matrix function, it has 6
independent entries:
Q_{11},Q_{12},Q_{13},Q_{22},Q_{23},Q_{33}
. Each
entry satisfies the same boundary conditions as the corresponding entry of

Q_{s}
on the three sides of the domain boundary:

\theta=0,\frac{\pi}{2}\text{ and }r=0.

Specifically, if
Q_{s,ij}=0
on a particular part of the boundary (say,

r=0
), then we impose the Dirichlet boundary condition
Q_{ij}=0
on
that same boundary segment. On the remaining boundary segments, where
Q_{s,ij}
does not vanish, we instead impose the Neumann boundary condition

\partial_{n}Q=0
. Consequently,
Q
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{r}Q_{11}(0,\theta)=\partial_{r}Q_{12}(0,\theta)=Q_{13}(0,\theta)=\partial_{r}Q_{22}(0,\theta)=Q_{23}(0,\theta)=Q_{33}(0,\theta)=0,\\
\partial_{\theta}Q_{11}(r,0)=Q_{12}(r,0)=Q_{13}(r,0)=\partial_{\theta}Q_{22}(r,0)=Q_{23}(r,0)=\partial_{\theta}Q_{33}(r,0)=0,\\
\partial_{\theta}Q_{11}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=Q_{12}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}Q_{13}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}Q_{22}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=Q_{23}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\partial_{\theta}Q_{33}\left(r,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=0.\end{array}

On
r=R_{0}
, we need to impose the Dirichlet boundary condition
Q=0
since
the support of
Q
is fully contained in
\Omega
. Guided by these boundary
conditions, we select the corresponding trigonometric basis to approximate
each entry. For instance,
Q_{11}
is approximated by

Q_{11}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\sum_{m=1}^{M}q_{11,lm}\cos\left(\frac{(2m+1)\pi r}{2R_{0}}\right)\cos(2l\theta).

A naïve approach would be to apply the discrete Fourier transform to
Q_{m}

and truncate the series. However, this does not ensure
Q_{s}+\eta_{1}I+Q\succeq 0
. To address this, we first select a slightly smaller
\eta_{0}=0.19
and compute the corresponding
Q_{m}
. Then we reconstruct
Q
using the
same idea. As long as
L,M
are sufficiently large, we obtain (
6.1
).

This method, however, typically requires very large
L,M
, which is
undesirable. To improve efficiency, we fix
L=150,M=300
and initialize

Q_{0}=0
with
\eta_{0}=0.19
. For every entry
Q_{i}
, we calculate

\delta Q_{m,i}:=\text{negative part of}(Q_{s}+\eta_{0}I+Q_{i}).

If
\|\lambda_{\max}(\delta Q_{m,i})\|_{L^{\infty}}<\delta_{\eta}:=\eta_{1}-\eta_{0}
, we terminate the iteration.

Otherwise, we approximate
\delta Q_{m,i}
by a trigonometric polynomial,
denoted
\delta Q_{i}
. Specifically, we take
\delta Q_{i}
to be the Fejér
transform of
\delta Q_{m,i}
in both
r
and
\theta
, which is equivalent
to convolving with the kernel:

K_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{1-\cos(Nx)}{1-\cos(x)}\right).

This algorithm is particularly easy to implement because convolution acts as a
Fourier multiplier. Thus, it suffices to compute the Fourier transform of the
kernel, whose coefficients are given by

\mathcal{F}_{x}K_{N}^{j}=\left(1-\frac{|j|}{N}\right),\quad|j|<N,

and vanish for
|j|\geqslant N
. We then update
Q_{i}
by

Q_{i+1}=Q_{i}+\delta Q_{i}.

Finally, we can obtain a smooth function
Q
satisfying (
6.1
).

6.2
Construction of spectral basis

Since the
Q
-norm
\|\cdot\|_{Q}
is controlled by the standard
L^{2}
-norm,
we begin by considering the following eigenvalue problem in Rayleigh quotient
form:

\lambda_{n}^{s}=\sup_{\dim(V_{n})=n-1}\inf_{u\neq 0,u\in V,u\bot V_{n}}\frac{\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{\Omega}}{\|u\|^{2}_{\Omega}},

(6.2)

where
V
is the space of admissible functions, namely divergence-free
axisymmetric functions. We obtain a sequence of
\lambda_{n}^{s}
in ascending
order with associated eigenfunctions
\psi_{n}^{s}
. These eigenfunctions will
constitute our spectral basis.

The solutions to this eigenvalue problem admit explicit representations in
terms of Bessel functions and spherical harmonics, as established in the
following lemma.

Lemma 4

Consider the eigenvalue problem (
6.2
) with

\Omega
being the unit ball in 3D. The first eigenpair is

\lambda_{0}^{s}=0,\quad\psi_{0}^{s}=(\cos\theta,-\sin\theta,0).

For
s\geqslant 1
, the remaining eigenvalues can be written as

\lambda_{n}^{s}=(\alpha_{l,m}^{i})^{2},\quad l\geqslant 1,\quad m\geqslant 1,\quad i=Y\text{ or }Z.

Case 1:
i=Z

Here,
\alpha_{l,m}^{Z}>0
denotes the m-th zero of the equation for
r
:

rJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(r)-\frac{1}{2}J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(r)=0,

(6.3)

where
J_{\nu}
is the Bessel function of the first kind. The corresponding
eigenfunction is

\psi_{n}^{s}=\psi_{ml}^{Z}=\frac{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha^{Z}_{ml}r)}{\sqrt{r}}Z_{l}(\theta).

Case 2:
i=Y

Here,
\alpha_{l,m}^{Y}>0
denotes the m-th zero of the equation for
r
:

(r^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))r\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(r)+(r^{2}-l(l-1))(r^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(r)=0,

(6.4)

where

\tilde{j}_{l}(r):=\frac{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(r)}{r^{\frac{3}{2}}}.

The corresponding eigenfunction is

\psi_{n}^{s}=\psi_{ml}^{Y}=(B_{ml}\tilde{j}_{l}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r)+lA_{ml}r^{l-1})Y_{l}(\theta)+\left(\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}(r\alpha^{Y}_{ml}\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r)+2\tilde{j}_{l}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r))+A_{l}r^{l-1}\right)X_{l}(\theta),

with

A_{ml}=-((\alpha^{Y}_{ml})^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}),\quad B_{ml}=(\alpha^{Y}_{ml})^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2).

The proof of the lemma is given in Appendix
F
.

Remark 8

(Scaling and Parity)

In our setting,
\Omega=B_{0}(R_{0})
with
R_{0}=32
. The eigenvalues and
eigenfunctions can be obtained by rescaling with respect to
R_{0}
.
Specifically, the eigenvalues are
\frac{\lambda^{s}_{n}}{R_{0}^{2}}
and the
eigenvectors are
\psi_{n}^{s}\left(\frac{r}{R_{0}},\theta\right)
. Recall
that, since
U
and
v
also satisfy different boundary conditions, in the
vector spherical harmonics expansion as in Section
5.6
, only
even
l
or only odd
l
appear. Hence, we retain only eigenpairs of certain
parities in the construction. We denote by
\lambda_{k}^{s,U}
the
k
-th
smallest eigenvalue among all
\frac{\lambda^{s}_{n}}{R_{0}^{2}}
with even
eigenfunctions
\psi_{k}^{s,U}=\psi_{n}^{s}\left(\frac{r}{R_{0}},\theta\right)
, and similarly define
\lambda_{k}^{s,v}
and
\psi_{k}^{s,v}
.

Let
V_{N}^{i}
denote the space spanned by the first
N
eigenvectors
\psi_{n}^{s,i}
for
i=U,v
respectively. We define the projection
I^{i}_{N}
as

I^{i}_{N}\psi=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\langle\psi,\psi_{k}^{s,i}\rangle\psi_{k}^{s,i}.

(6.5)

It is both the
L^{2}
and
H^{1}
projection onto
V_{N}^{i}
. By (
6.2
),
it satisfies

\|\psi-I^{i}_{N}\psi\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\lambda_{N+1}^{c,i}}\|\psi\|_{H^{1}},\quad\|\psi-I^{i}_{N}\psi\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\psi\|_{L^{2}},\quad\forall\psi\in V.

Recall the constant
K_{5}^{i}
from (
4.15
), we can set

K_{5}^{i}=\frac{\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{\infty}}{\eta_{2}+\lambda_{N+1}^{s,i}}.

Numerically, we find
\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{\infty}\leqslant 4.5
. Since
we hope
K_{5}^{i}\leqslant 0.45
, we need to choose
N
such that

\lambda_{N+1}^{s,i}>10
. Thus, we take
N=1257
for
U
and
N=1260

for
v
to ensure the required bound.

Finally, note that it is necessary to compute all eigenvectors corresponding
to eigenvalues smaller than 10. In particular, we need the following estimate
to guarantee that all eigenvalues below 10 have been identified: (This lemma
is still for the unscaled eigenvalue
\lambda^{s}_{n}
)

Lemma 5

Let
0=\beta_{l,0}<\beta_{l,1}<\beta_{l,2}<\cdots
denote the zeros of
J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)
and
\gamma=\sqrt{l(l-1)(l+2)}
.

\bullet

If
l=1
, define
\tilde{\beta}_{1,j}=\beta_{1,j}
;

\bullet

If
l\geqslant 2
and
\gamma\neq\beta_{l,j}
for any
j>0
,
denote
\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}
as the sequence obtained by sorting

\{\beta_{l,j},\gamma\}
in ascending order.

Let
s_{l}
be the unique positive root of:

Q_{l}(s)=s^{3}-4l^{2}s^{2}+l(l-1)(6l^{2}+11l+7)s-3l(l-1)^{2}(l+1)^{2}(l+2),

and
t_{l}=\sqrt{l(l+1)}
. Assume that
\sqrt{s_{l}}
is not a root of
(
6.4
) and that
t_{l}
is not a root of (
6.3
).

Then the following statements hold:

1.

The equation (
6.4
) has exactly one root on every
interval
(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j},\tilde{\beta}_{l,j+1})
for
j\geqslant 0
.

2.

The equation (
6.3
) has exactly one root on every
interval
(\beta_{l,j},\beta_{l,j+1})
for
j\geqslant 0
.

3.

The first zeros
\alpha_{l,1}^{Y}
and
\alpha_{l,1}^{Z}
satisfy the
lower bounds:

\alpha_{l,1}^{Y}\geqslant\sqrt{s_{l}},\quad\alpha_{l,1}^{Z}\geqslant t_{l}.

The proof of the lemma is given in Appendix
F
. In practice, we
use the lower bounds of
\alpha_{l,1}^{Y}
and
\alpha_{l,1}^{Z}
to determine
the largerst index
l
required, denoted by
l_{\max}
. For every
1\leqslant l\leqslant l_{\max}
, we then search all the intervals
(\beta_{l,j},\beta_{l,j+1})
and
(\widetilde{\beta}_{l,j},\widetilde{\beta}_{l,j+1})
for

\alpha_{l,1}^{Z}
and
\alpha_{l,1}^{Y}
, respectively, until the left endpoint
of the interval exceeds the prescribed threshold.

6.3
Eigenvalue problem

We subsequently solve the finite-dimensional eigenvalue problem numerically:

\langle\psi,Q\psi_{n}^{Q,i}\rangle_{\Omega}=\lambda^{Q,i}_{n}\langle\psi,\psi_{n}^{Q,i}\rangle_{E},\quad\forall\psi\in V_{N}^{i}.

on the space
V_{N}^{i}
. Recall that
\psi_{k}^{s,i}
are orthogonal in
L^{2}
and

H^{1}
. We will normalize them to obtain an orthonormal basis in
E
as

\widetilde{\psi}_{k}^{s,i}
and compute the matrix:

(A_{Q}^{i})_{k,j}=\langle\widetilde{\psi}_{k}^{s,i},Q\widetilde{\psi}_{j}^{s,i}\rangle_{\Omega},

(6.6)

using numerical integration, and solve the resulting eigenvalue problem:

A_{Q}^{i}\psi^{Q,i}_{n}=\lambda^{Q,i}_{n}\psi^{Q,i}_{n}.

Here, by an abuse of notation, we use
\psi^{Q,i}_{n}
to denote both the
eigenvector and its coordinates in the basis
\widetilde{\psi}_{k}^{s,i}
.

We solve the matrix eigenvalue problem numerically to determine all
eigenvalues
\lambda^{Q,i}_{n}\geqslant 0.46
. Numerical computations yield:

\lambda^{U}_{20}\approx 0.456<0.46,\quad\lambda^{v}_{25}\approx 0.453<0.6.

These numerics determine the rank used in the finite-rank operator. The
computed eigenvectors provide the foundation for a finite-rank approximation
of
L_{2}
, which corresponds to the second step in Section

4.2
.

6.4
Inversion on the subspace

Finally, we invert the operator
L
on the subspace, as in the last step in
Section
4.2
. We numerically solve equation (
4.11
) by employing the finite element method described in Section
5.5
,
using the
(\beta,\theta)
coordinates. We remark that for
\phi_{n}^{v}
, since

\widetilde{\lambda}
is not explicit in (
4.1
), we invert instead
the operator
P_{\overline{v}}(L^{v}-\overline{\lambda})v
and leave the small term

\lambda\psi^{v}_{n}
in the residue
r_{n}^{v}
. Additionally, we must
impose the orthogonality constraint:

\langle\phi_{n}^{v},\overline{v}\rangle=0,

which is numerically enforced by solving the following modified linear system:

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&y\\
y^{T}&0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
\mu\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}b\\
0\end{array}\right),

where the matrix
A
stands for the discretization of the linear operator
L^{v}-\overline{\lambda}I
, and
the vector
x,y,b
stands for
\phi^{v}_{n},\overline{v},Q\overline{\psi}_{n}^{v}

respectively.
\mu
is a scalar Lagrange multiplier ensuring the orthogonality
constraint.

The numerical solutions
\Phi
are then interpolated onto the explicitly
constructed divergence-free basis presented in Section
5.6
. To maintain accuracy, we subsequently refine our solutions using iterative
correction techniques detailed in Section
5.7
, until sufficiently
small residuals are obtained. Specifically, we iteratively minimize the
residual:

\left|-\frac{1}{2}\phi_{n}^{U}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\phi_{n}^{U}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\phi_{n}^{U}+\phi_{n}^{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}P_{n}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\phi_{n}^{U}-Q\psi_{n}^{U}\right|,

for
U
and

\left|-\left(\frac{1}{2}+\overline{\lambda}\right)\phi_{n}^{v}-\frac{1}{2}r\partial_{r}\phi_{n}^{v}+\overline{U}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\phi_{n}^{v}+\phi_{n}^{v}\cdot\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\overline{U}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}q_{n}-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\phi_{n}^{v}-Q\phi_{n}^{v}-\mu\overline{v}\right|,

where
\mu
should be again understood as the scalar Lagrange multiplier
ensuring the orthogonality constraint. Finally, we obtain exactly
divergence-free functions
\overline{\phi}^{U}_{n}
,
\overline{\phi}^{v}_{n}
, and we have to
modify
\overline{\phi}^{v}_{n}
by a projection to ensure orthogonality to
\overline{v}
.

Remark 9

Since we require
\phi^{U}_{n}
belongs to
L^{2}
, the boundary condition at
\beta=\frac{\pi}{2}
becomes

\phi^{U}_{n}(\frac{\pi}{2},\theta)=0.

When solving for
\phi^{U}_{n}
with finite element method, we must impose the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition, which differs from the condition for
\overline{U}
. Moreover, in the expansion (
5.12
), the basis functions
\sin((2m-1)\beta)
must be replaced by
\sin(2m\beta)
. Consequently, the coefficients
\mathrm{B}_{m,l}
-s are required to satisfy a modified condition.

7
On Rigorous Numerical Verification

In this section we outline the numerical framework we use for validation. The rigorous results of this paper are contained in Sections
1
–
6
; Section
7
describes the numerical procedure for the ongoing computer-assisted verification. We are implementing these routines and will soon release the code and reproducibility scripts in a public GitHub repository. A subsequent version will report the final constants (we expect only minor adjustments) and the full error budgets from the completed verification.

We focus our effort on verifying the bounds that enter Assumption
1
and the estimates used in Sections
4.2
and
6.2
–
6.3
. Throughout we work in the spherical coordinates and employ the change of variables

r=\tan\beta,\qquad(\beta,\theta)\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})\times[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2}],

so that all fields are expanded on trigonometric bases in
(\beta,\theta)
. The items below detail how we bound the residuals,
\!L^{\infty}
-norms, eigenvalue envelopes, and quadrature errors appearing in our verification, in a way that is fully compatible with Assumption
1
.

Before we go into the specific details of numerical verification for our method, we would like to make the following observations. First of all, we would like to point out that the numerical verification for the nonuniqueness problem of Leray-Hopf solutions of the 3D Navier–Stokes equations is much simpler than that for the 3D Euler singularity program in
[
12
,
14
,
13
]
.
This is largely due to the fact that
the 3D Euler singularity problem presents a much more challenging problem. There is

no
diffusion to provide an additional damping effect, so the stability framework must combine
L^{\infty}
and

C^{1/2}
controls with
singular weights
, to extract a small amount of damping from local terms. However, one has to control multiple nonlocal singular integral
operators that do not contribute to stability of the linearized operator. Because local damping is weak, one is forced to obtain very sharp
stability constants and use space-time linearized solves to control a finite–rank perturbation of
the linearized operator.

In contrast, in the present Navier–Stokes setting, the viscous term yields coercivity, so we can work in the norms
regular

L^{2}
and
H^{1}
without singular weights. The linearized operator is coercive in
L^{2}
with a relatively large coercivity coefficient. For
U_{1}
, the coecivity coefficient in the norm
L^{2}
is given by
1/4-\eta-M_{2}^{U}\approx 0.054
, where we take
\eta=0.191
and obtain an upper bound for
M_{2}^{U}\leqslant 0.005
. For
v_{1}
, the coercivity coefficient is given by
M_{5}^{v}\approx 0.16
, see Section
4.5
for more details. Our a posteriori bounds close using only the residuals
L^{2}
of
\overline{U}
and
\overline{v}
(plus a few pointwise
L^{\infty}
envelopes), rather than the weighted
L^{\infty}/C^{1/2}
controls required in the case of the 3D Euler singularity. The discrete operators we assemble are modest in size (about
1200\times 1200
), and the finite-rank verification reduces to a
static
generalized eigenproblem of size
25\times 25
. Thanks to strong viscous damping and the effective low-rank approximation of the compact operator
L_{2}^{U}
using a small number of generalized eigenfunctions
\psi_{j}^{Q,u}
corresponding to the largest eigenvalues, retaining
25
modes suffices to close the estimates via the Schauder fixed point theorem.

Moreover, because we approximate
\overline{U}
and
\overline{v}
by finite trigonometric tensor products in
(\beta,\theta)
, the residuals
E^{U}
and
E^{v}
admit
analytic

L^{2}
evaluation, and many pointwise quantities follow from explicit trigonometric formulas. The only delicate numerical step is the
Q
–inner product along the
\beta
–direction under the change of variable
r=\tan\beta
, for which we use a fourth-order composite quadrature together with a rigorous
W^{4,\infty}
bound on the scalar integrand
(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u})\cdot(Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})
to certify the quadrature error that enters the small matrices. These structural advantages, including viscous coercivity, low-dimensional spectral checks, and analytic trigonometric calculus, make our verification far lighter than that in the 3D Euler singularity case.

7.1
Analytic
L^{2}
residuals via a trigonometric representation

We expand the approximate self-similar profile
\overline{U}
and the (generalized) eigenfunction
\overline{v}
as finite trigonometric sums

\overline{U}(\beta,\theta)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta}}\ \sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{\theta}}\Big(\mathbf{A}^{ss}_{pq}\,\sin(p\beta)\sin(q\theta)+\mathbf{A}^{sc}_{pq}\,\sin(p\beta)\cos(q\theta)+\mathbf{A}^{cs}_{pq}\,\cos(p\beta)\sin(q\theta)+\mathbf{A}^{cc}_{pq}\,\cos(p\beta)\cos(q\theta)\Big),

where each coefficient
\mathbf{A}^{\star}_{pq}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
collects the three components of
\overline{U}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\beta},\mathcal{P}_{\theta}\subset\mathbb{N}
are finite index sets.
and similarly for
\overline{v}
. Because sums, products, and derivatives of sines/cosines remain finite trigonometric sums, every term in the residuals
E^{U}
and
E^{v}
stays in the same class. The only division introduced arises from the Jacobian in the change of variables

r=\tan\beta
, which produces factors of the form either
\sin(n\beta)/\sin(\beta)
or
\cos((2m+1)\beta)/\cos(\beta)
. Note that we never have
\cos((2m)\beta)/\cos(\beta)
in our equations. Such terms will be unbounded for
\theta\approx\pi/2
.
For
\sin(n\beta)/\sin(\beta)
, we know that this ratio admits an exact trigonometric expansion, so no true division remains.
Indeed, recalling that

\frac{\sin(n\beta)}{\sin(\beta)}=U_{n-1}(\cos\beta),

where
U_{n-1}
denotes the Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind, we obtain
the explicit cosine series

\frac{\sin(n\beta)}{\sin(\beta)}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1+2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\cos(2k\beta),&n=2m+1,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle 2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\cos\big((2k-1)\beta\big),&n=2m.\end{cases}

For
\frac{\cos\big((2m+1)\beta\big)}{\cos\beta}
,
we have for any integer
m\geqslant 0
and
\beta\not\equiv\tfrac{\pi}{2}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ \pi)
,

\frac{\cos\big((2m+1)\beta\big)}{\cos\beta}=(-1)^{m}\!\left(1+2\sum_{k=1}^{m}(-1)^{k}\cos(2k\,\beta)\right).

In particular, the ratio is a finite cosine polynomial in
\beta
(no division remaining).
Hence every such Jacobian factor can be represented exactly as a finite trigonometric sum and all inner products needed for

\|E^{U}\|_{L^{2}},\qquad\|E^{v}\|_{L^{2}}

are evaluated
analytically
.

We stress again the importance of imposing the correct boundary condition (
5.4
) for
\widetilde{v}
at
\theta=0
, If we allow
\widetilde{v}
to have modes
sin(m\theta)
or
cos(m\theta)
with
m\geqslant 2
, then the equation for
\widetilde{v}
will generate some unbounded terms that would lead to an unbounded residual error
E^{v}
.

7.2
Certified
L^{\infty}
bounds on trigonometric polynomials

Let
f
be any scalar component of
\overline{\phi}_{j}
,
\overline{v}
, or
\overline{U}
. Partition the
\beta
(or
\theta
) interval into
M
subintervals
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
of uniform mesh size
h
, and denote
f_{i}=f(x_{i})
. In each interval
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
, using the classical error bound for linear
interpolation with the Lagrange (Cauchy) form of the remainder, we obtain the following.

|f(x)|\leqslant\max\{f_{i},f_{i+1}\}+\frac{h^{2}}{8}\,\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([x_{i},x_{i+1}])},\qquad x\in[x_{i},x_{i+1}],

where
h=x_{i+1}-x_{i}
and
f_{i}=f(x_{i})
.

For a trigonometric polynomial with the highest wavenumber
N
we have the classical bound
\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{2}\|f\|_{\infty}
, which gives the local envelope

|f(x)|\leqslant\frac{\max\{f_{i},f_{i+1}\}}{1-\tfrac{(Nh)^{2}}{8}}\!,\qquad\Rightarrow\quad\|f\|_{\infty}\leqslant\max_{0\leqslant i<M}\frac{\max\{f_{i},f_{i+1}\}}{1-\tfrac{(Nh)^{2}}{8}}.

Taking
Nh\ll 1
makes the amplification factor close to
1
. In our runs we use a
300\times 150
grid in
(\beta,\theta)
, so with fixed spectral cutoff
N
we pick
M
large enough that
h=\frac{\pi}{2M}\ll N^{-1}
. In our computation, we take
N=600
for
\beta
and
N=300
for
\theta
, and choose
M=4096
for
\beta
and
M=2048
for
\theta
. All pointwise evaluations
f_{i}
are computed with interval arithmetic to enclose round-off.

7.3
Bounds for
\|\overline{v}\|_{\infty}
,
\|\nabla\overline{v}\|_{\infty}
, and
\|\nabla\overline{\phi}_{j}\|_{\infty}

Since
\overline{v}
and
\overline{\phi}_{j}
are trigonometric in
(\beta,\theta)
, we evaluate their point values and first derivatives analytically at the grid and then certify global suprema by (
7.2
). All evaluations carry interval arithmetic so that the reported
\!L^{\infty}
bounds are rigorous.

7.4
Far-field decay from the
(r,\beta)
mapping

The map
r=\tan\beta
sends
r\to\infty
as
\beta\uparrow\frac{\pi}{2}
, with

r=\tan\beta=\frac{1}{\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\beta}+O\!\left(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\beta\right).

Through the change of variable
\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)=\overline{U}/\cos\beta
and working in the
(\beta,\theta)
variable, we obtain an approximate solution
\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)
as follows:

\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)=\sum_{m,n}a_{m,n}\sin((2n{+}1)\beta)\sin(2m\theta).

Therefor, as
\beta\to\frac{\pi}{2}
(or
r\to\infty
), we have

\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)=A(\theta)+O\!\big((\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\beta)^{2}\big).

Passing back to the physical self-similar profile uses the smooth factor from the metric,

\overline{U}(\xi)=\widehat{U}(\beta,\theta)\cos\beta,

and using the asymptotic property
(\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\beta)^{2}\sim r^{-2}
, we conclude that

\overline{U}(\xi)=r^{-1}A(\theta)+O(r^{-3}),\qquad r=|\xi|\to\infty.

The
O(r^{-2})
term cancels by construction (consistent with transforming back to Cartesian coordinates without generating spurious singular modes). Differentiating
j
times yields

|\nabla^{\,j}\overline{U}(\xi)|\lesssim|\xi|^{-j-1},\qquad j\geq 0,

which matches the far-field hypothesis in Assumption
1
. This certifies that the numerically constructed
\overline{U}
has the required decay property.

7.5
Envelope for the largest eigenvalue of
Q(\beta,\theta)

The
3\times 3
symmetric positive definite matrix
Q(\beta,\theta)
has compact support in
r
. We approximate
Q
by a finite sine series in
\beta
for
0\leqslant r\leqslant R=32
, and set
Q(\beta,\theta)=0
whenever
r=\tan(\beta)\geqslant R
. In this way, both positivity and compact support are preserved, since the support is enforced exactly by truncating
Q
outside the ball
B_{0}(R)
. On each grid node
x_{j}=(\beta_{j},\theta_{j})
we compute closed-form eigenvalues of
Q(x_{j})
and take the largest,
\lambda_{\max}(Q(x_{j}))
, with interval arithmetic capturing floating-point uncertainty. To control the error of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\max}(Q(x_{j}))
within each subdomain
I_{j}
of size
h
, we use the formula

|\lambda^{Q,u}(Q(x))-\lambda^{Q,u}(Q(x_{j}))|\leqslant\|Q(x)-Q(x_{j})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant|x-x_{j}|\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant h\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{\infty}},

for any
x\in I_{j}
. Since
Q
is expressed in terms of trigonometric polynomials, we can compute
\nabla Q
analytically.
Applying the same envelope argument as (
7.2
) (componentwise to the trigonometric expansion of
\nabla Q
) yields a certified global bound on
\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{\infty}}
as follows

\|\nabla Q\|_{\infty}\leqslant\max_{0\leqslant i<M}\frac{\max\{|\nabla Q_{i}|,|\nabla Q_{i+1}|\}}{1-\tfrac{(Nh)^{2}}{8}},

where
|\nabla Q(x_{i})|
is understood as the maximum of each component in
\nabla Q(x_{i})
.
Thus, we obtain

\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}\ \leqslant\ \max_{j}\lambda_{\max}(Q(x_{j}))+Ch\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{\infty}},

for some computable absolute constant.
By taking
h
small enough, we can essentially bound
\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}
by
\max_{j}\lambda_{\max}(Q(x_{j}))
up to a very small error.

7.6
Quadrature for
\langle Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u}\rangle
and its error

In Section
4.2.2
and
6.3
, we need to compute the
Q
-inner product
\langle Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u}\rangle
or
\langle\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u}\rangle
, and estimate their errors. We will use
\langle Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u}\rangle
to illustrate how we estimate their errors. We first
write
Q(\beta,\theta)
as a truncated sine series in
\beta
(to enforce compact support in
r=\tan\beta
with
\,\mathrm{supp}\,Q\subset B_{0}(R)
,
R=32
) and trigonometric polynomials in
\theta
. The basis functions
\psi_{i}^{Q,u}
are Bessel/trigonometric in
(\beta,\theta)
; thus the
\theta
-integral is performed analytically. For the
\beta
-direction we use a
4
th-order composite rule, denoted by
\mathrm{Quad}
, on a uniform partition of
(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
with

h=\frac{\pi/2}{4096}.

If
I_{k}
denote the mesh subintervals in
\beta
and
h
the uniform mesh size in
\beta
,
the quadrature error for each inner product satisfies

\Bigl|\langle Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u}\rangle-\mathrm{Quad}(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u},Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})\Bigr|\ \leqslant\ \Bigl(\sum_{k}\bigl\|(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u})\cdot(Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})\bigr\|_{W^{4,\infty}(I_{k})}\Bigr)\,h^{5}\,R.

Validated range evaluation for
D_{m}=\partial_{\beta}^{m}F
.

On each
I_{k}=[\beta_{k},\beta_{k+1}]
we treat
\beta
as an interval and
evaluate the analytic expression for
D_{m}(\beta)=\partial_{\beta}^{m}\!\big[(Q\psi_{i})(Q\psi_{j})\big]

with interval arithmetic (preserving cancellations). Concretely,

r=\tan(\beta)
and the trigonometric polynomials
Q^{(p)}(\beta)
are computed as
intervals; the Bessel factors inside
\psi
are enclosed on
x=\kappa r\in\kappa\tan(I_{k})

using only a Taylor–series with a certified tail
. This yields a single interval
R_{m,k}
with

D_{m}(I_{k})\subseteq R_{m,k}
. If
\mathrm{width}(R_{m,k})
is not sufficiently small,
we bisect
I_{k}
and re-evaluate; on our mesh this terminates quickly. This procedure
provides rigorous and near-optimal bounds for
\|F\|_{W^{4,\infty}(I_{k})}

without pessimistic overestimation, since it avoids termwise
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
bounds
and preserves algebraic cancellations.

Remarks.

(i) This range-evaluation approach is fully rigorous and
preserves cancellations
because
we evaluate
D_{m}
as one expression; it avoids pessimistic constants that would arise from
bounding every product term separately by sup norms.
(ii) We do
not
use (
7.2
) for
D_{m}
or for any quantity involving Bessel
factors. For those, the supremum on
I_{k}
comes solely from direct interval range evaluation
(and, if needed, adaptive subdivision). Equation (
7.2
) is used only for
quantities that are trigonometric polynomials in
\beta
(e.g.,
Q
, its
\beta
–derivatives,
and other purely trigonometric terms).

Here
(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u})\cdot(Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})
is the scalar integrand after the analytic
\theta
–integration,
and
R
is the domain-size factor from the remaining integral. We compute explicit
formulas for the four
\beta
–derivatives of
(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u})\cdot(Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})
—equivalently,
for
\psi_{i}^{\!\top}Q^{2}\psi_{j}
—using the trigonometric/Bessel representations of

Q
and
\psi_{i}^{Q,u}
, then evaluate these with interval arithmetic on grid nodes.

Validated bound of the fourth
\beta
–derivative.

For clarity we first set

F(\beta,\theta):=\bigl(Q(\beta,\theta)\,\psi_{i}^{Q,u}(\beta,\theta)\bigr)\cdot\bigl(Q(\beta,\theta)\,\psi_{j}^{Q,u}(\beta,\theta)\bigr),

where “
\cdot
” is the Euclidean inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the
matrix-vector products are evaluated at
(\beta,\theta)
.
To simplify notation, throughout this subsection we
fix
\theta
and suppress it:
write
Q(\beta):=Q(\beta,\theta)
and
\psi_{i}(\beta):=\psi_{i}^{Q,u}(\beta,\theta)
.
All
\beta
–derivatives are taken with
\theta
held fixed. Define

H_{i}(\beta):=Q(\beta)\,\psi_{i}(\beta),\qquad F(\beta):=H_{i}(\beta)\cdot H_{j}(\beta)=(Q\psi_{i})(Q\psi_{j}).

All identities and bounds below are pointwise in
\theta
; once the
\beta
–estimates are
obtained, we perform the
\theta
–integration analytically.

1.

Analytic expression.

F^{(m)}(\beta)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\,H_{i}^{(k)}(\beta)\,H_{j}^{(m-k)}(\beta),\qquad H_{j}^{(k)}=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\,Q^{(l)}(\beta)\,\psi_{j}^{(k-l)}(\beta).

2.

Interval evaluation on
I_{k}
(Bessel via Taylor series only).

Set
\beta\in I_{k}
(interval),
r=\tan(\beta)
,
x=\kappa r
.
For
j_{\ell}(x)
use the entire–function series

J_{\nu}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!\,\Gamma(n+\nu+1)}\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}\Bigr)^{2n+\nu},\qquad j_{\ell}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2x}}\,J_{\ell+\tfrac{1}{2}}(x),

truncate at order
P
, and bound the tail on
x\in[x_{k}^{-},x_{k}^{+}]
by the ratio test:
if

\rho:=\frac{(x_{k}^{+}/2)^{2}}{(P+1)(P+\nu+1)}<1,

then the remainder satisfies

\Bigl|\sum_{n=P+1}^{\infty}\cdots\Bigr|\ \leqslant\ \frac{1}{1-\rho}\,\frac{(x_{k}^{+}/2)^{2(P+1)+\nu}}{(P+1)!\,\Gamma(P+\nu+2)}.

Evaluate the partial sum with directed rounding and inflate by this tail to obtain an
interval enclosure for
J_{\nu}(x)
, hence for
j_{\ell}(x)
. For
j_{\ell}^{\prime}(x)
, we use the exact recurrence

j_{\ell}^{\prime}(x)=j_{\ell-1}(x)-\frac{\ell+1}{x}j_{\ell}(x)
. This yields interval values for all Bessel terms appearing in
D_{m}
.

3.

Assemble
D_{m}
and adapt if needed.

Propagate all interval inputs (trig polynomials,
r
, Bessel enclosures) through the full

D_{m}
expression to obtain one interval
R_{m,k}\supset D_{m}(I_{k})
. If
\mathrm{width}(R_{m,k})

exceeds tolerance, bisect
I_{k}
and repeat (interval dependency decays rapidly with
|I_{k}|
).
Let
\{I_{k}^{\nu}\}
be the final partition with enclosures
R_{m,k}^{\nu}
.

4.

Sup norm on
I_{k}
.

\|D_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{k})}\ \leqslant\ \max_{\nu}\ \max\{|\inf R_{m,k}^{\nu}|,\,|\sup R_{m,k}^{\nu}|\},

\|(Q\psi_{i}^{Q,u})(Q\psi_{j}^{Q,u})\|_{W^{4,\infty}(I_{k})}=\max_{0\leqslant m\leq 4}\|D_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(I_{k})}.

Notes.

(1) Using the Taylor–series method keeps the pipeline simple and fully rigorous on each

x
-interval
[x_{k}^{-},x_{k}^{+}]
. We choose
P
minimal so that
\rho\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}

(and hence
1/(1-\rho)\leqslant 2
) and the series tail is below a prescribed tolerance.
In practice, a tail tolerance of
10^{-10}
is amply sufficient here.
(2) Near
x=0
we prefer the termwise–differentiated series for
j_{\ell}^{\prime}
(to avoid
1/x
); for moderate
x
either formula is valid.
(3) Because
Q\equiv 0
for
r\geqslant R
, we only evaluate on
x\in[0,\kappa R]
, so
P
stays modest in practice.

7.7
Finite-dimensional verification of the generalized eigenproblem

To construct our low-rank approximation operator, we need to solve the generalized eigenvalue problem. Let
A\psi=\lambda\,B\psi
be the discretized generalized eigenproblem and
A
and
B
are
1200\times 1200
matrices; here
B
is diagonal in our basis. With
m=25
retained eigenpairs
(\overline{\lambda}_{i},\overline{\psi}_{i})_{i=1}^{25}
we verify the spectral gap inequality

\Bigl(A-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{i}\,\overline{\psi}_{i}\overline{\psi}_{i}^{\!\top}\Bigr)\ \leqslant\ \overline{\lambda}_{m+1}\,B,

where bars denote numerically obtained quantities (with rigorously bounded errors). We then form the
25\times 25
matrices

G_{E}=\bigl(\langle\psi_{i},\psi_{j}\rangle_{E}\bigr)=I+\mathcal{E},\qquad G_{Q}=\bigl(\langle\psi_{i},Q\psi_{j}\rangle\bigr)=\mathrm{diag}(\overline{\lambda}_{i})+\varepsilon,

whose entrywise errors are controlled by the quadrature/pointwise bounds in Section
7.6
(contributing to
\epsilon_{4},\epsilon_{5}
). All matrix operations used to check (
7.7
) (condition numbers, residuals, inertia) are performed with interval arithmetic, so conclusions are certified. These verifications feed into the constants
K_{6},K_{7}
used in Section
4.2.2
.
