The research was in part supported by the NSF grants DMS-2205590 and DMS-2512878. We would like to thank Jiajie Chen, Jia Hao, Xuefeng Liu, Xiang Qin, and Vladimir Sverak for a number of stimulating discussions.

Appendix A
Sharp Sobolev Constant

We provide a sharp Sobolev constant in the following lemma.

Lemma 6

(Sobolev embedding)

For any vector function
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have:

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\leqslant K_{4}(\|u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}})^{2},\quad K_{4}:=\frac{3^{\frac{3}{4}}}{2^{3}\pi}\approx 0.0907.

Proof.

By
[
45
]
, we know that for each
u_{i}
,

\|u_{i}\|_{L^{6}}\leqslant K_{s}\|\nabla u_{i}\|_{L^{2}},\quad K_{s}:=\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{3^{\frac{1}{2}}\pi^{\frac{2}{3}}}.

Now we consider the vector function
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})
:

\|u\|_{L^{6}}=\|(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|u_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{6}}\leqslant K_{s}\|\nabla(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|u_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}.

By Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

|\nabla(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|u_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}|=\frac{|\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}\nabla u_{i}|}{(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|u_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\leqslant(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|\nabla u_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},

and we get

\|u\|_{L^{6}}\leqslant K_{s}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}.

It follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\|u\|_{L^{4}}\leqslant\|u\|^{\frac{1}{4}}_{L^{2}}\|u\|^{\frac{3}{4}}_{L^{6}}\leqslant K_{s}^{\frac{3}{4}}\|u\|^{\frac{1}{4}}_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|^{\frac{3}{4}}_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{3^{\frac{3}{4}}}{4}K_{s}^{\frac{3}{4}}(\|u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}),

and we conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Estimates of Finite Rank Approximation

We prove Lemmas
1
and
2
on estimates of quasi-orthonormal projection, and then prove Lemma
3
on estimates of finite-rank approximation. We recall that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}
is the energy inner product induced by the norm
\|u\|_{E}^{2}=\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\eta_{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, the diagonal matrix for approximate eigenvalues is
\overline{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\overline{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\overline{\lambda}_{m})
, and the Gram matrix is
G_{Q}=(\langle\psi_{i},Q\psi_{j}\rangle_{\Omega})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}
.

Proof of Lemma
1
.

We assume the representation

f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}d_{j}\overline{\psi}_{j}=\overline{\Psi}D\,,

where
D:=(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m})^{T}
, and
\overline{\Psi}:=(\overline{\psi}_{1},\cdots,\overline{\psi}_{m})
.
By definition, we have

\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=|(G_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}D|^{2},\quad\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}=|\overline{\Lambda}^{\frac{-1}{2}}G_{Q}D|^{2}.

(B.1)

Hence we have

|\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2}|\leqslant\|\mathbb{1}_{m}-(G_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\Lambda}^{-1}(G_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

This is the “Gram mismatch” constant
\varepsilon_{3}
defined in (
4.14
) in Section
4
.
We thus conclude the proof of (
4.13
)
using the definition of projections.∎

Proof of Lemma
2
.

Similar to the proof above, we assume the representation
f=\overline{\Psi}B
. By definition of
r^{e}_{j}
and orthogonality of
u
to
V_{\operatorname{lg}}
in the
Q
-inner product, we have

\langle f,u\rangle_{E}=-B^{T}\overline{\Lambda}^{-1}\langle R^{e},u\rangle_{E},\quad\langle f,f\rangle_{E}=B^{T}G_{E}B,

and we thus conclude the proof by Cauchy-Schwarz.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3
.

We decompose
V_{N}
into
V_{\operatorname{lg}}=\operatorname{span}\{\overline{\psi}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
and its orthogonal complement in the
Q
-inner product
V_{\operatorname{sm}}
. For any
f
, due to the orthogonality of
V_{\operatorname{sm}}
and
V_{\operatorname{lg}}
, we can write an orthogonal decomposition in the
Q
-inner product as

f=f^{Q}_{\operatorname{or}}+f^{Q}=f^{Q}_{\operatorname{or}}+f^{Q}_{\operatorname{sm}}+f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}},

where
f^{Q}_{\operatorname{or}}\perp V_{N}
,
f^{Q}_{\operatorname{sm}}\in V_{\operatorname{sm}}
,
f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}}\in V_{\operatorname{lg}}
hence they are mutually orthogonal. In particular
f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}}
coincides with the orthogonal projection
f^{Q,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}
.

Step 1: Quasi-orthogonal projection in
E
.

We invoke another orthogonal decomposition in the
E
-inner product as

f=f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}+f^{E}=f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}+f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}}+f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}},

where
f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}\perp V_{N}
,
f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}}\in V_{\operatorname{sm}}
,
f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}\in V_{\operatorname{lg}}
. Notice that
f-f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}
is the
E
-projection of
f
onto the space
V_{N}
, which coincides with
I_{N}f
. Thus
f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}=f-I_{N}f
, and from (
4.15
) we have

\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant K_{5}\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}\|_{{E}}^{2}.

(B.2)

V_{\operatorname{sm}}
and
V_{\operatorname{lg}}
are not orthogonal in the
E
-inner product, so
f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}
does not coincide with
f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}
, the orthogonal projection of
f^{E}
in the
E
-inner product in
V_{\operatorname{lg}}
, but we show that they are close. In fact

0=\langle f^{E}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}},f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\rangle_{E}=\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|^{2}_{E}+\langle f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}},f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\rangle_{E}.

As a consequence of Lemma
2
, we have

\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E}\leqslant\varepsilon_{4}\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}}\|_{E}\leqslant\varepsilon_{4}\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E}+\varepsilon_{4}\|f^{E}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E},

and we solve that

\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E}\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon_{4}}{1-\varepsilon_{4}}\|f^{E}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E},\quad\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}}\|_{E}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{4}}\|f^{E}-f^{E,\perp}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{E}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_{4}}\|f^{E}\|_{E}.

(B.3)

Step 2: Conclusion of the estimate.

Now
for
\mathcal{P}f=f
, the difference consists of three parts thanks to the orthogonal decomposition,

\displaystyle\langle-(L_{2}-L_{\operatorname{ap}})f,f\rangle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3},\quad I_{1}:=\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}(f-f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}})\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle I_{2}:=\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}^{2},\quad I_{3}:=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}\langle f,Q\overline{\psi}_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}\langle f,r_{j}\rangle_{\Omega}.

By
Q
-orthogonality, we have the estimate using (
B.2
), (
4.16
), and (
B.3
) that

\displaystyle

\displaystyle|I_{1}|\leqslant\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}(f-f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}}+f^{Q}_{\operatorname{lg}}-f^{E}_{\operatorname{lg}})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}(f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}+f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant(1+\mu)\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+(1+\frac{1}{\mu})\|Q^{\frac{1}{2}}f^{E}_{\operatorname{sm}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(B.4)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+\mu)K_{5}\|f^{E}_{\operatorname{or}}\|_{E}^{2}+(1+\frac{1}{\mu})\frac{K_{6}}{(1-\varepsilon_{4})^{2}}\|f^{E}\|_{E}^{2}=(K_{5}+\frac{K_{6}}{(1-\varepsilon_{4})^{2}})\|f\|_{E}^{2},

where
\mu=\frac{K_{6}}{K_{5}(1-\varepsilon_{4})^{2}}
is chosen to balance the coefficients of the two terms using
E
-orthogonality, leveraging the weighted AM-GM inequality.

For
I_{3}
, we expand it into double integrals and use Cauchy-Schwarz to get

\displaystyle|I_{3}|

\displaystyle=|\iint_{\Omega}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f(x)f(y)\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}(x)r_{j}(y)dxdy|

(B.5)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\iint_{\Omega}f^{2}(x)f^{2}(y)dxdy}\sqrt{\iint_{\Omega}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\overline{\lambda}_{j}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{j}(x)r_{j}(y)\overline{\lambda}_{i}^{-1}Q\overline{\psi}_{i}(x)r_{i}(y)dxdy}=K_{7}\|f\|_{{L^{2}}(\Omega)}^{2}.

Combining the estimates (
B.4
) and (
B.5
) with (
4.13
) on
I_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle(L_{2}-L_{\operatorname{ap}})f,f\rangle|\leqslant(K_{5}+\frac{K_{6}}{(1-\varepsilon_{4})^{2}})\|f\|_{E}^{2}+(\varepsilon_{3}\|\lambda_{\max}(Q)\|_{L^{\infty}}+K_{7})\|f\|_{\Omega}^{2}.

Here
\varepsilon_{3}
is the ”Gram mismatch” constant from Lemma
1
, and
K_{5},\varepsilon_{4},K_{6},K_{7}
are the constants from Section
4
.
We thus conclude the proof of the lemma.
∎

Appendix C
Derivation of Gradient

We derive the gradient of
\lambda(\widetilde{U})
by differentiating the
eigenvalue equation with respect to
\widetilde{U}
:

\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}gv^{r}(\widetilde{U})+\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g=\frac{\delta\lambda(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}gv^{r}(\widetilde{U})+\lambda(\widetilde{U})\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g,

where
g
represents the descent direction. Taking the inner product with
v^{l}(\widetilde{U})
, we have:

\left\langle v^{l},\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}gv^{r}\right\rangle+\left\langle v^{l},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g\right\rangle=\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle\frac{\delta\lambda(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g+\lambda(\widetilde{U})\left\langle v^{l},\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g\right\rangle.

Since

\left\langle v^{l},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})^{\ast}v^{l},\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g\right\rangle=\lambda(\widetilde{U})\left\langle v^{l},\frac{\delta v^{r}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g\right\rangle,

we simplify the expression by assuming
\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle\neq 0
:

\frac{\delta\lambda(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g=\frac{\left\langle v^{l},\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}gv^{r}\right\rangle}{\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle}.

Additionally, we note that

\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}gv^{r}=\Pi(g\cdot\nabla v^{r}+v^{r}\cdot\nabla g).

It follows via an integration by parts that

\frac{\delta\lambda(\widetilde{U})}{\delta\widetilde{U}}g=\frac{\langle g\cdot\nabla v^{r}+v^{r}\cdot\nabla g,v^{l}\rangle}{\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle}=\frac{\langle g\cdot\nabla v^{r},v^{l}\rangle-\langle v^{r}\cdot\nabla v^{l},g\rangle}{\langle v^{l},v^{r}\rangle}.

We thus conclude the proof of (
5.1
).

Appendix D
Expression of Operators in Spherical
Coordinates

For axisymmetric scalar
f
, our velocity vector
\widetilde{U}
, and pressure
\widetilde{P}
, we know we have the following operators in spherical coordinates:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\partial_{r}f)+\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}f),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{r}\widetilde{U}_{r}&\partial_{r}\widetilde{U}_{\theta}&\partial_{r}\widetilde{U}_{\phi}\\
\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{r}-\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\theta}}{r}&\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{\theta}+\frac{\widetilde{U}_{r}}{r}&\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{\phi}\\
-\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\phi}}{r}&-\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\phi}\cot\theta}{r}&\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\theta}\cot\theta}{r}+\frac{\widetilde{U}_{r}}{r}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\Delta\widetilde{U}_{r}-\frac{2\widetilde{U}_{r}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{\theta}}{r^{2}}-\frac{2\widetilde{U}_{\theta}\cot\theta}{r^{2}},\Delta\widetilde{U}_{\theta}+\frac{2\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{U}_{r}}{r^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\theta}}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta},\Delta\widetilde{U}_{\phi}-\frac{\widetilde{U}_{\phi}}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\right),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{P}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{r}\widetilde{P},\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}\widetilde{P},0\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widetilde{U}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}(r^{2}\widetilde{U}_{r})+\frac{1}{r\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\widetilde{U}_{\theta}\sin\theta).

And in variables of Section
5.5
, we compute:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\partial}_{\beta}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sin\beta(\cos\beta\partial_{\beta}f-\sin\beta f),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos^{2}\beta}{\sin\beta}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widetilde{\partial}_{\beta}\widehat{U}_{r}&\widetilde{\partial}_{\beta}\widehat{U}_{\theta}&\widetilde{\partial}_{\beta}\widehat{U}_{\phi}\\
\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}_{r}-\widehat{U}_{\theta}&\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}_{\theta}+\widehat{U}_{r}&\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}_{\phi}\\
-\widehat{U}_{\phi}&-\widehat{U}_{\phi}\cot\theta&\widehat{U}_{\theta}\cot\theta+\widehat{U}_{r}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\cot^{2}\beta\cos\beta\left(\cot\theta\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}+\frac{1}{4}(2\sin 4\beta\partial_{\beta}\widehat{U}+\sin^{2}2\beta(\partial_{\beta\beta}\widehat{U}-3\widehat{U}))+\partial_{\theta\theta}\widehat{U}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\cot^{2}\beta\cos\beta\left(-2\widehat{U}_{r}-2(\cot\theta\widehat{U}_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}_{\theta}),2\partial_{\theta}\widehat{U}_{r}-\frac{\widehat{U}_{\theta}}{\sin^{2}\theta},-\frac{\widehat{U}_{\phi}}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{P}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos^{2}\beta}{\sin\beta}(\widetilde{\partial}_{\beta}\widehat{P},\partial_{\theta}\widehat{P},0),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\operatorname{div}}_{\operatorname{sph}}\widehat{U}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos^{2}\beta}{\sin\beta}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\beta\partial_{\beta}\widehat{U}_{r}+\frac{1}{2}(3+\cos 2\beta)\widehat{U}_{r}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{U}_{\theta}\sin\theta)\right).

Appendix E
Spherical Harmonics

For a scalar function
p
, we can expand
p
with scalar spherical harmonics:

p(r,\omega)=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}p_{lm}(r)S_{lm}(\omega),\quad r\geqslant 0,\quad\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}.

where
S_{lm}(\omega)
is the spherical harmonics. For a vector field
u
,
we can also expand
u
with the vector spherical harmonics:

u(r,\omega)=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}(u_{lm}^{Y}(r)Y_{lm}(\omega)+u_{lm}^{X}(r)X_{lm}(\omega)+u_{lm}^{Z}(r)Z_{lm}(\omega)),

where

Y_{lm}(\omega)=S_{lm}(\omega)\vec{e}_{r},\quad X_{l}(\omega)=\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}S_{lm}(\omega),\quad Z_{lm}(\omega)=\vec{e}_{r}\times\nabla_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}S_{lm}(\omega).

Since we only consider axisymmetric functions, we choose
m=0
and
\omega

reduce to
\theta
:

Y_{l}(\theta)=S_{l}(\theta)\vec{e}_{r},\quad X_{l}(\theta)=S_{l}^{\prime}(\theta)\vec{e}_{\theta},\quad Z_{l}(\theta)=\vec{e}_{\phi}S_{l}^{\prime}(\theta),\quad S_{l}(\theta)=P_{l}(\cos\theta),

where
P_{l}
denotes the Legendre polynomial. Here we drop the subscript
m=0
for simplicity.

We have the reduced expansions

p(r,\theta)=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}p_{l}(r)S_{l}(\theta),\quad u(r,\theta)=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}(u_{l}^{Y}(r)Y_{l}(\theta)+u_{l}^{X}(r)X_{l}(\theta)+u_{l}^{Z}(r)Z_{l}(\theta)).

We note that
X_{l},Y_{m},Z_{n}
are orthogonal with one another for all
subscripts
l,m,n
. Furthermore, they are normalized in the
L^{2}
inner product:

\int_{0}^{\pi}Y_{l}(\theta)Y_{m}(\theta)\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{2\delta_{lm}}{2l+1},\quad\int_{0}^{\pi}X_{l}(\theta)X_{m}(\theta)\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta=\int_{0}^{\pi}Z_{l}(\theta)Z_{m}(\theta)\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{2l(l+1)\delta_{lm}}{2l+1}.

The gradients of
p
and
u
have the following forms, by Appendix
D
and the eigenvector properties of spherical harmonics and Legendre polynomials:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\left(p_{l}^{\prime}(r)Y_{l}(\theta)+\frac{1}{r}p_{l}(r)X_{l}(\theta)\right),

(E.1)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\left(\Delta_{r}p_{l}(r)-\frac{\mu_{l}}{r^{2}}p_{l}(r)\right)S_{l}(\theta),

(E.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Y}+\frac{2}{r}u_{l}^{Y}-\frac{\mu_{l}}{r}u_{l}^{X}\right)S_{l},

(E.3)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}(\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{Y}Y_{l}+\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{X}X_{l}+\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{Z}Z_{l})

(E.4)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}((2\mu_{l}u_{l}^{X}-(\mu_{l}+2)u_{l}^{Y})Y_{l}+(2u_{l}^{Y}-\mu_{l}u_{l}^{X})X_{l}-\mu_{l}u_{l}^{Z}Z_{l}),

where we define

\mu_{l}=l(l+1),\quad\Delta_{r}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}r^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}.

Appendix F
Eigenfunction in the Ball

In this section, we aim to prove Lemma
4
and Lemma

5
.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Step 1: Variational formulation.

The problem (
6.2
) can be equivalently written as

\lambda_{n}^{s}=\sup_{\dim(V_{n})=n-1}\inf_{\|u\|^{2}_{\Omega}=1,u\in V,u\bot V_{n}}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{\Omega}.

We apply a variational approach to reformulate it as an eigenvalue problem.
Consider the Lagrangian functional with multiplier
p
and
\lambda
:

\mathfrak{L}(u,p,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{\Omega}-\frac{\lambda}{2}(\|u\|^{2}_{\Omega}-1)-\langle p,\operatorname{div}u\rangle_{\Omega}.

Taking variations gives

\delta\mathfrak{L}=\langle\nabla u,\nabla\delta u\rangle_{\Omega}-\lambda\langle u,\delta u\rangle_{\Omega}-\langle p,\operatorname{div}\delta u\rangle_{\Omega}=\langle-\Delta u+\nabla p-\lambda u,\delta u\rangle_{\Omega}+\langle\partial_{\vec{n}}u^{b}-p^{b}\vec{n},\delta u\rangle_{\partial\Omega},

where we integrated by parts in the second step. The corresponding
eigenvalue problem reads:

-\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}u^{b}+\nabla_{\operatorname{sph}}p^{b}=\lambda^{b}u^{b},\quad\operatorname{div}_{\operatorname{sph}}u^{b}=0,

(F.1)

subject to the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition on
\partial\Omega

with the unit vector
\vec{n}
:

\partial_{\vec{n}}u^{b}-p^{b}\vec{n}=0.

Here
u
is constrained to the divergence-free subspace of axisymmetric
vector fields.

Step 2: Expansion in Spherical Harmonics.

We expand
u^{b}
and
p^{b}
in vector spherical harmonics as follows:

u^{b}=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}(u_{l}^{Y}(r)Y_{l}(\theta)+u_{l}^{X}(r)X_{l}(\theta)+u_{l}^{Z}(r)Z_{l}(\theta)),\quad p^{b}=\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}p_{l}(r)S_{l}(\theta).

From (
F.1
), taking the divergence yields
\Delta_{\operatorname{sph}}p=0
. Thanks to (
E.2
), we obtain for each
l
,

\Delta_{r}p_{l}(r)-\frac{\mu_{l}}{r^{2}}p_{l}(r)=0.

The regular solution is
p_{l}(r)=C_{l}r^{l}
, while the singular branch
r^{-l-1}
is discarded. Substituting into (
E.1
)(
E.4
),
the eigenvalue problem becomes

\displaystyle-\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{Y}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}(2\mu_{l}u_{l}^{X}-(\mu_{l}+2)u_{l}^{Y})+lC_{l}r^{l-1}=\lambda u_{l}^{Y},

(F.2)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{X}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}(2u_{l}^{Y}-\mu_{l}u_{l}^{X})+C_{l}r^{l-1}=\lambda u_{l}^{X},\quad-\Delta_{r}u_{l}^{Z}+\frac{\mu_{l}}{r^{2}}u_{l}^{Z}=\lambda u_{l}^{Z}.

Using (
E.3
), the divergence-free condition reduces to

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Y}+\frac{2}{r}u_{l}^{Y}-\frac{\mu_{l}}{r}u_{l}^{X}=0.

(F.3)

And the boundary condition is

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{X}(1)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Y}(1)-p_{l}(1)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Z}(1)=0.

Hence the system decouples into an eigenvalue problem for
u_{l}^{Z}
and an
eigenvalue system for
u_{l}^{Y}
and
u_{l}^{X}
.

Step 3: Solve
u_{l}^{Y}
and
u_{l}^{X}
.

We first solve for
u_{l}^{Y}
and
u_{l}^{X}
. When
l=0
, the divergence-free
condition (
F.3
) forces
u^{Y}_{l}=0
. Thus, we only consider

l\geqslant 1
. Plugging (
F.3
) into (
F.2
), we
obtain:

-\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}r^{2}}u_{l}^{Y}-\frac{4}{r}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Y}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}(l(l+1)-2)u_{l}^{Y}+lC_{l}r^{l-1}=\lambda u_{l}^{Y}.

(F.4)

A particular solution for this equation is

u_{l}^{Y}=lA_{l}r^{l-1},\quad C_{l}=\lambda A_{l}.

The homogeneous equation is related to the Bessel ODE. Its solution is:

u_{l}^{Y}=B_{l}\tilde{j}_{l}(kr),\quad\lambda=k^{2},\quad\tilde{j}_{l}(r):=\frac{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(r)}{r^{\frac{3}{2}}},

where
J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}
is the first kind of Bessel function. Hence the
general solution reads

u_{l}^{Y}=B_{l}\tilde{j}_{l}(kr)+lA_{l}r^{l-1}.

It follows that

u_{l}^{X}=\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}\left(r\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}+2\right)\tilde{j}_{l}(kr)+A_{l}r^{l-1}=\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}(rk\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(kr)+2\tilde{j}_{l}(kr))+A_{l}r^{l-1}.

It is not hard to check that the pair indeed solves (
F.2
).

Finally, we use the boundary condition to decide
k
. The boundary
condition for
u_{l}^{Y}
is:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Y}(1)-p_{l}(1)=B_{l}k\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(k)-(k^{2}-l(l-1))A_{l}=0.

(F.5)

For
u_{l}^{X}
, we simplify the condition
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{X}(1)=0
into

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{X}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}\left(r\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}r^{2}}+3\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\right)\tilde{j}_{l}(kr)|_{r=1}+(l-1)A_{l}

\displaystyle=-\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}((k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(k)+k\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(k))+(l-1)A_{l},

where we use that
\tilde{j}_{l}(kr)
is a homogeneous solution to
(
F.4
). By (
F.5
), this becomes

-B_{l}(k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(k)-(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))A_{l}=0.

To ensure nontrivial solutions with
A_{l}
and
B_{l}
, we require that
(
6.4
) holds:

(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))k\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(k)+(k^{2}-l(l-1))(k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(k)=0.

(F.6)

This condition determines all eigenvalues
\lambda=k^{2}
and the
corresponding coefficients
A_{l}
and
B_{l}
are uniquely determined up to
normalization, when
k>0
:

A_{l}=-(k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))\tilde{j}_{l}(k),\quad B_{l}=k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2).

Step 4:
k=0
case.

When
k=0
and
l>1
, we have
A_{l}=0
. In this case, both
u^{X}_{l}
and

u^{Y}_{l}
vanish, since

u_{l}^{X}=\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}\left(r\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}+2\right)\tilde{j}_{l}(kr),\quad u_{l}^{Y}=B_{l}\tilde{j}_{l}(kr).

When
k=0
and
l=1
,
u^{X}_{l}
and
u^{Y}_{l}
reduce to the constant
functions:

u_{l}^{X}=u_{l}^{Y}=1.

Since these solutions are nontrivial, we conclude that
\lambda_{0}^{s}=0
is
also an eigenvalue with the corresponding eigenvector:

\psi_{0}^{s}=(\cos\theta,-\sin\theta,0).

Step 5: Solve
u_{l}^{Z}
.

We now turn to the component
u_{l}^{Z}
. The corresponding eigenpairs are given
explicitly by

u_{l}^{Z}=\frac{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(kr)}{\sqrt{r}},\quad\lambda=k^{2}.

The boundary condition yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}u_{l}^{Z}(1)=kJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(k)-\frac{1}{2}J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)=0,

(F.7)

which characterizes all admissible values of
k
, and therefore determines
the corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda=k^{2}
. In particular,
k=0
is
excluded since
u_{l}^{Z}
vanishes, so
\lambda=0
is not an eigenvalue in
this case.

Summary

In summary, let
\alpha^{Y}_{ml}
be the
m
-th nonzero root of equation
(
6.4
) and
\alpha^{Z}_{ml}
be the
m
-th root of equation
(
6.3
). Then the eigenpairs are:

\lambda_{ml}^{Y}=(\alpha^{Y}_{ml})^{2},\quad A_{ml}=-((\alpha^{Y}_{ml})^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}),\quad B_{ml}=(\alpha^{Y}_{ml})^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2),

\psi_{ml}=(B_{ml}\tilde{j}_{l}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r)+lA_{ml}r^{l-1})Y_{l}(\theta)+\left(\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}}(r\alpha^{Y}_{ml}\tilde{j}_{l}^{\prime}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r)+2\tilde{j}_{l}(\alpha^{Y}_{ml}r))+A_{l}r^{l-1}\right)X_{l}(\theta),

and

\lambda_{ml}^{Z}=(\alpha^{Z}_{ml})^{2},\quad\psi_{ml}^{Z}=\frac{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(\alpha^{Z}_{ml}r)}{\sqrt{r}}Z_{l}(\theta).

Plus a zero eigenmode
(\lambda_{0},\psi_{0})=(0,(\cos\theta,-\sin\theta,0))
.
∎

Remark 10

In practice, it is often inconvenient to evaluate the derivative
J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(r)
directly. Instead, we make use of the recurrence identity

J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(r)=J_{l-\frac{1}{2}}(r)-\frac{l+\frac{1}{2}}{r}J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(r).

Substituting this relation, equation (
6.4
) can be rewritten as:

k(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))J_{l-\frac{1}{2}}(k)+(k^{4}-l(2l+1)k^{2}+l(l-1)(l+2)(2l+1))J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)=0,

while equation (
6.3
) becomes:

kJ_{l-\frac{1}{2}}(k)-(l+1)J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)=0.

Similarly, the expression for
u_{l}^{X}
takes the form:

u_{l}^{X}=\frac{B_{l}}{\mu_{l}\sqrt{r}}\left(\alpha^{Y}_{l}J_{l-\frac{1}{2}}(kr)-\frac{lJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(kr)}{r}\right)+A_{l}r^{l-1}.

Proof of Lemma
5
.

First we consider the equation (
6.4
). Since
J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(\gamma)\neq 0
for
l\geqslant 2
, it can be written in the form:

\frac{kJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(k)}{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)}-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{(k^{2}-l(l-1))(k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))}{(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))}=0.

If
l=1
, the equation becomes:

\frac{kJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(k)}{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)}-\frac{3}{2}+k^{2}=0.

Define:

R_{l}(k)=\frac{kJ_{l+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(k)}{J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(k)},\quad T_{l}(k)=\frac{(k^{2}-l(l-1))(k^{2}-(l-1)(l+2))}{(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))},\quad F_{l}(k)=R_{l}(k)-\frac{3}{2}+T_{l}(k).

We observe that
F_{l}(k)
is continuous except at
\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}
(
j\geqslant 1
). If
\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}
is a zero of
J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(x)
, then

\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}+}R_{l}(x)=+\infty,\quad\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}-}R_{l}(x)=-\infty,

and
T_{l}(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j})
is finite. If
\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}
is

\gamma
, we have

\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}+}T_{l}(x)=+\infty,\quad\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}-}T_{l}(x)=-\infty,

and
R_{l}(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j})
is finite. Therefore, for every

\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}
, in both cases we obtain we obtain

\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}+}F_{l}(x)=+\infty,\quad\lim_{x\rightarrow\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}-}F_{l}(x)=-\infty.

At
\tilde{\beta}_{l,0}=0
, Taylor expansion using the power series
expansion

J_{l+\frac{1}{2}}(x)=\frac{(x/2)^{l+\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(l+\frac{3}{2})}(1-\frac{x^{2}}{4(l+\frac{3}{2})}+O(x^{4})),

shows that

F_{l}(k)=\frac{3(1+l)^{2}}{l(2+l)(3+2l)}k^{2}+o(k^{2}).

Therefore,
F_{l}(k)>0
for sufficiently small
k
. We conclude that
F_{l}(k)
has at least one root on every interval
(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j},\tilde{\beta}_{l,j+1})
with
j\geqslant 0
. The case
l=1
follows by
a similar argument.

Next we derive a lower bound for the first root, and prove that assuming two
zeros in the same interval leads to a contradiction. Since
F_{l}(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j}+)=\infty
and
F_{l}(\tilde{\beta}_{l+1,j}-)=-\infty
, we denote
k_{2}>k_{1}
as the first two roots in

(\tilde{\beta}_{l,j},\tilde{\beta}_{l,j+1})
, which implies that
F_{l}^{\prime}(k_{1})\leqslant 0
.

Straightforward calculation shows that

R_{l}^{\prime}(k)=-\frac{R_{l}(k)^{2}+k^{2}-\left(l+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}{k},\quad T^{\prime}_{l}(k)=2k\left(1-\frac{l(l-1)^{3}(l+1)(l+2)}{(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))^{2}}\right).

When
F_{l}(k)=0
, we have
R_{l}(k)=\frac{3}{2}-T_{l}(k)
. Thus
F_{l}^{\prime}

simplifies into:

F_{l}^{\prime}(k)=-\frac{\left(\frac{3}{2}-T_{l}(k)\right)^{2}+k^{2}-\left(l+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}}{k}+T^{\prime}_{l}(k)=-\frac{kQ_{l}(k^{2})}{(k^{2}-l(l-1)(l+2))^{2}},

where

Q_{l}(s)=s^{3}-4l^{2}s^{2}+l(l-1)(6l^{2}+11l+7)s-3l(l-1)^{2}(l+1)^{2}(l+2).

It is easy to verify that for
l\geqslant 2
, we have by quadratic
equations that

Q_{l}^{\prime}(s)=3s^{2}-8l^{2}s+l(l-1)(3l+7)(2l+1)>0.

Therefore,
Q_{l}(s)
has a unique root
s_{l}>0
such that
Q_{l}(s)<0
for

s<s_{l}
, and
Q_{l}(s)
is positive and strictly increasing for
s>s_{l}
.

For
l=1
, we have
Q_{1}(s)=s^{3}-4s^{2}
, which satisfies the same
property.

Since
F_{l}^{\prime}(k_{1})\leqslant 0
, it follows that

k_{1}\geqslant\sqrt{s_{l}},

and that
0\leqslant Q_{l}(k_{1}^{2})<Q_{l}(k_{2}^{2})
. Hence, we obtain
F_{l}^{\prime}(k_{2})<0
. Thus
F_{l}
cannot cross the axis at
k_{1}
, and it has to hold
that
F_{l}(k_{1})=F_{l}^{\prime}(k_{1})=0
. We verify that
F_{l}(\sqrt{s_{l}})\neq 0
.
Hence, we reach a contradiction and conclude the proof of the lemma.

By a similar but simpler argument, the equation (
6.3
) has
exactly one root in every interval
(\beta_{l,j},\beta_{l,j+1})
for
all
l\geqslant 1,j\geqslant 0
. Moreover the first root satisfies the
lower bound

\beta_{l,1}\geqslant\sqrt{l(l+1)},

provided that
\sqrt{l(l+1)}
is not a root.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of Propositions
3
and
4

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Recall that we will use induction to prove
U_{\mathrm{near}}\in H^{\infty}
. As in the proof sketch, we have the equation for
U_{\mathrm{near}}

-\tfrac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{near}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{near}}+\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta U_{\mathrm{near}}=F,\quad\nabla\cdot U_{\mathrm{near}}=0,

(G.1)

with the right-hand side

F:=\tfrac{1}{2}U_{\mathrm{far}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\mathrm{far}}+\Delta U_{\mathrm{far}},\quad\nabla^{k}F(\xi)=O(|\xi|^{-3-k})\ \ (k\geqslant 0).

(G.2)

Fix a standard mollifier
\rho_{\epsilon}
with
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and denote

u^{\epsilon}=\rho_{\epsilon}*U_{\operatorname{near}},\quad F^{\epsilon}=\rho_{\epsilon}*F.

(G.3)

We know that
u^{\epsilon}\in H^{\infty}
is divergence-free, and satisfies the pointwise equation

-\frac{1}{2}u^{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla u^{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}[\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}}+\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})-\Delta u^{\epsilon}=F^{\epsilon},

(G.4)

where the commutator is computed as

[\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}}=\rho_{\epsilon}*(\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}})-\xi\cdot\nabla(\rho_{\epsilon}*U_{\operatorname{near}})=-\int\rho_{\epsilon}(y)y\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}(\xi-y)dy.

The commutator vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
.
More precisely, we have the estimate of its
H^{k-1}
-seminorm by Young’s inequality as

\|\nabla^{k-1}[\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\rho_{\epsilon}(y)y\|_{L^{1}}\|\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\epsilon\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}}<\|U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{H^{k}},\quad\forall k>0.

(G.5)

We recall two basic facts that will be used repeatedly: the boundedness of Leray projection in
L^{2}
, and that
mollification is bounded in
H^{k}
via
Young’s inequality: for any integer
k\geqslant 0
,

\|\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}=\|\rho_{\epsilon}*\nabla^{k}U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\rho_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}}\|\nabla^{k}U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}}=\|\nabla^{k}U_{\mathrm{near}}\|_{L^{2}}.

(G.6)

Next, we will obtain an estimate of the
H^{k-1}
-seminorm of the nonlinear term. Recall
U_{\operatorname{far}}\in W^{\infty,\infty}
and
\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}}\in H^{\infty}
. A canonical term in the expansion of
\nabla^{k-1}(\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}))
has the form
\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\nabla^{i}U_{\operatorname{s}}\otimes\nabla^{k-i}U_{\operatorname{t}})
, where
\operatorname{s},\operatorname{t}\in\{\operatorname{far},\operatorname{near}\}
,
0\leqslant i<k
. By Young’s inequality, all of the terms where at least one of
\operatorname{s},\operatorname{t}
is
\operatorname{far}
have
L^{2}
-norm bounded by
1+\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}
. When
\operatorname{s},\operatorname{t}=\operatorname{near}
and
i>0
, we have by Young’s inequality, Holder’s inequality, and Sobolev embedding that

\|\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\nabla^{i}U_{\operatorname{s}}\otimes\nabla^{k-i}U_{\operatorname{t}})\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|\nabla^{i}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla^{k-i}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}.

(G.7)

Finally, when
\operatorname{s},\operatorname{t}=\operatorname{near}
and
i=0
, we have, via a commutator representation, that

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\nabla^{i}U_{\operatorname{s}}\otimes\nabla^{k-i}U_{\operatorname{t}})\|_{L^{2}}=\|\Pi(u^{\epsilon}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}+\int\rho_{\epsilon}(y)(U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-y)-u^{\epsilon}(x))\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-y)dy)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}+(\iint\rho_{\epsilon}(y)(U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-y)-u^{\epsilon}(x))^{2}dydx)^{1/2}(\iint\rho_{\epsilon}(y)(\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-y))^{2}dydx)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}+(\frac{1}{2}\iiint\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\rho_{\epsilon}(z)(U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-y)-U_{\operatorname{near}}(x-z))^{2}dydzdx)^{1/2}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle(\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}+(\frac{1}{2}\iint\rho_{\epsilon}(y)\rho_{\epsilon}(z)(y-z)^{2}dydz)^{1/2}\|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}})\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}\lesssim\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}+\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{2},

where we use the Cauchy-Schwarz and (
G.6
) in the first inequality, the variance identity
E_{y}(y-E_{y})^{2}=1/2E_{y,z}(y-z)^{2}
in the second equality, and the difference-quotient bound
\|u(\cdot+h)-u(\cdot)\|\leqslant|h|\|\nabla u\|
(via the Lebiniz’s rule and Jensen’s inequality) in the second inequality.

Combined with (
G.7
), we already collect a good nonlinear estimate for
k>1
by Sobolev embedding and Young’s inequality:

\|\nabla^{k-1}\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1+\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}.

(G.8)

When
k=1
, we need to treat the term
\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}
with special care using Sobolev embedding as

\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{7/4}}\lesssim\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{2}}^{3/4}\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}}^{1/4}\lesssim\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{2}}^{3/4}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{1}}^{1/4},

where we use the interpolation inequality and (
G.6
) again.
Hence, we conclude

\|\nabla^{k-1}\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1+\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/4}\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}^{5/4},\quad k=1.

(G.9)

We are now ready to perform an induction on
k
to show that
U_{\operatorname{near}}\in H^{k}
. It holds for
k=0,1
, and suppose it holds for any index no greater than
k
, at level
k>0
we take derivatives of (
G.4
):

-\frac{1+k}{2}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}(\xi\cdot\nabla)\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{k}([\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}})+\nabla^{k}(\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}))-\Delta\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}=\nabla^{k}F^{\epsilon},

(G.10)

Take inner products with a smooth function
\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}
, where
\chi_{R}
is a standard cutoff function with radius
R
, for example
1-\widetilde{\chi}(\xi/R)
, we get the following estimate as we take
R\to\infty
.

The drift term has the estimate via an integration by parts as

\langle(\xi\cdot\nabla)\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\cdot(\chi_{R}\xi)\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=-\frac{3}{2}\langle\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle+O(\int_{R<|\xi|<2R}|\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}|^{2}),

where we use the fact that
|\nabla\chi_{R}|\lesssim 1/R
. The diffusion term similarly yields

\langle-\Delta\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon},\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=\langle\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon},\chi_{R}\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon},\nabla\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle=\langle\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon},\chi_{R}\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\rangle+O(\frac{1}{R}).

In the same fashion, the commutator term enjoys the estimate by (
G.5
)

|\langle\nabla^{k}([\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}}),\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle|=|\langle\nabla^{k-1}([\rho_{\epsilon}*,\xi\cdot\nabla]U_{\operatorname{near}}),\chi_{R}\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\rangle|+O(\frac{1}{R})\lesssim\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+O(\frac{1}{R}),

and the nonlinear term enjoys a similar estimate by (
G.8
) and (
G.9
)

|\langle\nabla^{k}(\rho_{\epsilon}*\Pi(\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U})),\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle\leqslant\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}(1+\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/4})+O(\frac{1}{R}).

Again, by Young’s inequality,
the right hand side of (
G.10
) has the trivial estimate

\langle\nabla^{k}F^{\epsilon},\chi_{R}\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\rangle\leqslant\|\nabla^{k}F^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla^{k}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\|F^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

We can take the limit
R\to\infty
and collect the estimates, treating
\|U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{H^{k}}
as a known constant:

\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1+\|\nabla^{k+1}u^{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{7/4},

and we conclude a uniform bound on
\|u^{\epsilon}\|_{H^{k+1}}
independent of
\epsilon
. Thus, we conclude that
U_{\operatorname{near}}\in H^{k+1}
by the theory of mollifiers. We can take a weakly convergent subsequence of
u^{\epsilon}
in
H^{k+1}
. Since they converge to
U_{\operatorname{near}}
in distributions, we know the limits are identical and conclude
U_{\operatorname{near}}\in H^{k+1}
.

We conclude by induction
that
U_{\operatorname{near}}\in H^{\infty}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

As in the proof sketch, we only need to elaborate on the procedure to show that
|\xi|U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
.

Step 1:
L^{2}
-level estimate, that
|\xi|U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
.
Recall that
U_{\operatorname{near}}
is smooth, and (
G.1
) holds in a strong sense with
F\in H^{\infty}
, with decay at the infinity
\nabla^{k}F=O(|\xi|^{-3-k})
. We rewrite (
G.1
) in the velocity-pressure formation

-\frac{1}{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U}+\nabla P-\Delta U_{\operatorname{near}}=F,

(G.11)

where we can represent the pressure term by taking divergence and using Riesz transform
R_{i}
as

\Delta P=-\operatorname{tr}((\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}),\quad P=\sum_{i,j}R_{i}R_{j}(\widetilde{U}_{i}\widetilde{U}_{j}).

(G.12)

We introduce the following cutoff version of the radial weight
|\xi|^{2}
as
\phi_{L}
:

\phi_{L}=|\xi|^{2}\chi_{L}+(2L)^{2}(1-\chi_{L}).

It holds that
\phi_{L}=|\xi|^{2}
if
|\xi|<L
,
\phi_{L}=|2L|^{2}
if
|\xi|>2L
,
\phi_{L}
is radially increasing since
\chi_{L}
is radially decreasing, and that
|\Delta\phi_{L}|\lesssim 1
is uniformly bounded in
L
due to the estimates on derivatives of
\chi_{L}
. Now we take inner products of (
G.11
) with
\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}
, where the cutoff radius
R
is much larger than
L
, and we get the following estimates as we take
R\to\infty
.

The drift term has the estimate via an integration by parts as

\displaystyle\langle-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\frac{3}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\frac{1}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\xi\cdot\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L})U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\frac{L^{2}}{4}O(\int_{R<|\xi|<2R}|U_{\operatorname{near}}|^{2}),

where we use again the fact that
|\nabla\chi_{R}|\lesssim 1/R
, and the radial increasing property of
\phi_{L}
. To see this,
since
\phi_{L}=\phi_{L}(|\xi|)
is radially increasing, we have

\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\phi_{L}=|\xi|\,\phi_{L}^{\prime}(|\xi|)\geqslant 0
, hence

\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L})=\phi_{L}\,\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\chi_{R}+\chi_{R}\,\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\phi_{L}\geqslant\phi_{L}\,\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\chi_{R}.

Therefore, for a lower bound, we may discard the nonnegative contribution

\chi_{R}\,\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\phi_{L}
and bound the remaining term on

\mathrm{supp}\,\nabla\chi_{R}=\{R<|\xi|<2R\}
using

\phi_{L}=(2L)^{2}
and
|\xi\!\cdot\!\nabla\chi_{R}|\lesssim 1
.

The diffusion term has a similar estimate via an integration by parts twice as

\displaystyle\langle-\Delta U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\Delta(\chi_{R}\phi_{L})U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+O(\frac{L^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{L}{R})+O(\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle),

where we use the uniform bound on the Laplacian of
\phi_{L}
. The nonlinear term splits into

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{U},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|=|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L}),|U_{\operatorname{near}}|^{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|\langle|\xi|\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}},\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|+\frac{L^{2}}{R}+\langle\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}}U_{\operatorname{near}},U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{R}+\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle},

where we use the divergence-free of
\widetilde{U}
in the first equality,
\widetilde{U}\in L^{\infty}
and the estimate
|\nabla\phi_{L}|\lesssim\sqrt{\phi_{L}}
in the first inequality. For the second inequality, we use the fact by (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) that
|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}},\widetilde{U}\in L^{4}\cap L^{\infty}
, and then
apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

The pressure term is controlled by (
G.12
) using Calderón–Zygmund theory
\|P\|\lesssim\|\widetilde{U}\|^{2}_{L^{4}}\lesssim 1
:

|\langle\nabla P,\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|=|-\langle P\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L}),U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{R}+\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

The forcing term has the trivial estimate by Cauchy-Schwarz

\langle F,\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim\langle|\xi|F,\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

We can take
R\to\infty
and collect the estimates that

\frac{1}{4}\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim 1+\sqrt{\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

As a consequence,
\langle U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle
has a uniform bound in
L
and we can take the limit
L\rightarrow\infty
to conclude that

|\xi|U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2},\quad\quad|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}.

(G.13)

Step 2:
H^{1}
-level estimate, that
|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
.
Take derivatives of (
G.11
) to get

-\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}+(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}+\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla^{2}\widetilde{U}+\nabla^{2}P-\Delta\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}=\nabla F.

(G.14)

Now we take inner products of (
G.14
) with
\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}
, where the cutoff radius
R
is much larger than
L
, and we get the following estimates as we take
R\to\infty
. We will first take
\alpha=0
to derive an intermediate result
|\xi|\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
, and then take
\alpha=2
to conclude.

For
\alpha=0,2
, the drift term has a similar estimate as that in Step 1:

\displaystyle\langle-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\frac{\alpha+3}{4}\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\frac{1}{4}\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|^{\alpha}\xi\cdot\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L})\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{\alpha+3}{4}\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\frac{L^{2}}{4}O(\int_{R<|\xi|<2R}|\xi|^{\alpha}|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}|^{2}).

The diffusion term has a similar estimate

\displaystyle\langle-\Delta\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle=\langle\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\Delta(\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha})\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+O(\frac{L^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{L+L^{2}}{R})+O(\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle),

where we use the bound
\Delta(\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha})\lesssim|\xi|^{\alpha}
uniformly in
L
. The first nonlinear term has the estimate

|\langle(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|\lesssim\||\xi|^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2}}(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\sqrt{\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

When
\alpha=0
, we have the estimate via (
G.13
) and the decay of
U_{\operatorname{far}}
:

\||\xi|^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2}}(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\||\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}}\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\||\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1.

and when
\alpha=2
, we have the estimate by Gagliardo–Nirenberg that

\||\xi|^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2}}(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\||\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{far}}\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\||\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/2}\|\nabla(|\xi|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}})\|_{L^{2}}^{3/2}\lesssim 1+\||\xi|\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/2}.

We collect the useful estimate for the first nonlinear term as

\||\xi|^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2}}(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1+\||\xi|^{\alpha/2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/2}

(G.15)

The second nonlinear term splits, similarly as in step 1, into

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla^{2}\widetilde{U},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|=|\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{far}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}),|\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}|^{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\langle|\xi|^{2}\widetilde{U}\cdot\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{far}},\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|+\frac{L^{2}}{R}+\langle\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\sqrt{|\xi|^{\alpha}}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{R}+\sqrt{\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

The pressure term is controlled by (
G.12
) using Calderón–Zygmund theory and the fact that
|\xi|^{\alpha}
is an
A_{2}
-weight for
-3<\alpha<3
(see Thm. 5.24 in
[
24
]
), so that for
\alpha=0,2
, we have by (
G.15
) that

\|\nabla P|\xi|^{\alpha/2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla(\widetilde{U}\otimes\widetilde{U})|\xi|^{\alpha/2}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\||\xi|^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2}}(\nabla\widetilde{U})^{2}\|_{L^{2}}\|\widetilde{U}^{2}\|_{L^{2}}}\lesssim 1+\||\xi|^{\alpha/2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/4},

As a consequence we have

\displaystyle|\langle\nabla^{2}P,\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle|=|-\langle\nabla P,\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}^{T}\nabla(\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha})\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{L^{2}}{R}+(1+\||\xi|^{\alpha/2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\|_{L^{2}}^{3/4})\sqrt{\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

Finally the forcing term has the trivial estimate by Cauchy-Schwarz

\langle\nabla F,\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim\langle|\xi|^{2}\nabla F,\sqrt{\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim\sqrt{\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\chi_{R}\phi_{L}|\xi|^{\alpha}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

We first choose
\alpha=0
and take
R\to\infty
to conclude a uniform bound on
\langle\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle
. Taking the limit
L\rightarrow\infty
yields
|\xi|\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}
.
Now we choose
\alpha=2
and take
R\to\infty
to get

\frac{1}{4}\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle\lesssim 1+\sqrt{\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle}.

As a consequence,
\langle\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}},\phi_{L}|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\rangle
has a uniform bound in
L
and we can take the limit
L\rightarrow\infty
to conclude that

|\xi|^{2}\nabla U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2},\quad\quad|\xi|^{2}\nabla^{2}U_{\operatorname{near}}\in L^{2}.

(G.16)

We have hence conclude the proof.
We remark that by the same argument, we can moreover show that
|\xi|^{l}\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}
for any
l\leqslant k+1
so that we can extract a decay of
O(|\xi|^{-5/2-k})
for
\nabla^{k}U_{\operatorname{near}}
using the dyadic argument. This is not necessary for our purpose.
∎
